# Everything A Studio Lot Needs

The campus around the stages: how it is laid out, what runs under it, what it sells and who it sells to. Sized for Sugar Land — 809 hectares of land, four stages, 13,200 m² of floor, fifteen minutes from Reykjavík.

Benchmarked throughout against real facilities that publish their numbers: Shinfield, Sky Studios Elstree, Trilith, Origo Budapest, Pinewood, Cinespace. Estimates are marked as estimates.

## What is in here

12 domains, 681 items. Each item says what the thing is, what it has to do, why it matters, and — where it differs — what Iceland does to it. Items are tiered: **Essential** is the thing not working without it, **Expected** is what a professional operator has, **State of the art** is what puts a facility ahead of the ones it competes with.

### The domains

1. **Site masterplan and adjacency** — 50 items (26 essential, 15 expected, 9 state of the art)
2. **The backlot and exteriors** — 65 items (24 essential, 24 expected, 17 state of the art)
3. **Roads, yards, parking and vehicle movement** — 52 items (21 essential, 21 expected, 10 state of the art)
4. **Campus utilities and resilience** — 57 items (21 essential, 20 expected, 16 state of the art)
5. **Network, data and connectivity at campus scale** — 53 items (32 essential, 16 expected, 5 state of the art)
6. **Running a studio lot as a business** — 63 items (30 essential, 26 expected, 7 state of the art)
7. **Vendors, services and the crew base** — 52 items (18 essential, 20 expected, 14 state of the art)
8. **Security, confidentiality and privacy at campus scale** — 54 items (26 essential, 23 expected, 5 state of the art)
9. **Energy, carbon and sustainability at campus scale** — 55 items (15 essential, 21 expected, 19 state of the art)
10. **Planning, permits and the neighbours** — 64 items (39 essential, 15 expected, 10 state of the art)
11. **Amenity, hospitality and why people choose you** — 62 items (13 essential, 29 expected, 20 state of the art)
12. **Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær** — 54 items (31 essential, 18 expected, 5 state of the art)

---

## 1. Site masterplan and adjacency

*Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær, Iceland (809 ha; 4 stages / 13,200 m²)*

### Essential

#### Developed core footprint versus the 809 ha land bank

Build the working lot inside ~38 ha (4.7% of 809 ha); hold 771 ha in reserve. Density benchmarks: Shinfield 26.3 ha/18 stages, Sky Studios Elstree 13 ha/13 stages, Cinespace Chicago 24 ha/33 stages, Sunset Waltham Cross 36.8 ha/21 stages. The 38 ha target is an estimate.

**Why.** Spreading four stages across 809 ha turns every crew move into a drive and multiplies utilities, security, lighting and snow clearance cost.

**Here.** Family land carries no price signal, so nothing forces compactness. The discipline has to be a written masterplan rule, not an economic one.

#### The comb plan: one shared scenery apron as the organising move

A single scenery apron 60 m wide × 320 m long. All four stages present their elephant doors to the west side; construction, paint, plaster, greens and drapes face it from the east. No set ever crosses a road.

**Why.** A set crossing a road needs banksmen, escorts, a closure and a weather window. A set crossing an apron needs a forklift and four minutes.

**Here.** Origo Budapest uses the same principle in reverse — service buildings ringed around the stages to block exterior noise — which also works as a windbreak here.

#### Maximum workshop-to-stage haul distance

≤150 m door-to-door from each stage's primary construction workshop; ≤260 m worst case anywhere in the comb (60 m across the apron + up to 200 m along it). Sky Studios Elstree pairs ~23,200 m² of workshop with 13 stages on 13 ha; Origo has 8,409 m² of workshop/warehouse.

**Why.** Each extra 100 m of haul on a 12 m flat adds a banksman and roughly 6–8 minutes a load; a 40-load set build loses half a day.

**Here.** Wind, not distance, is the binding constraint on horizontal haul — a 4 × 2.4 m flat is a sail in a 20 m/s gust.

#### Elephant door alignment and truck turning geometry

All set-haul doors on a common centreline. Apron provides 25 m outer turning radius and a 30 m straight run-in for a 16.5 m artic. Doors on the leeward gable wherever possible; where not, a wind lobby with interlocked leaves.

**Why.** A door that needs a shunt to reach costs 10 minutes per load and one paint job per week to the trailer.

**Here.** A 12 m × 9 m door open on the windward face of a 30 m stage will not close again in a gale and will pressurise the room.

#### Water stage placed at the low point and the end of the run

S2 (3,200 m², 2,400 m² tank) sits at the lowest point of the core and at the downstream end of the apron. At 3 m working depth the tank holds ~7,200 m³; fill and dump route to an on-site attenuation lagoon, never the municipal storm system. Depth and volume are estimates.

**Why.** Dumping 7,200 m³ into a town system is a consent refusal, and a wet stage upslope of a dry one is a flood risk to both.

**Here.** Water is abundant and cheap, but discharge consent from Veitur/Mosfellsbær is the constraint. An on-site lagoon lets you re-use the fill instead of re-treating it.

#### Separation distance between stage envelopes

Minimum 15 m clear between stage envelopes, 20 m stage-to-workshop, so a tender can loop every face. NFPA 140 is the international reference standard for soundstage fire strategy; the governing numbers here come from byggingarreglugerð 112/2012 and a registered fire engineer.

**Why.** Stages are large, combustible-content, high-value single compartments. Too close and one fire takes the lot; too far and the apron plan collapses.

**Here.** Fire service response is from Mosfellsbær/Reykjavík; the lot should assume it fights the first 15 minutes itself and design access accordingly.

#### Stage-adjacent production offices, one block per stage

1,200–1,800 m² of production office per stage, sharing a wall or within 30 m of the stage door, with its own entrance and its own parking. Sunset Waltham Cross planned 7 stage-adjacent production offices for 21 stages; Shinfield carries ~12,100 m² (130,000 sq ft) of office for 18 stages.

**Why.** A producer who can see the stage door walks over. A producer 400 m away sends an email and the note lands an hour late.

**Here.** With HODs imported and housed, the office block is also where they live half their waking hours — daylight, showers and drying rooms belong in it.

#### Walk-time budget as a binding masterplan rule

90% of daily crew trips — stage↔office, stage↔basecamp, stage↔canteen, stage↔toilet — under 3 minutes / 250 m at 1.4 m/s walking speed. Nothing a shooting crew touches daily sits more than 400 m from its stage. Enforced as a test on every drawing revision.

**Why.** Adjacency arguments lose to plot-shape arguments unless there is a number to fail against.

**Here.** Add a weather factor: a 250 m walk in horizontal sleet is not a 250 m walk. Covered routes shorten the effective distance.

#### The cost of a long walk, priced in money

Estimate: crew of 150, four transits a day, 10 extra minutes each = 100 crew-hours/day. At a blended loaded ISK 9,000/hr that is ~ISK 900,000/day (≈USD 6,600) and ~ISK 54m over a 60-day shoot — the price of putting basecamp 300 m too far away.

**Why.** It converts a masterplan preference into a line item a financier can read, which is the only way adjacency survives value engineering.

**Here.** Icelandic crew rates plus the imported-HOD premium make crew-hours expensive, and a ~1,000-person national base means you cannot fix a bad plan by hiring more people.

#### Basecamp weather hardening

Trailer anchor points on every bay rated for uplift at vb,0 = 36.0 m/s. A 2.5 m bund or slatted timber screen at 40–50% porosity on the exposed side of each pad. Solid walls are worse than porous screens — they create separated flow and worse gusts downwind.

**Why.** An unanchored star wagon in a Reykjavík gale is a claim, an injury and a lost day.

**Here.** Iceland is among the windiest inhabited places on earth; the National Annex publishes only the fundamental vb,0 with no zoning, so site measurement carries the design.

#### Commissary at the centroid of the stage cluster

One ~900-seat commissary placed so no stage door is more than 200 m away, plus a satellite servery at the backlot. A 60-minute break with a 6-minute round trip leaves 48 minutes of actual eating and sitting.

**Why.** Meal penalty is charged by the minute. A canteen 500 m away eats the break, then eats the schedule.

**Here.** In winter the walk is the meal break's real cost. Cover it and heat it, or people eat at their trucks and the lot loses its social centre.

#### Grid connection compound sized for a refusal

Reserve 6,000 m² for an 11/33 kV intake substation plus ≥2 MW of behind-the-meter generation and ≥4 MWh of battery. Landsvirkjun reports the system at full capacity, has rejected supply requests from new industrial load (hydrogen, e-fuels) and has curtailed ~10% of delivery to industrial customers.

**Why.** If the connection is capped below four-stages-at-once demand, the lot physically cannot sell its own inventory.

**Here.** This is the single most Icelandic constraint on the plan: cheap, clean power that you may not be able to get. The electrical masterplan must precede the spatial one.

#### SFX, pyrotechnic and LPG store, set apart

Dedicated pad ≥60 m from any occupied building, downwind of the stage cluster, on the opposite side of the core from the vendor village, with its own tender access, bunding and blast-relief construction.

**Why.** Co-locating pyro with workshops is how a paint fire becomes a site evacuation.

**Here.** Import licensing and small national stock mean productions bring their own; the store must handle a big single delivery rather than a trickle.

#### Building orientation set by measured wind, not assumption

Align each stage's long axis with the dominant strong-wind direction so the largest wall is not the windward face; set-haul doors on the leeward gable. Commission a 12-month on-site met mast at 10 m and 30 m before any orientation is fixed. Design to ÍST EN 1991-1-4, vb,0 = 36.0 m/s.

**Why.** A 30 m tall, 6,000 m² stage is the biggest sail on the lot. A wrong axis is a permanent operating cost you can never re-lay.

**Here.** Reykjavík airport wind data does not describe Mosfellsdalur — valley channelling and downslope acceleration are local. Measure on the plot.

#### Fire and emergency access loop

Continuous tender loop within 18 m of every building face, 4.5 m clear width and 4.5 m headroom, no dead end over 45 m without a turning head. Hydrants at 90 m spacing on a ring main with two independent feeds. Confirm all figures against byggingarreglugerð 112/2012 and the local fire service.

**Why.** A stage that a tender cannot reach on three sides is a stage that will not be insured for a pyro day.

**Here.** The loop must stay open in snow, which makes it one of the priority snow-melt or priority-plough routes, not an afterthought.

#### Snow storage and ash removal pads

Two 2,500 m² snow dump pads with oil interceptors, downslope and downwind of the stages, sized for a 0.6 m clearance event over 4 ha of paving (≈24,000 m³ loose). Same pads take swept volcanic ash. Keep a 3 m³ road sweeper and a washer on the fleet. Volumes are estimates.

**Why.** With nowhere to put snow it gets pushed against stage doors; ash washed into drains sets and blocks them permanently.

**Here.** Eyjafjallajökull (2010) and Grímsvötn (2011) both dropped ash across inhabited Iceland. Ash is a masterplan drainage and intake problem, not just an air-quality one.

#### The pedestrian spine

A 6 m wide covered, lit, snow-melted pedestrian spine linking gate → offices → commissary → each stage → basecamp, under 700 m end to end, physically separated from every road and the apron by kerb and bollard.

**Why.** If crew share a surface with a 16.5 m trailer, either the trailer waits or someone gets hurt. Both are expensive.

**Here.** Cover, light and heat are what make a walkable lot walkable in December; an uncovered spine at 64°N is a car park generator.

#### Medical, welfare and helipad

First-response building at the gate with a paramedic on shift and defibrillators at 12 points (max 90 s reach anywhere in the core). A 25 m × 25 m helipad 150 m from the stage cluster on the lee side. Landspítali in Reykjavík is ~18 km / ~21 min by road.

**Why.** A serious injury at the far backlot is 3 km from the gate before it is 18 km from a hospital.

**Here.** Winter road closure on Route 1 is precisely when the helipad matters. Coordinate the site with Isavia and the air ambulance operator at design stage.

#### Phasing so phase one earns while phase two goes up

Phase 1: S3+S4 (4,000 m²), one workshop, one office block, one basecamp, gate, energy centre core. Phase 2: S1 (6,000 m², 30 m). Phase 3: water stage and backlot. Shinfield ran six phases and had 80,000 sq ft operational within six months of starting on site.

**Why.** Revenue from day one funds the rest and proves the lot to bookers before the expensive 30 m room is committed.

**Here.** The 35%/25% rebate under Act 43/1999 expires at the end of 2028, so phase 1 must be shooting well before then to build a track record under the incentive.

#### Construction segregation while phase one is shooting

Separate construction gate and haul road on the opposite side of the core from the shooting gate; no plant crosses the apron. Contractual limits of 2.5 mm/s PPV and 60 dB(A) at any live stage wall during shooting hours, with an embargo window written into the build contract.

**Why.** A live stage and a piling rig 80 m away cannot coexist. If this is not contractual, phase 1 revenue simply evaporates.

**Here.** Icelandic ground is rock; breaking and blasting are noisier than driven piles elsewhere, so the embargo has to be real and priced into the contractor's programme.

#### Construction laydown and batching, retained across phases

3 ha of laydown with a concrete batching plant, aggregate stockpile and 8–12 weeks of steel and cladding storage, retained through every phase and converted to backlot at the end. Reykjavík's Sundahöfn container port is roughly 20 km by road (estimate).

**Why.** Sea freight to Iceland is weather-dependent; you must be able to hold months of structure on site or a missed sailing stops the programme.

**Here.** Everything structural is imported. Laydown is the buffer that decouples an Icelandic build programme from North Atlantic weather.

#### Expansion plots reserved with utilities stubbed and capped

Reserve two further stage plots (each ~120 m × 90 m plus 60 m of apron) and one 6,000 m² office plot inside the core, with HV, water, foul, storm, fibre and district heat brought to the plot edge, capped and surveyed. Stubbing now versus retrofitting later is roughly 10–15× cheaper (estimate).

**Why.** Shepperton needed a ~100-acre land deal to expand. A lot that has to dig up its own apron loses the booking that prompted the expansion.

**Here.** Grid capacity is the real reserve to hold. Reserve the electrical headroom in the connection agreement, not just the ground.

#### Site levels, cut-and-fill balance and geotechnical survey

Balance cut and fill on site; excavated basalt becomes wind bunds and sub-base. Ground investigation must map lava tubes, cavities and depth to rockhead across the whole 38 ha core before any stage position is fixed.

**Why.** A 30 m stage founded over an undetected lava cavity is a redesign discovered after piling has started.

**Here.** Young lava terrain routinely hides voids. This survey is cheap relative to what it prevents and must precede, not follow, the masterplan freeze.

#### Seismic movement joints between adjoined buildings

Movement joints between the stage cluster and its attached office and workshop blocks sized for calculated drift plus 25%, with flexible couplings at every service-tunnel crossing. The Icelandic NA to ÍST EN 1998-1 gives agR from 0.1 g to 0.5 g by zone; Reykjavík sits near the South Iceland Seismic Zone and the Reykjanes Peninsula Oblique Rift.

**Why.** Buildings that pound against each other in an event destroy exactly the adjacency the masterplan was built to create.

**Here.** The published Icelandic hazard map is more than two decades old and under revision; commission a site-specific assessment rather than reading the zone off the map.

#### Everything at grade on the scenery route

All stage and workshop doors at a single grade level across one continuous apron slab — no docks, no ramps, no thresholds anywhere on the scenery route. Six dock-height bays (1.2 m) exist only at the central stores and receiving building.

**Why.** A ramp on a scenery route is where flats get dropped and ankles get turned. Grade-level is why every modern stage is a slab-on-ground.

**Here.** A continuous slab is also what makes snow-melt loops and apron drainage simple; every level change is a place ice forms.

#### Surface water, attenuation and flood freeboard

8,000 m³ attenuation lagoon plus swales sized for a 1-in-100-year event with a 40% climate allowance. All finished floor levels ≥600 mm above modelled flood level. The water stage's ~7,200 m³ dump discharges to the lagoon over 6–8 hours. Volumes are estimates pending hydraulic modelling.

**Why.** An 809 ha catchment above a 38 ha core makes overland flow, not rainfall on roofs, the real flood risk.

**Here.** Very high rainfall, rapid snowmelt and ash-blinded drainage combine. Design every gully to be rodded and every interceptor to be dug out.

### Expected

#### Plot ratio and building coverage cap for the core

Gross plot ratio 0.30–0.40 across the 38 ha core (Shinfield ≈0.35: ~92,900 m² GIA on 26.3 ha). Cap building footprint at 25% of core area; the other 75% is apron, road, parking, lay-down and planting. Ratios computed from published areas — treat as estimates.

**Why.** Below 0.25 the lot reads as an industrial estate and walking dies; above 0.45 there is no room to swing a 16.5 m trailer or store a struck set.

**Here.** Every square metre of hard surface is a square metre to clear of snow and ash, so low ratios are more expensive here than elsewhere.

#### Interconnection of the two 2,000 m² stages

S3/S4 joined by a 12 m deep acoustically isolated link lobby with two 10 m × 8 m sliding acoustic leaves, giving 4,000 m² clear when open. Sky Studios Elstree combines stages to 60,000 sq ft (5,570 m²) by the same trick.

**Why.** Two 2,000 m² stages that only sell as two 2,000 m² stages are worth far less than one that also sells as a 4,000 m².

**Here.** The combined room is the answer to summer night exteriors that cannot be shot outdoors between mid-May and late July.

#### Basecamp pads, sited and powered

Two hardstanding basecamp pads of 6,000 m² each, within 200 m of the stages they serve, each with 60 shore-power posts (32 A/63 A single-phase and 400 V three-phase), potable water, foul connection and fibre.

**Why.** Shore power removes idling diesel from a live stage door — noise, fumes and carbon — and gives reliable heat at −10 °C.

**Here.** Shore power off hydro is close to free against imported diesel, and it is the difference between a trailer that starts in January and one that does not.

#### Crew parking, sized and distributed

800 spaces at ~25 m²/space ≈ 2.0 ha, split into four lots of 200, each within 250 m of the stage cluster. 25% with 11 kW AC charging, 10% with 50 kW DC. Wind-porous screening, snow-melted footways out of each lot.

**Why.** One big remote car park creates a shuttle problem and a 10-minute tail on every wrap.

**Here.** Near-universal car ownership and a ~1,000-person national crew mean people will drive from Reykjavík; a shuttle-only plan strands everyone when Route 1 closes.

#### Discreet cast arrival route

A separate 4 m gated cast lane from the main gate to a screened drop-off within 20 m of each stage's cast entrance, out of sight of the vendor village, the café and any public frontage. Camera coverage but no public overlook.

**Why.** Talent security and image control are booking criteria; a lot where the lead walks past a public café loses the job.

**Here.** Iceland's small population and short sightlines make a single photograph travel fast; screening is cheap insurance.

#### Vendor village on the outer edge of the workshop belt

3.5 ha of leasable vendor plots — lighting, grip, camera, costume, SFX, transport, catering — with their own gate, ≤400 m from the stages. Trilith runs ~38–40 production vendors on site; Sky Elstree carries ~250,000 sq ft of workshop alongside 13 stages.

**Why.** A grip head who walks to the truck at 07:00 does not lose a morning to a Reykjavík round trip.

**Here.** With everything imported by sea on weather-dependent sailings, on-lot vendor stock is the only defence against a missed ship.

#### Strike, waste and re-use yard

0.8 ha yard with 12 × 40 yd³ roll-on containers, timber and steel sorting, a covered set-store for reusable flats and a re-use exchange. On the workshop side, screened, ≤300 m from every stage. Sky Studios Elstree scored 95% — 'outstanding', the highest of any studio globally — in albert's Studio Sustainability Standard 2024.

**Why.** Strike is the biggest tonnage a lot generates. A yard that is far away means the waste stacks against the stage door instead.

**Here.** Landfill and export routes are limited and expensive; re-use is a cost saving here, not just a badge.

#### Backlot: area, distance and services

20 ha backlot on the lee side of the ridge, 600–900 m from the stage cluster, with its own basecamp pad, water, foul, fibre, tender loop and 1,600 A three-phase feeds, plus an 8,000 m² poured concrete VFX pad. Trilith's 162 ha (400-acre) backlot includes a 2-acre concrete VFX pad at 1,600 A three-phase.

**Why.** A backlot without power and drainage is a field, and a field costs a generator farm and a week of rigging every time it is used.

**Here.** 809 ha means the backlot can be genuinely large — but it must still be serviced, or the distance advantage becomes a cost.

#### Road hierarchy that keeps trucks off the apron

Three tiers: a 9 m two-way perimeter service loop (25 m outer turning radius), 7 m internal service roads, and the apron, which is not a road. 15 km/h site limit, no through route past a stage door, no reversing manoeuvre within 20 m of a cast entrance.

**Why.** Once a service loop shortcut runs past a stage door, that door becomes a traffic hazard and a noise source for the life of the lot.

**Here.** Snow narrows every road by the width of the windrow; design widths as clear-of-snow widths, not as-built widths.

#### Wayfinding and addressing scheme

Every building numbered and named: Stage 1–4, Workshop A–F, Office 1–4, Backlot Plot 1–12, Gate 1–3. A 100 m grid (A1–H8) painted on aprons, signed at every junction, and printed on every call sheet, delivery note and permit.

**Why.** A driver from Rotterdam with a 40 ft container has to find Workshop C at 03:00 in the dark without phoning anyone.

**Here.** Icelandic addressing is street + number. The lot's internal scheme must be registered with Þjóðskrá and lodged with 112 so emergency services can be given a precise point.

#### Three-zone security geography

Public zone outside the line (café, screening, visitor centre); controlled zone (lot, ANPR gates, 2.4 m fence, thermal PTZ at 120 m spacing); secure zone (individual stage compounds able to be fenced separately for the life of one production).

**Why.** A production with a confidentiality clause will not book a lot that cannot fence its stage independently of the rest of the site.

**Here.** An 809 ha perimeter is undefendable; secure the 38 ha core properly and treat the land beyond as monitored, not fenced.

#### Screening theatre and post placed away from the stages

A 60-seat DCI 4K screening theatre plus two 12-seat review rooms sited with the office group, 300–450 m from the nearest stage wall, with their own after-hours entrance and parking.

**Why.** A 22:00 screening next to a live stage means a projector, a bar and forty people arriving during a take.

**Here.** Also the lot's public face — the room the film fund, the rebate committee and the municipality get shown.

#### Crew accommodation held adjacent but outside the line

4 ha adjacent to, but outside, the studio perimeter for 120–180 serviced apartments for imported HODs and long-stay crew, plus 40 short-stay units. Trilith pairs its 700-acre studio with the 235-acre Town at Trilith next door — 15 miles of trails, 19 parks, 54 acres of forest.

**Why.** HODs housed 20 minutes away cost a shuttle fleet, a 40-minute morning and the informal after-wrap conversations that make a lot feel like a studio.

**Here.** With a ~1,000-person national crew, most HODs are imported and must be housed. Housing is not an amenity here, it is production infrastructure.

#### Acoustic setback and the noise-absorbing outer belt

Core sited ≥500 m from the nearest dwelling and ≥400 m from Vesturlandsvegur, with the workshop belt and vendor village placed between the road and the stages. Origo Budapest does exactly this — service buildings ringed around the stages specifically to block exterior noise.

**Why.** It is far cheaper to buy quiet with layout than to buy it with 60 dB of extra wall build-up on four stages.

**Here.** Wind noise on cladding and roof is the dominant external source here, not traffic — which makes the sheltering bunds an acoustic measure as well.

#### Gates, holding and the Route 1 dependency

Three gates — main (crew and cast, ANPR, two lanes), freight (HGV, weighbridge, three-vehicle holding), construction. A holding lay-by for 8 HGVs inside the line so a 16.5 m trailer never queues on the public road. Mosfellsbær is ~18 km / ~21 min from Reykjavík on Route 1.

**Why.** A truck queue on Vesturlandsvegur is a planning breach and the fastest way to lose the municipality's goodwill.

**Here.** Route 1 closes in winter storms. A lot whose only plan is 'crew drive out from Reykjavík' loses whole shooting days; on-site beds and a storm protocol are the mitigation.

### State of the art

#### Covered scenery link along both faces of the apron

Cantilevered canopy 12 m deep × 320 m long on each side, 8 m clear height, designed to ÍST EN 1991-1-4 with vb,0 = 36.0 m/s. Snow-melted slab beneath. Covers the whole door-to-door move for flats, plaster, paint and drapes.

**Why.** Rained-on flats and plaster get rebuilt, not dried. This canopy is the cheapest schedule insurance on the lot.

**Here.** Reykjavík monthly mean wind is 4–6 m/s, ~6 m/s Nov–Apr. Rain arrives horizontally, so overhead cover alone is not enough — pair it with porous side screens.

#### Central energy centre and a walkable service tunnel

Central utility plant at the geometric centre, ≤250 m to every stage, holding geothermal heat exchangers, chillers, HV switchroom, standby generation and ring-main heads. A 2.4 m × 2.4 m walkable service tunnel runs the length of the apron spine.

**Why.** A walkable tunnel means adding a stage in phase 3 does not mean digging up phase 1's apron in the middle of a shoot.

**Here.** Frost heave and ash-laden surface water make buried direct services hard to maintain; a tunnel is inspectable in February.

#### Backlot street axis set for a 2.5° winter sun

Main backlot street axis roughly NE–SW. At 64.1°N the sun peaks at ~2.5° altitude on 21 December (4 h 07 min of daylight, sunrise ~11:20, sunset ~15:30) and ~49° on 21 June. A raking axis gives usable modelling light instead of flare into every window.

**Why.** Get the axis wrong and half the year the only usable light on the street is artificial.

**Here.** Nowhere else does one axis decision swing between four hours of light and twenty-one. Solar-altitude modelling belongs in the masterplan, not the shooting schedule.

#### Permanent backlot lighting infrastructure for the dark half

24 fixed 20 m lighting towers on ring circuits with distribution boxes at 60 m spacing across the backlot, plus 400 A tie-ins at each standing-set plot. Designed as infrastructure, capitalised once, not rented per production.

**Why.** Without built-in towers a December backlot day is three hours of rigging and a generator farm for four hours of natural light.

**Here.** Iceland's winter shooting day is effectively all-artificial; the lot that owns the towers sells the winter, and every competitor does not.

#### Blackout compounds for the no-darkness half of the year

Two tensioned blackout exterior structures adjacent to the backlot — 3,000 m² and 1,200 m² — for night exteriors between mid-May and late July, when 21 June gives 21 h 08 min of daylight and civil twilight runs through the night.

**Why.** Otherwise every summer night exterior moves indoors, gets rescheduled, or leaves the country.

**Here.** This is the specific thing that loses Iceland summer bookings. A lot that solves it prices at a premium June–July.

#### Wind shelter belts and earth bunds around the core

4–6 m earth bunds cut from site excavation ringing the core, planted with wind-hardy natives (Betula pubescens, Salix phylicifolia). Shelter extends ~10–15× barrier height downwind, so a 5 m bund shelters 50–75 m of apron. Target 40–50% porosity in planted screens.

**Why.** Shelter is what makes an open apron workable. Nobody carries a flat, dresses a set or rigs a truss in a 20 m/s gust.

**Here.** Native tree cover is minimal and establishment is slow — bunds work on day one, planting matures over 15–20 years. Plan for both.

#### Geothermal snow-melt under the apron and every critical surface

Snjóbræðsla under the full 19,200 m² apron, the cast and crew entries, the pedestrian spine and the tender loop, run on ~30–35 °C district-heating return water. Iceland has over 1,000,000 m² of snow-melt paving (two-thirds in Reykjavík), 215 MW installed and 378 GWh/yr.

**Why.** A ploughed apron still has ice, and a 4 × 2.4 m flat on ice is an injury and a stopped shoot.

**Here.** This is the clearest Icelandic advantage in the whole masterplan: the fuel is waste heat that would otherwise be discarded at ~35 °C.

#### On-lot mobility that is not a private car

30 enclosed heated 4–6 seat electric buggies, 40 e-cargo bikes on studded winter tyres, covered heated racks and 12 kerbside charging hubs. Free at point of use. No private car inside the perimeter during a shoot day.

**Why.** The moment crossing a lot needs a car, parking demand doubles, the pedestrian realm dies and the apron fills with idling vehicles.

**Here.** Enclosed and heated is not a luxury here — open buggies go unused from October to April and people take their cars instead.

#### Digital site layer over the physical plan

A site digital twin carrying live stage status, elephant-door state, road and apron closures, basecamp allocation, snow-melt zone status, tender-loop status and vehicle bookings — pushed to a crew app and to gate ANPR. One source of truth for who and what is where.

**Why.** Four stages running simultaneously will contend for the same apron, the same basecamp and the same crane pad; without arbitration they collide.

**Here.** Also the mechanism for pushing weather and road-closure warnings to a crew that mostly commutes in from Reykjavík.

### What gets got wrong

- Free land is the biggest trap in this brief. With 809 ha and no land cost, every stakeholder will argue for more space between things, and the plan will sprawl. Sprawl is irreversible, and it is what separates a working lot from an industrial estate with stages on it.
- The electrical masterplan must precede the spatial masterplan. Landsvirkjun reports the system at full capacity, has refused supply to new industrial load and has curtailed ~10% of delivery to industrial customers. If the connection is capped below four-stages-at-once demand, the site layout is academic — you cannot sell inventory you cannot power.
- Wind, not distance, sets the scenery route. A 60 m open apron is unusable in a 25 m/s gust regardless of how short the haul is. Bunds, porous screens and canopies are load-bearing programme items, not landscape decoration.
- Do not take prevailing wind direction from Reykjavík airport data. Mosfellsdalur channels and accelerates wind locally. A 12-month on-site met mast at 10 m and 30 m before orientation is frozen is cheap next to a permanently wrong stage axis.
- The 2028 rebate cliff (Act 43/1999 expires at year-end 2028) is a phasing constraint, not a finance one. Phase one has to be shooting and delivering under 25%/35% well before then, or the lot opens into an unknown incentive regime with no track record.
- The summer no-darkness problem is a masterplan item. Between roughly mid-May and late July there is no true darkness, and 21 June gives 21 h 08 min of daylight. If blackout compounds and the combinable 4,000 m² stage are not in the plan, every summer night exterior leaves the country.
- The winter four-hour day (4 h 07 min on 21 December) means the backlot's permanent lighting towers are infrastructure, not equipment. Capitalise them in the masterplan budget or the backlot is a summer-only asset.
- Snow-melt paving depends on district-heating return water. Confirm with Veitur that a site outside the built-up area can actually receive and return the flow — the 30–35 °C return is the fuel, and without a return connection the whole 19,200 m² scheme falls back to expensive primary heat.
- Bunds built from excavated rock are a landform change and need consent under the Mosfellsbær aðalskipulag, which is on its 2022–2040 revision. Do not assume site-won material can simply be piled where you want it.
- Ash is a drainage and intake problem before it is an air-quality problem. Every gully must be rodded and every interceptor dug out; specify the sweeper and the washer in the fleet, not in a contingency line.
- Screening a lot with planting in Iceland is a 15–20 year project. If screening has to work on opening day, it must be built (bunds, timber screens, buildings), not grown.
- Basecamp shore power is worthless if the visiting trailers are US-spec 120 V. Specify the post configuration around what actually arrives in Iceland, and stock adaptors.
- Value engineering will attack the seismic movement joints, the service tunnel and the apron canopy first, because none of them appear in the leasable area. Losing any of the three converts an adjacency plan into three separate buildings that happen to be near each other.
- A 25 m helipad needs Isavia and air-ambulance coordination at design stage, not as a late application. So does anything tall enough to matter near an approach.
- Register the internal addressing scheme with Þjóðskrá and lodge it with 112. An unregistered private grid means an ambulance is given a co-ordinate the crew cannot read back.
- Do not put the vendor village inside the secure line. Vendors need 24-hour access and their own deliveries; if they sit inside the production perimeter, either security is theatre or the vendors cannot trade.
- Sunset Waltham Cross is the cautionary tale: 91 acres, 21 stages, £700m, full planning consent granted July 2022, paused 2023, abandoned March 2025. A masterplan that only works fully built is a masterplan that fails if the market turns. Phase one must be a complete, sellable studio on its own.
- An exterior standing set on the backlot has to survive vb,0 = 36.0 m/s. Standard US or UK backlot flat construction will not; the backlot needs its own structural standard, or the first winter takes the street.
- Plot ratio around 0.35 means roughly two-thirds of the core is open surface that has to be cleared, drained, lit and, in places, heated. Budget the operating cost of the open space, not just its capital cost.

---

## 2. The backlot and exteriors

*Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær, Iceland (campus scale: standing sets, permanent street facades, exterior tank, outdoor backings and screens, hardstanding, exterior lighting, weathering, and the sub-arctic limits on all of it)*

### Essential

#### Backlot land take and clear-horizon sightline reserve

Proposed 42 ha working backlot inside the 809 ha, plus a 180 ha sightline reserve east and south held at max 4 m built height for 180 degrees of clean horizon (estimate). Refs: Warner Bros Leavesden backlot quoted at 32 ha / 79 acres with a 180-degree uninterrupted horizon; Shinfield c.9 acres of hardstanding in three lots of 136,000 / 143,000 / 110,000 sq ft.

**Why.** Without a protected sightline the camera finds a stage roof, a mast or a car park in every wide, and every wide becomes a paint-out.

**Here.** Treeless open ground screens nothing. Esja fills the north-east horizon and reads as mountain, not city — orient sets so it is either the shot or behind camera.

#### Backlot orientation to prevailing wind

Street set long axis within +/-20 degrees of the prevailing easterly so facades present edge-on to the strongest wind; open build lots kept at least 5 building-heights downwind of solid structures to clear the separation bubble (estimate).

**Why.** Wrong orientation turns every facade into a sail and every gap between buildings into a wind tunnel that grounds condors and lifts flats.

**Here.** Reykjavik mean wind 5.2 m/s annual, 6.4 m/s October–March; the fundamental basic wind velocity for Iceland under ÍST EN 1991-1-4 is 36 m/s.

#### Heavy effects hardstanding (the concrete pad)

Proposed 6 ha of 250 mm reinforced concrete on 450 mm crushed rock, for 25 t axle and 100 kN/m2 outrigger point loads, with a 3 m grid of M24 cast-in anchor sockets at 50 kN each (estimate). Ref: Trilith's special-effects pad is 2 acres of poured concrete with 1,800 A of three-phase power.

**Why.** Set builds, crane outriggers, MEWPs and effects rigs all need certified bearing; soft ground means track mats, delay and sinking towers.

**Here.** Basaltic aggregate is local and cheap; frost design to at least 1.0 m depth with a drained sub-base, and a surface finish that survives ash and de-icing.

#### Backlot drainage, interceptors and discharge consent

Falls of 1:80 to filter drains; Class 1 oil/silt interceptors (5 mg/l) on all vehicle and effects areas; a separately consented outfall for tank dewatering at max 100 l/s with agreed temperature and residual-disinfectant limits (estimate).

**Why.** You cannot empty 14,000 m3 into a salmon river. An unconsented discharge stops the tank, not just the shot.

**Here.** 875.9 mm of rain over 154.9 precipitation days a year (Reykjavik 1991–2020 normals) means the lot is draining for half the year.

#### Backlot power spine and tie-in kiosks

Ring main to 8 backlot kiosks, each 1,600 A 400/230 V TN-S three-phase on camlock plus 125/63/32 A outlets; two kiosks uprated to 2,500 A at the street and tank. Refs: Trilith's 2-acre VFX pad carries 1,800 A three-phase; Bovingdon's Market Runway offers only 100 A single phase.

**Why.** Backlot power is what separates a lot from a field; without it every night exterior becomes diesel, noise and fuel logistics.

**Here.** Grid capacity is the binding constraint — Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load, so backlot amps must come out of the campus's already-secured connection, not be added to it.

#### Backlot water: fire main, effects main, potable

Fire ring main with hydrants at 90 m spacing delivering at least 1,500 l/min at 2 bar; a separate 150 mm effects main to street and tank with six 4-inch Storz take-offs for rain towers; potable standpipes at every unit base pad.

**Why.** Rain bars, fire gags, wash-down and firefighting compete for the same water; one undersized main and the rain stops mid-take.

#### Facade primary structure and wind design

Hot-dip galvanised steel frame designed to ÍST EN 1991-1-4 with vb,0 = 36 m/s, giving peak velocity pressure of roughly 1.7–2.0 kN/m2 at 10–15 m (estimate) — six to eight times a normal scenic flat. Foundations as permanent buildings: piled or 1.2 m pads against frost.

**Why.** Scenic construction blows down here. Anything meant to stand longer than one production must be engineered as a building and then dressed as a set.

**Here.** Salt-laden wind reaches well inland; assume a C4–C5 corrosivity environment (estimate) and specify galvanising plus marine-grade fixings throughout.

#### Facade skin, weatherline and drained cavity

Rainscreen build-up: proprietary board substrate, drained and ventilated 25 mm cavity, GRC/GRP or real reclaimed brick slips; no untreated timber or plaster on external faces; all horizontal ledges and cills falling at least 5 degrees.

**Why.** Wet-climate backlots rot from the parapet down. A street built like a set lasts two seasons; one built like a building lasts twenty.

**Here.** Coronation Street used reclaimed Salford brick and slate for authenticity; the Icelandic equivalent has to be imported, so choose the skin at build, not by patching.

#### Fire strategy for standing sets

Compartmentation every two facade bays with twin-layer board firewalls, fully automatic sprinklers to interior voids, a central addressable alarm, fire-retardant treatment on all timber and a dedicated fire main. Universal rebuilt to exactly this after the 2008 fire took 3–4 acres and 13 blocks.

**Why.** Backlot streets are continuous voids full of dry timber; one electrical fault destroyed Universal's in hours and cost $200m to rebuild.

**Here.** Assume the lot fights the first 15 minutes itself (estimate) — an on-site tender and trained crew are not optional at this distance from Reykjavik.

#### Maintenance regime and sinking fund

Full repaint of 25% of frontage annually, roofs and gutters cleared twice a year, sealant and fixing survey annually, and a reserve of 2.5–3.5% of replacement cost per year (estimate). Budget four full-time carpenters and painters for 420 m of street (estimate).

**Why.** Standing sets do not stand by themselves; deferred maintenance shows on camera within two years and doubles cost within five.

**Here.** Wind-driven rain over 155 precipitation days a year pushes the paint cycle to roughly twice temperate-climate frequency (estimate).

#### Storm tie-down and strike protocol

Written trigger levels (estimate): at forecast 20 m/s mean strike flags, banners and lightweight dressing; at 25 m/s lower all screens and towers and clear MEWPs; at 30 m/s close the lot. Every temporary element carries a rated tie-down point and a strike card.

**Why.** Icelandic storms are forecast days out; the failure mode is not the storm but having no agreed threshold and arguing about it at 2 a.m.

**Here.** Bind the protocol to the Icelandic Met Office's colour-coded regional warnings rather than to a crew judgement call.

#### Seismic design of exterior structures

Design to ÍST EN 1998-1; the Icelandic National Annex zonation gives horizontal reference acceleration agR from 0.10 g to 0.50 g across the country. Check tank walls for sloshing, tall masts for resonance, and facades for out-of-plane failure of anything a crew stands under.

**Why.** A facade that survives wind can still shed cladding in a quake, and everything overhead on a backlot is a life-safety element.

**Here.** The capital area sits west of the South Iceland Seismic Zone but has felt M6-plus events; take site-specific agR from the National Annex map, not the low end of the range.

#### Standing set build cost and amortisation

Reference points: the BBC's replacement EastEnders exterior set cost £86.7m, 45% over budget and four years late, for a single square; Universal's 13-block New York Street rebuild cost $200m. A 420 m two-sided street to Icelandic wind and weather standards: €28–45m (estimate).

**Why.** Owners price a street like scenery and discover they bought a small town; the number decides whether the backlot ever earns.

**Here.** Add roughly 25–40% over UK cost for imported steel, board and finishes, sea freight and a weather-dependent programme (estimate).

#### Planning consent and the permanence question

Two consent classes: street set and tank consented as permanent buildings with full structural sign-off; production builds under a standing framework permit with a 12-month life and pre-agreed make-good. Leavesden's backlot only held temporary consent from 2021 before permanent permission followed.

**Why.** A studio that must seek permission per build cannot promise a start date; the framework consent is itself the product being sold.

**Here.** Confirm the route with Mosfellsbær planning and the national building authority — deiliskipulag amendment lead times, not design, will set the programme.

#### Weir, horizon line and level tolerance

Continuous overflow weir on the camera-far edge, level to +/-3 mm over its full length, discharging to a balance tank at 5% of basin volume; surround deck set 25–50 mm below the water line so no coping reads. Baja's weir produces a 420 ft seamless horizon against the Pacific.

**Why.** The entire illusion is one level tolerance; 10 mm of differential settlement puts a grey line across the ocean in every wide.

**Here.** Frost heave and seismic settlement both attack that tolerance — specify adjustable weir plates and re-level annually as a maintenance item.

#### Tank filtration, turnover and clarity

Six-hour turnover of the 1.2 m body, about 2,300 m3/h through sand or glass media plus UV, with a four-hour turnover on the deep pit for dive clarity. Baja's plant delivers 9,000 US gal/min of filtered sea water; Malta quotes clarity to around 18 m after filtration.

**Why.** Surface and underwater work both die on turbidity; a tank without a real plant is a pond after two shooting days.

**Here.** Airborne ash, volcanic dust and wind-blown organic load demand oversized pre-filtration and a permanent surface skimmer loop.

#### Freeze protection and ice management

Trace-heated pipework and valve pits, tank held above 4 °C or fully drained between shows, weir channel heated from district-heat return, and a written ice plan for decks and walkways. Reykjavik's January mean is 0.7 °C and the annual mean 5.1 °C (1991–2020).

**Why.** Ice in a weir channel breaks the horizon line, ice on a deck injures crew, and freeze damage to buried plant is the expensive failure.

**Here.** The tank cannot simply sit full and idle over winter the way a Maltese or Mexican tank can — it is either heated, or drained and winterised.

#### Water safety unit

Permanent unit: dive supervisor, four standby divers, surface rescue craft, an agreed hyperbaric plan with the nearest chamber named, weekly testing to bathing-water standards, and rescue-to-surface within 30 seconds from any point in the tank (estimate).

**Why.** Water work is the highest-consequence thing the lot does; the safety unit is the licence to operate, not an overhead line.

**Here.** Cold-water protocols still apply in a heated tank once crew are climbing in and out in 4 °C air — heated changing and rewarming must be on the deck, not at the unit base.

#### Condors and MEWPs — the wind problem

MEWPs carry a wind rating of either 0 or 12.5 m/s (28 mph) under the aligned ANSI/CSA/ISO standards; above that the platform must be lowered and work stopped. Reykjavik's October–March mean alone is 6.4 m/s, so gusts cross the limit routinely — an estimated 40–60% of winter days lose condor hours.

**Why.** Schedules imported from LA or Atlanta assume condors are always available; here they are a fair-weather tool and no plan should depend on one.

**Here.** Own a fleet, but design every lighting plan from masts, ground-based big sources and towers first, with condors as upside.

#### On-site meteorology and forecast contract

Two automatic weather stations at 10 m and 30 m logging mean and gust wind, direction, rain, temperature and visibility at one-minute resolution, tied to a contracted Icelandic Met Office site forecast with six-hourly outlooks and warning feeds into the production office.

**Why.** Trigger levels only work against measured wind at set height; a regional forecast does not know what the street set does to a gust.

#### Published daylight and exterior-capacity model

Reykjavik gets 21 h 09 m of daylight at midsummer and 4 h 07 m at the December solstice; December records 12.6 hours of sunshine for the whole month against 209 h in May and 1,368 h a year (1991–2020). There is no astronomical darkness from late May to late July.

**Why.** Exterior schedule capacity here is a published fact, not a guess, and it sets how many honest exterior days the lot can sell each month.

**Here.** The backlot is a day-exterior asset in summer and a night-exterior asset in winter; the calendar sells two different products, and the rate card should say so.

#### Unit base pads and exterior crew welfare

Hardstanding base for 40 vehicles with 32 and 63 A hookups, a foul drainage connection for honeywagons, potable water and a district-heat connection for heated marquees, sized for a 250-person unit (estimate).

**Why.** A backlot with no base means generators, tankers and mud, and every hour of unit move is charged to the production.

**Here.** Heated, wind-sheltered shelter within 100 m of set is a safety requirement, not a comfort — exposure at 3 °C with driven rain is the actual hazard.

#### Environmental and heritage constraints on exterior build

Pre-build nesting bird survey with an assumed mid-April to mid-July constraint window, archaeological survey and clearance with Minjastofnun, and wetland and moss protection — Icelandic moss and heath take decades to recover from vehicle tracking.

**Why.** One drive across intact moss is a permanent scar and a press story; one missed nesting season is a shutdown.

**Here.** Verify exact statutory dates and thresholds with Minjastofnun and Umhverfisstofnun rather than assuming — they bind the build calendar, not the shoot calendar.

#### The controlled-exterior case against real locations

The lot sells certainty: no travel day, no permit, no public, power and water at the mark, a written strike protocol, a scanned twin and a repeat visit that matches. The comparison is not against Icelandic scenery but against days lost to weather, permits and unit moves.

**Why.** If the lot cannot articulate this, productions shoot the real thing and rent only the stages, and the backlot never earns its land.

**Here.** Iceland's real exteriors are world-class and nearly free; the backlot must sell what they cannot — repeatability, control, security and a day exterior in December.

### Expected

#### Secondary lots — granular, grass and lava gravel

Proposed 12 ha compacted granular lots (CBR >= 15%, 300 mm capping) and 20 ha managed grass and lava gravel for period, rural and battle work, each gated, numbered and separately bookable. Ref: Bovingdon sells backlots by surface — 3 acres concrete, 10.5 acres tarmac runway, 15.3 and 16.25 acres grass on clay.

**Why.** Not every build needs concrete; grass and gravel are cheaper, photograph better and let you sell the same acreage to a second kind of production.

**Here.** Grass lots are unusable roughly November to April without matting; publish which lots are all-weather and which are seasonal.

#### Effects services spine (air, gas, grey water, spare ducts)

Ducted spine along the street and tank apron: four spare 150 mm ducts, a 10 bar / 3,000 l/min compressed air ring, propane manifold points fed from bunded storage at least 50 m from any set, and a grey-water return line.

**Why.** Retro-trenching a finished backlot means cutting the road you just dressed; empty ducts cost almost nothing at build.

#### Backlot comms, timecode and video infrastructure

Single-mode fibre to every kiosk (12-core minimum), outdoor Wi-Fi 6E at -65 dBm or better to the lot edges, DAS and repeater coverage for radio and cellular, plus PTP/genlock and LTC distribution to street and tank for virtual production and multicam.

**Why.** A backlot with no bandwidth shoots blind; remote editorial, dailies and VP all now expect stage-grade connectivity outdoors.

#### The permanent street facade

Proposed 420 m of continuous two-sided frontage in four blocks, 18–22 m street width, buildings of 2.5–4 storeys (9–15 m), 56 individual shopfronts. Refs: Paramount's New York Street is 5 acres in eight districts; Universal's rebuilt New York Street covers 13 city blocks and cost $200m after the 2008 fire.

**Why.** A street is the only backlot asset that books itself: it sells a place rather than a surface, and productions pay for dressing they do not have to build.

#### Facade scale and forced perspective

Hero frontage at 9/10 scale, far end of the vista at 7/10, storey heights compressed above first floor. Coronation Street's Granada backlot was built at 7/10; the 2013 Trafford Wharf rebuild moved to 9/10 for realism and camera room.

**Why.** Full scale eats land and money; too small and wide lenses, steadicam and drones expose the cheat immediately.

**Here.** Low raking sun for most of the year makes forced-perspective joints read harder than in a high-sun climate — keep the cheat above 6 m.

#### Practical rooftops and roof-level working

Every facade block gets a load-bearing roof deck at 3.0 kN/m2 with demountable edge protection, permanent stair and ladder access, and rigging beams over the street rated for 500 kg point loads.

**Why.** Rooftop action, chases and lighting positions are half the value of a street set; a decorative roof forfeits all of it.

**Here.** Decks must also carry snow, drain driven rain and be strippable inside a storm-warning lead time.

#### Owned street furniture and practical lighting library

Sixty lamp standards on switched DMX circuits, plus hydrants, post boxes, signage, kerbs, bins and EV heads across US, UK, Nordic and period variants; all barcoded on an asset register with a six-monthly condition survey.

**Why.** Dressing a street from zero is the biggest cost a production faces on a backlot; owning the kit is what makes the day rate competitive.

#### Volcanic ash and tephra response plan

Cover all screens and open water, shut outdoor air intakes, keep a wash-down rig with 5,000 l stored, hold pre-filter and HEPA stock for site plant, and issue lens and camera protection kits. Reykjanes has erupted twelve times across 2021–2025; Eyjafjallajökull 2010 closed European airspace.

**Why.** Ash is abrasive silicate — it scours paint, blocks filters, contaminates tank water and destroys optics, and it arrives with hours of notice.

**Here.** Reykjanes fissure eruptions are mostly lava with little ash; the real exposure is a Katla- or Grímsvötn-class event dropping fine tephra on the capital region.

#### Backlot commercial model and rate card

Three revenue lines: per-lot day hire (indicative €3,500–9,000/day for a dressed street block, €1,200–2,500/day for open hardstanding — estimate), long-lease build lots priced per m2 per month, and a sightline-exclusivity premium. UK open-backlot comparables run around £2,000–5,000/day.

**Why.** Backlot income is what pays for the land; with no published rate card, productions assume the lot comes free with the stages.

**Here.** Price in ISK with a euro/dollar collar — productions budget in USD and the króna moves more than the margin does.

#### Exterior horizon tank — basin geometry

Proposed 90 x 90 m basin at 1.2 m nominal with a 30 x 30 m pit at 6 m and a 1:8 vehicle ramp; about 14,000 m3 / 3.7m US gal. Refs: Malta shallow tank 91 x 122 m at 1.8 m; Cinecittà 72 x 145 m at 1.6 m and 10,303 m3; Lantica horizon tank 75 x 75 m at 1.4 m; Baja Tank 1 at 17m US gal over 33,400 m2.

**Why.** Exterior tanks are wide and shallow because the horizon, not the depth, is the product; the pit exists for the one shot that needs it.

**Here.** Whether a true sea horizon is possible depends entirely on whether the land reaches the Faxaflói shore with a west-south-west aspect. Inland, the horizon is a weir plus a screen — and costs more.

#### Fill, drain, reservoir and water reuse

Fill from a lined 15,000 m3 raw-water reservoir at 1,200 m3/h (about 12 hours) and drain by gravity plus pumps in about 8 hours. Malta fills its shallow tank in 8–9 hours and empties in 6; Baja's Tank 1 takes around 40 hours either way.

**Why.** Fill and drain time is schedule — a day spent filling is a day the production pays for and does not shoot.

**Here.** Store, filter and reheat the same body of water rather than discharging; the reheat energy already spent is the reason to keep it.

#### Weather and wave effects plant

Wave paddles or pneumatic dump gates (Avatar: The Way of Water used two 19,000 lb wedges on an 800 hp, 400 gpm hydraulic system), four 5,000 l tip tanks, rain towers to 30 m, ritter fans, and a 250 kg/h snow and foam plant.

**Why.** A tank is a hole in the ground until it can make weather; the effects plant is what productions actually book.

**Here.** Ritter fans are near-redundant — the site supplies its own wind. Spend that budget on wind mitigation and calm-day scheduling instead.

#### Tank floor fixings, rigging pit and vehicle ramp

Cast-in stainless anchor grid at 3 m centres in the tank floor rated 100 kN, four corner winch pads at 250 kN, a 12 x 12 m removable false floor over the pit, and a submerged track for boat and vehicle tows.

**Why.** Retro-fixing into a finished tank means draining it; every rig point not cast in at build costs a shoot day later.

#### Fresh versus salt water decision

Specify fresh. Salt imposes a corrosion allowance on every fixing, kills reuse, complicates discharge consent and needs a dedicated salt plant. Malta and Baja use sea water because they sit on it. Provide salt dosing to the deep pit only, as a paid option.

**Why.** Salt water looks identical on camera and costs an estimated 20–30% more over the tank's life in corrosion and consent.

#### Exterior blue and green screens at scale

Proposed reversible screens: 60 x 14 m primary and 36 x 10 m secondary. Refs: Pinewood's exterior tank backing is 240 x 60 ft (73 x 18 m); Studio Babelsberg's permanent exterior green screen is 63.2 x 10.5 m / 663 m2 with a 3.5 x 3.5 m infinity cove; Bridge Studios offers 100 x 60 ft.

**Why.** An exterior screen lights screen and subject with the same daylight — the one thing an interior cyc cannot do — and it is how a tank sells a sea it does not have.

#### Screen chroma specification and upkeep

Digital green measured to a documented spec, screen luminance held within +/-1/3 stop of subject key, screen-to-subject distance at least 6 m to control spill; repaint or re-skin annually with a photometric log and a reference chart shot at every setup.

**Why.** A faded, patchy or spill-heavy screen costs a production more in roto than the screen ever earned in hire.

**Here.** Low sun for most of the year rakes across the screen and throws long shadows onto it — the screen has to be lit, not merely hung.

#### Painted sky backings and translights outdoors

Two hand-painted sky backings, 60 x 15 m and 30 x 9 m, on tensioned frames, stored dry and deployed on calm days. Refs: Universal's Falls Lake backing is 200 x 50 ft (1986); Paramount's Blue Sky Tank 175 x 75 ft; Fox's Sersen Lake 366 x 85 ft raked at 14 degrees; Cinecittà 86 x 18 m.

**Why.** A painted sky is an in-camera answer with no roto and no post cost, and a lot selling finish quality should still own one.

**Here.** Painted cloth outdoors here is fair-weather stock, not a permanent structure — budget it as deployable inventory with a replacement cycle.

#### Balloon and airborne soft sources

Helium balloon lights (2.4 kW LED units at around 399,000 lm; 4 ft self-inflating types around 105,000 lm) are effectively unusable above 8–10 m/s (estimate). Specify tethered rigid softbox towers as the primary soft night source and treat balloons as a sheltered-lot option.

**Why.** Balloons are the standard soft night-exterior answer worldwide and the first thing that fails in wind.

#### Owned large-fixture exterior package

Four SoftSun 100K, eight 18 kW HMI, twelve ARRI M90-class 9 kW, twenty-four LED equivalents such as the Nanlux Evoke 5000B (rated equivalent to a 9 kW HMI, 555,250 lux at 1 m), plus Maxi Brutes and 12-light Dinos, all in IP-rated exterior housings.

**Why.** On a remote lot in a thousand-person national crew base, the rental house is you; not owning the big units means chartering them from Europe on sea freight lead times.

**Here.** Order spares and consumables in the same shipment — a blown 18 kW globe is a two-week problem, not a two-hour one.

#### Modular exterior blackout tenting

Aluminium-framed, ballasted or ground-anchored tents in 10 m bays, blackout fabric at 99.99% opacity, internal grid at 5 m, wind-rated to 20 m/s deployed and strikeable in under four hours.

**Why.** It turns an exterior into a controllable interior without losing the location, and in June it is the only way to shoot night at all.

**Here.** Ballast, not pegs — much of the ground is lava and gravel, and the wind case governs the anchorage design.

#### Picture-vehicle compound and stunt road

A 1.2 ha secure compound with 40 covered bays, wash-down with interceptor, fuel and charging; plus a 600 m sealed process and stunt road with a 300 m straight, a skid pan and a camera-car turnaround, bookable separately from the street.

**Why.** Car work is the highest-value backlot use that needs no set; without a sealed road it goes onto public highway with closures and permits.

**Here.** Public-road closures for driving work are slow and weather-dependent here — owning the road is a real scheduling advantage, and it doubles as a winter driving-conditions asset.

#### Aerial operations area

A designated drone operating area with a published airspace assessment and Samgöngustofa/EASA-compliant procedures, plus a marked helicopter pad of at least 25 x 25 m with windsock, lighting, fuel and a NOTAM process.

**Why.** Aerials sell both the backlot and the 809 ha around it; without a permitted flight area every aerial day becomes a fresh permission negotiation.

**Here.** Volcanic activity and ash advisories can close airspace at short notice — build that into the aerial-day cancellation terms.

#### Backlot security and sightline privacy

Perimeter to both backlot and sightline reserve, thermal and PTZ coverage over street and tank, drone detection, a controlled-photography policy enforced at the gate, and no elevated public vantage point within 2 km (estimate).

**Why.** Backlot builds leak; a studio that cannot guarantee no long-lens vantage loses tentpole work to one that can.

**Here.** Small country, strong hiking culture and open sightlines from public roads — the sightline reserve is a security asset as much as a photographic one.

#### Make-good bonds on temporary build lots

Every temporary lot carries a bonded make-good: pre-condition survey, retained deposit of €50–150 per m2 of disturbed ground (estimate), a 12-month reinstatement window, and no ground broken without an approved reinstatement method statement.

**Why.** Backlots silt up with half-struck sets; the bond is the only mechanism that reliably gets them removed.

### State of the art

#### Geothermal snow-melt under hardstanding

District-heat return loops at 25–40 °C under the effects pad, street roadway and unit base; proposed coverage c.4 ha at 150–250 W/m2 design (estimate). Iceland has around 740,000 m2 of snow-melt installed, 215 MW of capacity and 378 GWh a year of consumption.

**Why.** Ploughing a dressed cobbled street destroys it; melting keeps continuity, keeps the lot open and removes the salt that eats scenic paint.

**Here.** This is waste heat already paid for — the single line item where an Icelandic lot is structurally cheaper than any competitor.

#### Permanent survey control network

Twelve forced-centring geodetic pillars on the current ISN realisation, survey nails on a 20 m grid across street and tank, and every facade and tank feature captured to +/-5 mm with the epoch recorded.

**Why.** Repeat visits, matchmove and set extensions all need one origin; without it every returning production re-surveys and re-argues scale.

**Here.** Iceland deforms — successive national datums (ISN93, ISN2004, ISN2016) exist because of crustal motion across the rift, so date every control record.

#### Interchangeable facade panel system

Facades on a 1.2 m panel module with hook-on brackets and quick-release fixings so a shopfront changes period in a two-day turnaround; three alternate skins per hero frontage kept racked, numbered and stored in a dedicated 600 m2 building (estimate).

**Why.** A single-look street books once a year; a street with three looks books three times, and the change is a scenic day rather than a construction period.

#### Shoot-through practical interiors

At least eight shopfronts and four hallways built with 6–10 m of practical interior behind the glass, lit and finished to stage standard, so a camera can shoot out of a window or follow an actor through a door in one move.

**Why.** Without practical depth every doorway needs a stage match and a separate relight; with it a scene plays continuously.

#### Roadway and interchangeable ground treatments

Structural reinforced-concrete roadway carrying removable 2.4 x 1.2 m cassette panels — setts, asphalt, dirt, tram rail, period tarmac — with camera-track pockets and a 300 mm sand-fill layer for dressed surfaces.

**Why.** Ground is the first thing a wide reads. Repaving between shows is the most expensive backlot habit; cassettes make it a lift-and-swap.

#### Scenic ageing and patination programme

A standing ageing crew and materials library — limewash, iron-oxide staining, moss inoculation, controlled rust — with a written rule on which elevations are allowed to weather naturally and which are arrested with a breathable consolidant.

**Why.** New sets read new. Controlled age is the difference between a backlot and a business park with signage.

**Here.** The climate ages sets for free and faster than you want; the skill here is choosing what gets protected, not what gets aged.

#### Geothermal tank heating

Plate heat exchangers off the district-heat main. Raising 14,000 m3 from 6 °C to 21 °C takes about 244 MWh, or roughly 4,700 m3 of 80 °C geothermal water cooled to 35 °C (calculated estimate). Nauthólsvík does this at city scale, holding a sea lagoon at 15–19 °C.

**Why.** Cold water is the hardest limit on exterior water days; heat turns an unusable tank into a year-round asset.

**Here.** This is the one thing Iceland does better than Malta or Baja — heat is cheap, abundant and already piped. It is the tank's whole competitive argument.

#### Floating thermal cover and standing-loss control

Open-water standing loss at 15 K above ambient in 6 m/s wind runs roughly 400–800 W/m2, i.e. 3.2–6.5 MW over 8,100 m2 (estimate). A deployable floating cover in 12 m panels cuts that 60–75% (estimate) and winches off in under an hour.

**Why.** Without a cover you are heating the North Atlantic; with one, the tank holds temperature overnight between shoot days instead of being reheated.

**Here.** Wind makes the loss worse than the temperature difference alone suggests — the cover matters more here than at any warm-climate tank.

#### Retractable, porous screen engineering for wind

Wind is the governing case: a 73 x 18 m solid screen at 1.7 kN/m2 sees roughly 2.2 MN of lateral load (calculated) — unbuildable as a fixed sail. Answer: perforated PVC mesh at 30–50% porosity on motorised roller masts, auto-retracting at a 15 m/s mean or 20 m/s gust trigger (estimate).

**Why.** Every large exterior screen elsewhere assumes benign weather; here the screen must be a machine that stows itself or it becomes debris.

**Here.** This is the hardest single piece of exterior engineering on the lot, and the one an imported design will get wrong.

#### Outdoor LED sky and digital backing

Sunlight-readable outdoor LED, IP65, at least 5,000 nits, 3.9 mm pitch, on tracked carriages so it works as horizon, sky card or interactive source; 40 x 8 m proposed (estimate), stowable into a sheltered bay.

**Why.** It solves what a painted backing cannot — a moving sky, a correct time of day, and genuine interactive light on water.

**Here.** In four hours of December daylight an LED sky is the only way to shoot a summer exterior; it is the direct counter to the latitude.

#### Exterior infinity cove

One hard outdoor cove: 30 x 20 m floor with a 6 m radius cove rising to 10 m on two sides, in painted GRC, drained and trace-heated, for product, vehicle and creature work. Babelsberg pairs its exterior green screen with a 3.5 m cove for edge-free 90-degree corners.

**Why.** Daylight against a horizonless corner is a combination no stage can give and no location can control.

**Here.** Heat and drain it — standing water and ice in a cove destroy both the surface and the seamlessness.

#### Permanent lighting masts

Eight fixed masts at 24–36 m around street and tank, designed for the full 36 m/s wind case, with winch-down luminaire carriages serviced at ground level, climbable ladders, aviation marking as required and 400 A per mast.

**Why.** Masts do what condors cannot here — stand up in wind, stay rigged between shoot days and get serviced without a MEWP.

**Here.** The winch-down carriage is the whole point: you cannot send a crew up 30 m in a gale, and gales are the normal condition.

#### Shore power for exterior lighting, not generators

Size shore power so a full night exterior — around 2 MW of fixtures plus 500 kW of effects (estimate) — runs off the grid connection, with 2 MWh of containerised battery buffering for peaks and generators retained only as backup.

**Why.** Diesel on a backlot means noise, fuel logistics, emissions and a sustainability claim you cannot make; shore power is the entire point of an Icelandic lot.

**Here.** With Landsvirkjun refusing new industrial supply, backlot load must be scheduled against stage load inside one fixed allowance — batteries buy peak headroom the grid will not sell.

#### Wind shelter: belts, fences and screens

Multi-row shelter belts of Sitka spruce, lodgepole pine and native birch at 30–50% porosity give useful shelter for 10–15 tree heights downwind and measurable effect to about 20 H; a 12 m belt shelters roughly 120–180 m. Bridge with demountable 6 m timber wind fences on the tank apron.

**Why.** Shelter is the cheapest lighting, grip and safety equipment on the lot — it buys condor hours, screen hours and crew endurance.

**Here.** Trees take 20–30 years to reach height here. Plant on day one and use engineered fences to bridge the gap; nothing else on this list has a longer lead time.

#### Night-for-day and day-for-night infrastructure

Summer night exteriors need blackout rather than lighting; winter day exteriors need a synthetic sun — hard 18 kW and 100K sources on masts at 20-plus degrees elevation, daylight-balanced, with LED sky for the horizon and negative fill for contrast.

**Why.** Without both, the lot only shoots day in summer and night in winter, halving the usable year for every production that books it.

#### Lidar digital twin of the backlot

Lidar and photogrammetry capture at build and after every major change: registered point cloud at 5 mm or better, photogrammetric and Gaussian-splat assets, monthly HDRI sun studies, all licensed to hirers as part of the booking.

**Why.** It lets set extensions, previz and reshoots happen without returning to Iceland — exactly what a remote lot must be able to offer.

**Here.** The further the lot is from the production's home base, the more the digital twin is worth; here it is a core product, not a VFX nicety.

#### The 809 ha as a consented location portfolio

Catalogue and pre-consent the land itself — lava field, river, moss heath, lake edge, coastal flat, farm buildings, gravel road — each with an access track, parking pad, lighting-power point and an agreed environmental method statement.

**Why.** The land is the lot's biggest asset; turning it into permitted, serviced, bookable locations is far cheaper than building anything.

**Here.** Icelandic exteriors are what productions come for. The studio's edge is offering them controlled, permitted, powered and fifteen minutes from Reykjavik.

### What gets got wrong

- The headline exterior screen everyone copies is physically impossible here. A Pinewood-scale 73 x 18 m solid backing at Iceland's ~1.7 kN/m2 peak velocity pressure carries roughly 2.2 MN of lateral load (my calculation). Any fixed exterior screen must be perforated, motorised and self-retracting, or it is debris. An imported design will assume otherwise.
- Condors are a fair-weather tool in Iceland. MEWPs are rated to 12.5 m/s (28 mph) under the aligned ANSI/CSA/ISO standards; Reykjavik's winter mean wind alone is 6.4 m/s. Any lighting plan or schedule that assumes condor availability — the LA and Atlanta default — will fail. Estimate: 40-60% of winter days lose condor hours.
- Reykjavik records 12.6 hours of sunshine in the whole month of December (1991-2020 normal) and 4 h 07 m of daylight at the solstice. A backlot marketed on day exteriors cannot deliver them for roughly a third of the year without a synthetic sun on masts.
- From late May to late July there is no astronomical darkness. Night exteriors in summer are a blackout-tenting and LED problem, not a lighting problem — and the tenting has to be wind-rated, which most blackout systems are not.
- Whether a true horizon tank is even possible depends on a fact not in the brief: whether the 809 ha reaches the Faxafloi shore with a west/south-west aspect. Inland at Mosfellsbaer the sea horizon is blocked, so the 'horizon' must be made by weir plus screen — more cost, more maintenance, and a level tolerance of +/-3 mm to hold against frost heave.
- Backlot power comes out of the same fixed grid allowance as the stages. With Landsvirkjun refusing new industrial supply, a 2 MW night exterior is not additive — it is a scheduling conflict with the stages. Battery buffering is the only way to buy peak headroom.
- The 25%/35% rebate is legislated to expire at end-2028 (Act No. 43/1999). A three-year street build completing in 2029 is a different business from one earning in 2027. Sequence the backlot to be revenue-generating before the incentive question is settled.
- I could not verify online the site-specific characteristic snow load (sk) for Mosfellsbaer under the Icelandic National Annex to IST EN 1991-1-3, nor the site-specific agR under IST EN 1998-1/NA:2010. Both must be pulled from the National Annexes before any exterior structure is designed. The 36 m/s basic wind velocity is from a load-zone reference tool and should be confirmed against the National Annex zonation.
- Standing sets cost like buildings, not scenery. The BBC's EastEnders replacement set came in at GBP 86.7m, 45% over budget and four years late, for one square. The risk on a street build is programme and weather, not design.
- A backlot street is one continuous fire compartment full of dry timber unless deliberately built otherwise. Universal's 2008 fire took 3-4 acres and thirteen blocks and cost $200m to rebuild. At Mosfellsbaer, assume the lot fights the first fifteen minutes itself (estimate).
- Shelter belts take 20-30 years to reach useful height in Iceland. If they are not planted before the first stage opens, the lot never gets them. This is the longest lead item in the entire domain and the easiest one to defer.
- My tank thermal numbers are calculations, not published figures: ~244 MWh and ~4,700 m3 of geothermal water for the first heat-up, and 3.2-6.5 MW of standing loss uncovered. Verify with a proper thermal model before sizing heat exchangers — the standing-loss figure in particular is sensitive to wind and could be materially higher.
- Do not specify salt water. Malta and Baja use it because they sit on the sea; an inland heated tank that wants to reuse and reheat its water gets nothing from salt except corrosion, consent difficulty and an estimated 20-30% lifetime cost premium.
- 155 precipitation days a year understates the problem. Icelandic rain is horizontal. Rainscreen details, cill falls and cavity drainage designed for temperate vertical rain will leak, and a facade that leaks rots from the parapet down.
- Painted sky backings and balloon lights are consumable deployables here, not capital assets. Budget them with a replacement cycle rather than depreciating them over a decade.

---

## 3. Roads, yards, parking and vehicle movement

*Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbaer, Iceland (campus/lot scale)*

### Essential

#### Heavy spine road (stage-door route)

9.0 m carriageway two-way + 1.5 m bound hard verges. Design vehicle: 16.5 m tractor/semi at 44 t and a 6-axle step-frame low-loader. Swept path proved against Directive 96/53/EC's 12.5 m outer / 5.3 m inner concentric circles. Widths are an estimate for this lot.

**Why.** A low-loader that cannot make a corner reverses 300 m past working stages or blocks the spine for an hour; every other movement queues behind it.

**Here.** Iceland's routine limits are 10 t single / 11.5 t drive axle, 18 t tandem, 26 t tri-axle, 44 t artic. US low-loader assumptions (80,000 lb+ routine) do not transfer; heavier needs a Samgöngustofa exemption.

#### Stage apron / elephant-door forecourt

Per stage: 30 m clear depth x full door width + 8 m either side, 250 mm reinforced concrete, kerb-free, laid to 1:80 fall away from the threshold. Sized so a 16.5 m artic can square onto the door in one movement without entering the spine. Estimate.

**Why.** Undersized aprons force trucks to swing across the spine to line up on a door, which is where lot collisions and door strikes actually happen.

#### Turning heads and hammerheads

Every cul-de-sac ends in a turning head with a 26 m diameter turning circle between kerbs (high-reach fire appliance figure from UK Approved Document B); hammerheads sized 20 m x 20 m minimum. Nothing on the lot requires a reverse longer than 25 m.

**Why.** Reversing is the single largest cause of vehicle incidents on studio lots; designing it out beats managing it.

**Here.** Icelandic byggingarreglugerd 112/2012 requires fire-service access to buildings and plots to be agreed with the local fire chief — here Slokkvilid hofudborgarsvaedisins (SHS). Their numbers govern, not ADB's.

#### Vertical clearance envelope

5.5 m minimum clear height over every road and yard; 6.5 m on the heavy spine and crane routes. No gantries, pipe bridges, cable catenaries or signage below that. Height-restrictor goalposts only where a low structure is unavoidable, set 300 mm below the true obstruction.

**Why.** A loaded low-loader plus set piece can exceed 5 m; a strike takes out both the vehicle and the service you hung over the road.

#### Gradients, crossfall and level thresholds

Max longitudinal gradient 1:20 (5%) on the spine, 1:12 (8.3%) absolute anywhere; crossfall 1:40 (2.5%). Stage thresholds and unit base pads laid within 1:100 (1%). No vertical steps over 6 mm at joints.

**Why.** Low-loaders ground out on breakover; a trailer parked on more than 1% cannot be levelled on its jacks and its doors and slide-outs bind.

**Here.** Anything above 5% is unusable in freezing rain. Ice, not snow, is the Icelandic surface problem — flatter is safer than gritted.

#### Junction with the public road and right-turn refuge

Single controlled site access with a 25 m x 3.5 m deceleration/right-turn lane, 15 m entry radii for a 16.5 m artic, and 60 m of internal holding before the gate so an arriving truck never stands on the public carriageway. Estimate.
