**Why.** A queued lorry on a 90 km/h national road is a fatality waiting to happen and the fastest route to a planning condition capping your movements.

**Here.** Access will be off Vesturlandsvegur (Route 1) or Thingvallavegur (Route 36); both are Vegagerdin roads and any new junction needs their approval and their sight-line standards.

#### Pavement build-up and design traffic

Heavy spine and yards: 260 mm CBGM base + 100 mm binder + 40 mm SMA surface on 400 mm capping, designed for ~5 msa over 20 years. Secondary roads ~1.5 msa. Unit base and laydown: 200 mm PQ concrete slab, C35/45, A393 mesh top and bottom. Estimates.

**Why.** Studio yards fail from static point loads and slow heavy turning, not from traffic volume; a road-spec pavement rutted by parked trailers is a permanent repair liability.

#### Frost protection and non-frost-susceptible subgrade

Excavate frost-susceptible silt and replace with non-frost-susceptible crushed rock to 1.2-1.5 m below finished level where ground investigation shows risk; Icelandic building regulation uses a frost-free founding depth of 1.2-2.0 m as its reference. Separation geotextile throughout.

**Why.** Frost heave lifts slabs differentially, then thaw-weakening drops the bearing capacity right when spring construction traffic peaks.

**Here.** Vegagerdin runs a real-time frost-depth forecast used to impose spring axle-load restrictions on Icelandic roads. Build the lot so you are never the reason a load is refused.

#### Drainage, oil interceptors and ash-tolerant gullies

Class 1 full-retention interceptors on unit base, fuel, wash and laydown catchments; Icelandic regulation 884/2017 sets a 15 ppm oil limit on separator discharge. Oversized gully pots with 300 mm silt sumps; attenuation sized for the wet-climate design storm plus a blocked-gully allowance.

**Why.** Ash and grit blind conventional gullies in a single event, and a hydrocarbon release from a genset or fuel point becomes an Umhverfisstofnun matter.

**Here.** Regulation 884/2017 (varnir gegn oliumengun fra starfsemi a landi) is the governing instrument; it also requires bunds designed to be filled entirely with water.

#### Lot lighting for a four-hour winter day

Full LED road and yard lighting to ~20 lux average on roads, 50 lux on unit base and loading, 100 lux at gates and pump-out points; columns rated for the 36 m/s basic wind, warm 2700-3000 K, full cut-off, on astronomical time switch plus presence dimming.

**Why.** For five months the entire lot operates in darkness. Lighting is a safety system, not amenity, and it is also the thing that leaks into every backlot night shot.

**Here.** Reykjavik's shortest day is about 4 h 07 m (sunrise ~11:22, sunset ~15:30). Around midsummer there is no true darkness at all — zoned, fully dimmable circuits let you kill lighting for a June exterior.

#### Crew car parking count

700 marked spaces plus 150 overflow. Two derivations: Shinfield's consented 1,375 spaces against ~41,800 m2 of stage is 33 spaces per 1,000 m2, which gives 435 here; a peak-day headcount of ~900 at 1.25 occupancy and 85% car mode gives ~610. Provide 700. Estimate.

**Why.** Under-provision pushes crew onto verges and the public road; gross over-provision is 2 ha of pavement you clear of snow all winter for nothing.

**Here.** There is effectively no useful public transport to a lot in Mosfellsdalur and crew are housed across the capital region, so car mode share is far higher than at a UK or Georgia lot.

#### Visitor, production office and accessible parking

60 visitor bays at the gate lobby, 90 office bays, accessible bays at 6% of total (42) at 3.6 m x 6.0 m with 1.2 m hatched transfer zones, all within 50 m of a level entrance and inside the snow-melted zone.

**Why.** Visitors who wander into an operational yard looking for a door are the commonest near-miss on a working lot.

#### Unit base hardstanding

1.6 ha of 200 mm PQ concrete laid to 1% or flatter, marked in 4.0 m x 16.0 m trailer bays with 9.0 m aisles, holding ~40 trailers plus 20 trucks. Kept as one contiguous rectangle, not fragmented, so a base can be laid out any way the production wants.

**Why.** A production that cannot fit its base on the lot puts it in a field with generators and a shuttle, which erases the reason to be on a lot at all.

**Here.** Level concrete matters more here than elsewhere: trailers must be jacked and stabilised against wind, and jacks need a hard, flat, non-heaving surface.

#### Honeywagon standings and pump-out

6 dedicated standings, each 4.5 m x 14 m, with a sealed 100 mm foul connection, potable fill and washdown. Trailer tanks run to ~7,500 L waste and ~1,900 L fresh; service is typically every 1-3 days. Standings sited within 60 m of a stage door and downwind of catering.

**Why.** A honeywagon parked without a foul connection needs a tanker every day, which is a truck movement, a cost and a smell you scheduled into your own lot.

#### Construction laydown and scenic yard

1.5 ha of surfaced laydown adjacent to the workshops with 6 m aisles, timber and steel racking, a covered 800 m2 bay, and 400 A 3-phase. Sized to hold roughly two weeks of imported material — i.e. two sailings' worth of buffer. Estimate.

**Why.** Laydown sized to weekly consumption fails the first time a sailing is missed; the yard is the shock absorber between the sea and the build.

**Here.** Eimskip's Blue Line runs weekly, Reykjavik-Rotterdam in about 3 days and Reykjavik-Teesport in about 4, on a 7-day rotation. Missing a cut-off costs a week, not a day.

#### Refuse and recycling yard, seven streams

800 m2 covered, bunded yard with separate compactors and bays for paper, plastic, organic, textiles, metals, glass, hazardous and mixed — Icelandic workplaces must sort into those seven categories under the 2023 circular-economy act. Weigh-in point and stream-level reporting to production.

**Why.** Waste sorting is a legal duty here and a rebate/sustainability reporting input; a single mixed skip destroys both.

**Here.** Under the Act on the Circular Economy paper, plastic, organic and mixed are collected at the workplace; textiles, metals, glass and hazardous must be taken to a collection centre or covered by a contract.

#### Main gate, security lodge and ANPR

Two-lane in / two-lane out with rising-arm barriers and road blockers, ANPR on all lanes, pedestrian turnstile lodge, and a target throughput of 240 vehicles/hour inbound. Pre-registered vehicle clears in under 20 seconds; unregistered visitor processed in under 3 minutes.

**Why.** The gate is the lot's throughput ceiling. At call time 400 cars arrive in 40 minutes and a 60-second gate transaction queues them onto the national road.

#### Separate heavy goods gate and marshalling lane

Dedicated goods gate with the weighbridge, a 120 m marshalling lane holding 6 artics off the public road, driver welfare (toilet, kettle, wifi) and a call-forward screen. Physically separate from the crew and visitor gate.

**Why.** Mixing a 44 t artic with 400 arriving crew cars at one gate is how a lot gets its first pedestrian fatality.

**Here.** Drivers arrive off a weather-delayed ship on an unpredictable schedule; a marshalling lane with welfare is the difference between waiting and going away.

#### Fire and ambulance access loop

Continuous appliance loop reaching 100% of every stage perimeter, minimum 3.7 m clear width, 12.5 t (pump) to 17 t (high-reach) carrying capacity, 16.8-26.0 m turning circles between kerbs — UK ADB figures, to be replaced by SHS's own on agreement. Hydrant spacing and route kept snow-free.

**Why.** A stage fire with a blocked appliance route is the one lot failure that ends the business.

**Here.** Byggingarreglugerd 112/2012 requires fire and ambulance access to buildings and plots to be agreed with the fire chief. Snow clearance of the appliance loop must be first priority in the winter plan, ahead of parking.

#### Muster points and evacuation routing

Four muster points, each 400 m2, hard-surfaced, lit, wind-sheltered and clear of the appliance loop and of any stage's 30 m collapse zone, with a marshalling board and headcount reader tied to gate ANPR and pass control.

**Why.** You cannot account for 900 people in a January gale at a muster point in the middle of a car park you have just filled with cars.

**Here.** Sheltered and heated matters: an evacuation at -5 C with 25 m/s wind becomes a casualty event within twenty minutes.

#### Lot fleet, snow plant and winter service plan

2 ploughs with gritters, 1 blower, 1 regenerative-air sweeper (ash-rated), 2 telehandlers, 6 electric utility vehicles, 2 crew shuttles. Written winter plan with named priority routes: appliance loop, stage aprons and gate first, then unit base, then parking.

**Why.** Winter service without a written priority order defaults to whoever shouts loudest, which is never the fire route.

**Here.** Reykjavik's own service prioritises main and bus routes and clears residential streets only above ~10 cm — mirror that logic internally and publish it so productions can plan around it.

### Expected

#### One-way clockwise perimeter loop

6.5 m single-lane one-way ring, ~2.4 km, no dead ends, 12 passing bays 3.5 m x 25 m at ~200 m centres. Clockwise so that in right-hand traffic the stage and yard frontage stays on the driver's nearside. Estimate.

**Why.** Two-way movement on a lot means reversing, banksmen and stopped trucks. One-way removes the head-on conflict and lets you time journeys deterministically.

#### Kerbs, edge restraint and overrun strips

Full-height 125 mm kerbs only where pedestrians need protection. All bend exteriors get a 1.5 m flush overrun strip in 200 mm concrete block or PCC. Radii externally protected with removable bollard sockets, not fixed steel.

**Why.** Trailers cut corners by design. Unprotected verges become mud that gets tracked onto the lot and into the stages.

#### Surfacing chosen against studded-tyre wear

Stone mastic asphalt with imported hard aggregate (PSV/LA-tested) on trafficked roads; PQ concrete on all standing areas. Budget a surface-course renewal every 8-10 years on the spine. Estimate.

**Why.** Studded tyres abrade asphalt an order of magnitude faster than plain tyres and generate the PM10 dust that then blows into stages and filters.

**Here.** Studded tyres are legal 1 Nov - 15 Apr and banned outside that window precisely because they grind the asphalt; minimum legal tread in the winter window is 3.0 mm.

#### Heavy-duty crane and plant pads

Four dedicated pads, 20 m x 20 m, 350 mm C40/50 concrete on 500 mm Type 1, designed for 250 kPa allowable bearing. Sized for an all-terrain crane imposing ~890 kN through a single outrigger; industry guidance commonly caps permissible ground pressure at 200 kPa on unengineered ground.

**Why.** A crane that sinks an outrigger 100 mm is a dropped load. Purpose-built pads remove the daily ground-condition argument between the lot and the lift supervisor.

#### Snow storage and meltwater management

Two dedicated snow dumps totalling ~4,000 m2 (about 5% of paved area), bunded, falling to an interceptor. Sited downwind of stages and never over a drainage field. Cleared surfaces are pushed to the dump, not to the edge of the bay next door. Estimate.

**Why.** Without a designated dump, ploughed snow eats the parking and unit base you actually need in the month you most need it, and refreezes into ridges.

**Here.** Snow dumps carry grit, oil and volcanic dust; Icelandic discharge rules make their meltwater a pollution risk, not a drainage afterthought.

#### Road markings, signage and wayfinding

Thermoplastic with high-friction anti-skid at junctions; all directional signage bilingual Icelandic/English; stage numbers 600 mm high and lit; retroreflective delineator posts at 25 m on the spine so the road edge reads under snow cover.

**Why.** Most drivers arriving here are imported, on their first visit, in the dark, in weather. Signage failure shows up as vehicles in the wrong yard.

**Here.** A national crew base of roughly a thousand means a large share of drivers and HODs are foreign and unfamiliar; assume no local knowledge.

#### Speed management

20 km/h lot-wide limit, 10 km/h in unit base and yards. Enforced by geometry and 75 mm speed tables (never round humps, which ground low-loaders and break trailer suspensions), plus ANPR average-speed logging tied to the vehicle permit system.

**Why.** Humps chosen without checking breakover damage the exact vehicles you built the lot for; tables slow cars without punishing trailers.

#### Cast and artiste discreet parking

12-16 bays behind a controlled gate with its own approach, sight-screened, direct to the artiste trailer line and to a stage door — never through the crew car park or past the main gate lobby.

**Why.** Talent arrival is the one vehicle movement with a privacy and security requirement; if it shares a route with crew and visitors you cannot control it.

#### Motorcycle, cycle and shuttle interchange

40 motorcycle bays, 60 secure covered cycle spaces with e-bike charging, and a sheltered shuttle stand for two 12 m coaches with a lay-by so a coach never blocks the spine. Shinfield consented 64 motorcycle and 160 cycle spaces.

**Why.** A crew shuttle from Reykjavik is the only realistic way to cut single-occupancy trips here, and it needs a real stand, not a kerb.

**Here.** Fifteen minutes from Reykjavik makes a contracted crew coach viable; a shelter with heat is not optional in a January gale.

#### EV charging

175 x 22 kW AC bays (25% of marked spaces), 4 x 150 kW DC for vans and shuttles, all on dynamic load management capping the site draw at ~600 kW against a 3.85 MW unmanaged connected load. Ducting and bay-side chambers to 100% of spaces on day one.

**Why.** Retrofitting charge points means digging up a car park you cannot close. Ducting everything is cheap now and impossible later.

**Here.** Electric vehicles are about one in three new Icelandic registrations and eight in ten are 'new energy' (FIB, 2025) — demand will outrun any fixed provision. Load management matters because the grid, not the site, is the constraint.

#### Overflow and event parking

1.2 ha of reinforced-grass / cellular confinement overflow for 150 cars and wrap-party or premiere events, with a bound access spur so vehicles never cross soft ground to reach it. Estimate.

**Why.** Peaks are real but rare — crowd days, unit moves, two productions overlapping. Paving for the peak is waste; mud is worse.

#### Catering circus and dining position

1,200 m2 hardstanding for catering trucks, dining bus or marquee, with 125 A 3-phase, 32 mm potable supply, a trapped grease interceptor and a bunded grey-water discharge. Positioned upwind of unit base and at least 30 m from any fuel or genset pad.

**Why.** Catering is the highest-frequency vehicle movement on the lot and the only one with a food-hygiene and grease-trap requirement.

**Here.** A marquee needs a wind-rated frame and ballast; standard event tentage will not survive an Icelandic gale.

#### Generator pads

6 bunded pads, 6 m x 14 m, 300 mm concrete falling to a sealed sump, each with a 400 A 3-phase paralleling/changeover point back to lot distribution, earth spike, spill kit and acoustic screening. Pads sited so exhaust never faces a stage door or air intake.

**Why.** Even a fully electrified lot needs generator standing for redundancy, high-draw shoot days and productions arriving with their own hire units.

**Here.** Iceland's grid is at capacity — Landsvirkjun has declined supply to new large industrial users, and new generation (Burfellslundur 2026, Hvammsvirkjun 2028) is behind that demand. Genset pads are your hedge on the connection agreement, not a legacy item.

#### Fuel storage and dispensing

2 x 25 m3 double-skinned above-ground tanks (HVO and a small diesel reserve), deliberately each below the 50 m3 threshold at which regulation 884/2017 requires level indication and earthing of steel tanks. Bund sized to hold the tank full of water. Card-controlled dispensing with per-vehicle metering.

**Why.** Fuel is the lot's largest single environmental risk and its most stealable asset; metering per vehicle is also how you bill a production honestly.

**Here.** HVO must be imported by sea like everything else, so tank sizing is set by sailing frequency, not by weekly burn.

#### Vehicle wash, wheel wash and ash washdown

Drive-through wheel wash on the laydown exit and a 12 m x 6 m covered wash bay with a recycling water plant, high-pressure lances and a Class 1 interceptor. Rated to handle abrasive volcanic fines without pump damage.

**Why.** Mud and grit tracked from a construction yard onto the spine ends up on a stage floor, and after an ash event every vehicle on the lot needs washing before its filters are changed.

**Here.** After Eyjafjallajokull 2010, wetting roads and imposing low speed limits was the measure used at Vik to stop ash resuspension — plan the same as a standing procedure.

#### Crane and cherry-picker standing / MEWP compound

Secure 2,000 m2 compound for scissor lifts, boom lifts, telehandlers and forklifts, with 40 charging points, level concrete, and a wind-sheltered corner for machines that cannot be laid down. Most boom MEWPs have a manufacturer operating limit around 12.5 m/s wind.

**Why.** MEWPs stored loose on a lot are the machines that get stolen, get wind-damaged and get used flat.

**Here.** Wind stops MEWP work far more often here than rain does; the compound needs a monitored anemometer feeding the lot's daily work plan.

#### Freight receipt and container yard

Hardstanding for 30 x 40 ft containers stacked two high, on a 250 mm slab, with reach-stacker or side-lifter access, plus a marked empties row and a 25 m x 25 m devanning apron under cover. Twist-lock ground plates on every stand.

**Why.** Containers arrive faster than they are unpacked. Without a yard they end up as ad-hoc storage on roads and aprons and never leave.

**Here.** Everything structural is imported. Sundahofn is roughly 20-25 minutes away, so the lot is functionally an inland extension of the port — and empties must be returned to keep demurrage down.

#### Set and scenic store yard

3,000 m2 of external hardstanding plus racking for standing sets, flats, rostra and vehicle-mounted rigs, laid out in bays addressable by a stores system, each bay with a marked footprint and a maximum stack height.

**Why.** Stored sets are the asset that makes a repeat client come back. Unaddressed, they become a scrap heap you pay to remove.

**Here.** External storage in this climate needs weather-rated wrapping and tie-down; UV is not the enemy, horizontal rain and wind are.

#### Skip and roll-on-roll-off standing

8 RoRo standings, each 3.5 m x 8 m on a 250 mm slab with 12 m of clear pull-off, plus 20 m of overhead clearance for the hook lift and a one-way loop so a skip lorry never reverses across a pedestrian route.

**Why.** Skip exchanges are the most frequent third-party heavy movement on a lot and the one most often designed around after the fact.

**Here.** Open skips fill with rain and snow and the contents blow out. Lidded or netted as standard, and anchored.

#### Delivery windows and booking system

All non-emergency deliveries pre-booked in 30-minute slots. Heavy and abnormal loads restricted to 09:30-11:30 and 13:30-15:30, outside crew arrival (07:00-08:30) and wrap (17:00-20:00). Slots issue an ANPR-linked permit; no permit, no entry.

**Why.** Unbooked deliveries collide with unit moves and crew flow. Slotting converts the gate from a bottleneck into a schedule.

**Here.** Windows must be weather-conditional as well as clock-based: high-sided vehicles cannot run in the gusts that occur here weekly, so the booking system needs a met feed and an automatic re-slot rule.

#### Livestock control: cattle grids and stock fencing

Cattle grids at both site accesses, 4.5 m wide, rated for 44 t, with side gates; 1.2 m stock fencing to the full studio perimeter where it abuts the remaining 800-odd hectares of grazing land.

**Why.** Sheep on the lot are not a joke: they get onto roads, into laydown and under vehicles, and they are somebody's property.

**Here.** Icelandic sheep graze free-range through the summer and move on their own across unfenced land. On 809 hectares of family land this is a daily operational fact, not an edge case.

### State of the art

#### Swept-path model as a live design artefact

AutoTrack/Vehicle Tracking model of the whole lot for a named design-vehicle schedule: 16.5 m artic, 18.75 m drawbar, 12 m rigid, 13.7 m artiste trailer, 4-axle honeywagon, 32 m telescopic low-loader, fire pump, bendy shuttle bus. Re-run before any kerb line changes.

**Why.** Geometry that was fine on day one fails the first time a longer trailer or a new crane arrives; without the model nobody can say yes or no before it is on site.

**Here.** Add the Icelandic snow-plough and gritter to the schedule — they need the same swept path and they run when visibility is worst.

#### Geothermal snow melting under critical surfaces

Snjobraedsla loops under ~8,000 m2: stage aprons, unit base, main pedestrian routes, gate and ramps. Reykjavik systems are designed around 180 W/m2 peak and roughly 430 kWh/m2/yr — here ~1.44 MW thermal and ~3.4 GWh/yr. PEX at 250-300 mm centres, 60-80 mm below surface.

**Why.** Ploughing a stage apron at 04:00 with a shooting crew arriving is noise, damage and delay. Melted surfaces also end salt and grit tracking into stages.

**Here.** Roughly two-thirds of the heat in Reykjavik's snow-melt comes from spent space-heating return water at ~30-35 C, one-third from hot supply. Design the campus heating loop to cascade, or you pay full price for melt.

#### Wind shelter to roads, yards and parking

Porous shelter fencing at ~50% permeability, 6 m high, on the exposed edges of the unit base, parking and laydown; effective shelter extends roughly 10-15 x fence height downwind. Structure designed to ÍST EN 1991-1-4 NA basic wind velocity vb,0 = 36.0 m/s.

**Why.** Wind decides whether trailers can be levelled, MEWPs can lift, skips stay put and doors can open. Shelter buys operating days.

**Here.** Iceland's records: 74.2 m/s gust (Gagnheidi, 16 Jan 1995) and a 50.9 m/s ten-minute mean. Design for survival, then design separately for the wind that stops work.

#### Sheltered / structured parking deck

Multi-storey or deck-covered parking for ~350 cars, on the windward side so it acts as a shelter belt for the yards behind it. Shinfield's consented scheme includes a 601-bay, 6-level deck within a 1,375-space total.

**Why.** Covered parking removes snow clearance, de-icing and windscreen scraping from 350 cars every winter morning and stops door damage in gales.

**Here.** A car deck also protects vehicles from ashfall abrasion, which pits paint and clogs cabin filters.

#### Trailer service pedestals (power, water, foul, data)

Per bay: 1 x 63 A and 1 x 32 A 400 V 3-phase IEC 60309, 2 x 230 V 16 A, potable water with backflow prevention, 100 mm foul gully to the site sewer, and 1 Gb fibre. 20 bays at 63 A is ~872 kVA. RCD per outlet, pedestals heated and raised 600 mm above finished level.

**Why.** Pedestal hookups are what let a base run without diesel; they are also the difference between a two-hour and a two-day base setup.

**Here.** Iceland is 230/400 V 50 Hz, so European CEE hookups are native — but visiting US productions arrive with 120 V equipment and shore-power converters must be on the lot's inventory.

#### Trailer tie-down and wind stabilisation

Cast-in ground anchors on a 4 m grid across the unit base, each rated 25 kN, with a documented tie-down scheme and a wind action plan: strap at forecast gust 25 m/s, close slide-outs and awnings at 20 m/s, evacuate high-sided units above 33 m/s. Thresholds are estimates.

**Why.** An unanchored artiste trailer is a 4 m tall sail. This is the Icelandic failure mode nobody plans for until the first one goes over.

**Here.** Among the windiest inhabited places on earth. A trailer line that would be fine at Shepperton is a write-off here twice a winter.

#### Weighbridge

18 m x 3.5 m pit-mounted weighbridge, 80 t capacity, at the goods gate, integrated with the delivery booking system and ANPR so every inbound and outbound load is weighed and recorded.

**Why.** You cannot prove axle compliance for an outbound abnormal load, or bill waste and scrap by weight, without one — and disputes over both are constant.

**Here.** Icelandic axle limits are tight and spring thaw restrictions are real; a weighbridge is how you keep your own fleet legal on Vegagerdin roads.

#### Gate-to-stage journey times as a published SLA

Target: main gate to any stage door under 4 minutes for a car at 20 km/h; under 6 minutes for an escorted artic; under 90 seconds for a fire appliance on the priority route. Published on the lot map and measured by ANPR timestamps. Estimates.

**Why.** Journey time is the number a production actually feels. Publishing and measuring it is what separates an operated lot from a landlord's car park.

#### Vehicle movement permit and road-closure process

Single lot-wide permit system covering abnormal loads, crane lifts, road closures for filming on lot roads, and hot works in yards. 24-hour notice, published on the daily lot bulletin, with a named marshal and a diversion route for every closure.

**Why.** Lot roads are also shooting locations. Without a closure process a unit dresses a road and a delivery drives through the shot.

#### Helicopter and air ambulance landing area

One 30 m x 30 m marked landing area on a 200 mm slab, clear of a 1:8 approach surface in the prevailing wind directions, lit, with a wind sock, fire point and a segregated vehicle route to it. Never used as parking or laydown.

**Why.** Landhelgisgaeslan's air ambulance is the realistic route to a Reykjavik hospital for a serious lot injury, and it also serves aerial unit work.

**Here.** Fifteen minutes from Reykjavik by road is thirty-plus in a winter storm; the pad is the redundancy on a road that closes.

### What gets got wrong

- The grid, not the site, is the binding constraint. Landsvirkjun has declined supply to new large industrial users and new generation (Burfellslundur wind Q4 2026, Hvammsvirkjun 2028, Theistareykir extension) lags demand; the OECD's 2025 Iceland survey flags 2025-26 as the tight window. Snow melt, 175 charge points and a fully hooked-up unit base is several MW. Secure the Veitur/Landsnet connection agreement before you set a single kerb line, and design the genset pads as a real fallback, not a legacy feature.
- Weather windows, not clock windows, govern deliveries. High-sided curtainsiders and empty containers cannot move in the gusts Iceland gets weekly. A booking system with only time slots will be 40% no-show in winter; it needs a met feed and an automatic re-slot rule, and the marshalling lane needs driver welfare because the alternative is drivers leaving.
- Freight granularity is seven days, not one. Eimskip's Blue Line is weekly (Reykjavik-Rotterdam ~3 days, Reykjavik-Teesport ~4, seven-day rotation). Every laydown, container and consumable store must be sized in sailings, not in days. Missing a Rotterdam cut-off does not delay you by a day, it delays you by a week — and a sailing can itself be weather-delayed.
- American truck assumptions break here. Iceland's routine limits are 10 t single / 11.5 t drive axle, 18 t tandem, 26 t tri-axle, 44 t artic, with exemptions through Samgongustofa. Kit specified against US low-loader practice will need permits, escorts and sometimes a different trailer — check this before you buy a rig or quote a client a move.
- Studded tyres will eat your surfacing. They are legal 1 Nov - 15 Apr and are banned outside that window specifically because they grind asphalt and throw PM10 dust. Budget a spine resurfacing cycle, and expect the dust in stage filters. Concrete on standing areas, hard imported aggregate in the surface course.
- Frost heave and spring thaw-weakening are not weather, they are a design case. Icelandic practice founds to a frost-free depth of 1.2-2.0 m and Vegagerdin runs a real-time frost-depth forecast to impose spring axle restrictions. Build on frost-susceptible subgrade and you will get differential heave under slabs and a bearing-capacity collapse in the exact weeks construction traffic peaks.
- Snow melt is only cheap if you cascade it. Reykjavik's systems take roughly two-thirds of their heat from spent space-heating return water at ~30-35 C. If the campus heating loop is not designed to hand its return water to the snow-melt loops, you are buying ~1.4 MW of primary geothermal heat at full price for the privilege of not ploughing.
- December is dark for the entire working day and June has no darkness at all. Reykjavik's shortest day is about 4 h 07 m; around midsummer it never gets properly dark. Every yard, road and pedestrian route needs designed lighting, and it must be zoned and fully dimmable so a June exterior can kill it. This is a two-sided problem and most lots only solve one side.
- Volcanic ash is a road-and-vehicle event, not just an air-quality one. Fine ash blinds gullies, destroys sweeper pumps, pits paint and clogs filters. Have a standing procedure: wet the roads and drop the speed limit to stop resuspension (the measure used at Vik in 2010), then wash down, then change every filter on the fleet, then clean out the gully sumps.
- Sheep. Iceland's free-ranging summer grazing means livestock on unfenced land, and the studio sits inside 809 hectares of it. Cattle grids at both accesses and a stock-proof perimeter are day-one items; nobody remembers them until an animal is under a low-loader.
- The rebate cliff distorts your sizing. The 25%/35% incentive is legislated to expire at the end of 2028. Sizing pavement, parking and unit base for the utilisation of 2027-28 risks two hectares of surfacing you clear of snow forever. Build the peak into overflow, reinforced grass and phased pours rather than into permanent concrete.
- Almost every driver is a visitor. With a national crew base of roughly a thousand, most HODs and many drivers are imported, on a first visit, in darkness and weather. Wayfinding, marshalling, bilingual signage and a marshal on every abnormal move do more for throughput here than any amount of extra carriageway width.

---

## 4. Campus utilities and resilience

*Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbaer, Iceland (809 ha, 4 stages / 13,200 m2)*

### Essential

#### Grid connection agreement with Veitur, sized once for the finished lot

Connect at 11 kV, not LV. Two feeders, ~630 A each. Contract firm capacity 12 MW on day one against an estimated day-one diversified demand of 4.5–6 MW. Veitur distributes electricity in Mosfellsbaer; its three intakes from Landsnet are Korpa, Geithals and Hnodraholt (Veitur).

**Why.** Iceland's system is at full capacity. Capacity refused later cannot be bought at any price. The first agreement is the only cheap one.

**Here.** Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load (e-fuels, smelters, crypto) citing system capacity and permit delays, and is under ESA investigation over it. A 5 MW studio is a distribution customer, not an industrial contract — that is the loophole to use, quickly.

#### Site primary substation and 11 kV switchroom

Two incomers + bus-section + 12 outgoing ways, six of them spare and cabled to blank ends. 25 kA / 3 s fault rating, IP54, SF6-free vacuum RMUs. Dedicated building on a plinth 600 mm above the 1-in-200 flood level, wind-rated for 66 m/s gusts, with a 20 t transformer laydown and craneage.

**Why.** This one room decides whether stages five through eight are buildable. Spare ways cost 3% now and are unbuildable later without an outage.

**Here.** Shinfield Studios gave up to 1.5 hectares of a 65-acre site to its electricity substation. Take the land now, before the masterplan freezes.

#### Diversified demand study, done properly and revisited every year

Connected production load estimated 5.5 MVA (2 × 1,600 A TPN on the big stage, 1,600 A water stage, 1,250 A each on the twins). Apply 0.35–0.45 diversity → 2.0–2.5 MW production, plus 1.5–2.0 MW base building and workshops, plus 0.6 MW LED volume. Estimated annual consumption 6–9 GWh.

**Why.** Size off connected load and you buy a substation twice too big; size off the average and you brown out on the first big-unit day.

**Here.** Anchor: Sky Studios Elstree's 3 MWp roof array covers at least 40% of site annual energy (SSE Energy Solutions), implying roughly 7 GWh/yr for a comparably sized stage estate.

#### Standby generation: define the island before you buy the machines

Three tiers. Tier 1 (life safety, fire pumps, smoke control, security, comms, BMS): 100% standby, under 15 s. Tier 2 (technical core — data centre, post, LED volume processing, water stage life support and filtration, wardrobe/freezers): UPS-backed, under 15 s. Tier 3 (full production power on all four stages): optional and expensive.

**Why.** A lot that carries only life safety still loses the shoot day. A lot that carries everything buys 8 MW of diesel it runs 20 hours a year.

#### Standby fuel storage, bunded and compliant

2 × 50 m3 double-skinned tanks with interstitial leak detection, giving 72 hours at full standby load (estimate: 3 × 2,000 kVA at 75% burns roughly 1,000 l/h total). Reg. 884/2017 requires bunds over 90% of tank volume, level indication and earthing above 50 m3, and 10-yearly bottom thickness checks; underground lines pressure-tested every 5 years.

**Why.** Iceland is an island with weather-dependent freight. 24 hours of fuel is not resilience, it is a countdown.

**Here.** Double-skinned tanks with interstitial leak detection satisfy the bund requirement under reg. 884/2017 and are the right answer on a seismically active, high-groundwater site.

#### Earthing, bonding and a single site earth reference

TN-S throughout with a site earth mat under the substation, target earth electrode resistance under 1 ohm. Continuous earth ring around the campus tied to every unit substation, stage steel, fire main and tank. Step and touch potential study. Separate clean-earth reference for audio and camera, bonded at one point only.

**Why.** Hum bars, buzz on the boom and camera-to-genset potential differences all come from more than one earth reference.

**Here.** Verify the earthing system Veitur actually presents at the point of connection — Icelandic LV networks are not uniformly TN, and older/rural sections have run IT. Design your own island earth rather than inheriting one.

#### Buried services throughout — no overhead line anywhere on site

All HV, LV, comms and lighting underground in a shared duct bank with draw pits at 60 m and at every change of direction. Minimum 30% spare ducts. External lighting columns on hinged bases, wind-rated to 66 m/s with vibration dampers.

**Why.** Overhead distribution on this site is a guaranteed annual outage. Buried is 3–4× the capital cost and pays back in one storm.

**Here.** On 10–11 December 2019 the IMO issued its first-ever red alert; a 149 mph (67 m/s) gust was recorded at Skalafell, roughly 32 km east of Reykjavik, and 40 towers failed nationally. That is the design storm for this site.

#### Incoming potable water: two mains, two directions

Two independent connections from the Mosfellsbaer network on separate routes, each sized for 100% of site demand (estimate 25 l/s peak instantaneous, excluding fire and tank filling). Bulk meter with remote read, double-check backflow preventers (Category 5 for the water stage and workshops).

**Why.** A single main means one contractor's bucket takes out the lot's drinking water, its sprinklers' top-up and its water stage in one go.

**Here.** Mosfellsbaer runs its own vatnsveita but buys cold water wholesale from Veitur — so there is one utility and one commercial counterparty behind two pipes. Confirm true source diversity, not just two pipes from one main.

#### Fire main ring and hydrants

250 mm ductile iron ring main around the whole developed area, valved into sections so any one break isolates without losing hydrant coverage. Hydrants at 90 m maximum spacing (NFPA 24 practice) and within 45 m of every stage door. Static pressure 8–10 bar, residual not below 3.5 bar at 70 l/s.

**Why.** Hydrant coverage from the public main alone will not reach a 6,000 m2 stage's far corner, and Icelandic fire water demand is calculated at 70 l/s.

**Here.** Slokkvilid hofudborgarsvaedisins covers Mosfellsbaer; a rural-edge site should assume a longer first-attendance time than an urban Reykjavik plot and size on-site provision accordingly (estimate).

#### Dedicated fire water tank and pump house

1,000 m3 dedicated fire tank (2 hours at 70 l/s plus sprinkler demand), auto-refilled, never drawn on by any other service. Two 100%-duty pumps to EN 12845 / NFPA 20: one electric on the essential board, one diesel with its own day tank. Weekly automatic churn test, annual full flow test.

**Why.** Public mains pressure in a rural municipality is not a fire strategy. The tank and the diesel pump are what the insurer actually underwrites.

#### Water stage tank: fill, drain and make-up

Estimated 6,000 m3 at 2.5 m working depth over 2,400 m2. Dedicated 200 mm fill main at 40 l/s fills in about 42 hours; fill must not be able to depress fire main pressure — interlock it. Make-up for evaporation estimated 5–15 m3/day. Separate 300 mm gravity drain-down.

**Why.** Filling and dumping the tank is the single largest hydraulic event on the campus and will break every other water service if it is not isolated from them.

#### Controlled tank drain-down route

6,000 m3 cannot enter the foul sewer. Route to an on-site 1,500 m3 attenuation basin, de-chlorinate and dechlorinate-test before discharge, then release to the surface water system at a consented rate (target under 50 l/s, i.e. a 33-hour drain). Silt and hydrocarbon traps ahead of discharge.

**Why.** An uncontrolled dump is a pollution incident, a fish kill and a criminal matter, and it is the most likely way this lot ends up in the news.

**Here.** Discharge lands in the Varma / Leirvogsa catchment, which holds salmonids. The consent will be tighter than the volume suggests and should be negotiated at planning, not at first fill.

#### Foul drainage and the connection to Veitur's system

Fully separate foul and surface systems (never combined). Gravity to a duty/standby packaged pumping station with 24-hour emergency storage, twin rising mains, level telemetry to the SCADA. Design peak foul flow estimated 12–18 l/s with 1,500 people on site.

**Why.** Combined systems overflow. Iceland's capital-area system already discharges diluted sewage in heavy rain because it is combined; do not join that problem.

**Here.** Capital-area wastewater goes to Anaust and Klettagardar for mechanical treatment and is pumped about 4 km out into Faxafloi (Veitur). Trade effluent standards are set by that treatment level.

#### Oil separators on every vehicle and plant surface

Class I full-retention separators to IST EN 858 on unit base, truck marshalling, workshop yard, fuel fill point and genset compound. Discharge under 5 mg/l for Class I; reg. 884/2017 sets 15 mg/kg for fuel-facility separators, with monthly checks and automatic closure devices.

**Why.** A lot runs 60+ vehicles and a fuel point on hardstanding that drains to a salmon river. The separators are the whole environmental case.

#### Surface water attenuation and greenfield discharge

Attenuate the estimated 20–25 ha of impermeable area to greenfield runoff rate (about 5 l/s/ha, so roughly 100–125 l/s total). Estimated storage 5,000–7,000 m3 in swales, a wet basin and permeable pavement. Design to 1-in-30 no surface flooding, 1-in-100 plus 20% climate uplift no building flooding.

**Why.** 809 hectares of family land means the lot is its own upstream catchment. Discharge unattenuated and you flood your own backlot before you flood anyone else's.

**Here.** Reykjavik's climate normal is 870 mm/yr (IMO, 1991–2020); Mosfellsdalur, under Esja and Mosfellsheidi, is materially wetter (estimate 1,000–1,300 mm/yr). Reykjavik combined-sewer flood work uses a Chicago design storm with a 10-minute burst at a 5-year return period.

#### Geothermal district heat connection

Two 150 mm connections from the Veitur mains on separate routes, water delivered at up to 80 °C, billed per cubic metre (Veitur). Estimated site heat demand 2.5–4 MW peak for 45,000 m2 gross plus tank heating. Pressure-reducing and metering station at the energy centre, then distribute at 80 °C site-wide.

**Why.** Heat here is cheap, low-carbon and abundant. Anything the lot burns or resistively heats is a design failure.

**Here.** The Reykir and Reykjahlid low-temperature fields sit 4–7 km away in Mosfellsbaer and supply about a third of the capital area's peak hot water, historically up to 1,700 l/s; Veitur is drilling seven more wells at Reykir. The resource is literally next door.

#### Plant room strategy: central energy centre plus local per-stage plant

Central: 11 kV switchroom, transformers, gensets, fuel, UPS, BESS, fire pumps and tank, compressed air, potable break tanks, district heat intake and metering, BMS head end, water treatment. Local per stage: air handling, dehumidification, stage LV boards, stage-specific control. Nothing rotating within 15 m of an acoustic envelope.

**Why.** Central wins on efficiency, redundancy and one maintenance access. Local wins on acoustics, tenant metering and the ability to shut one stage without touching another.

**Here.** Because there are no boilers or chillers to centralise, the central plant here is mostly electrical and water — which makes the energy centre smaller and the case for per-stage air handling stronger than at a UK lot.

#### Ash and volcanic gas filtration on every air and combustion intake

Two-stage filtration on all AHUs and genset intakes: ISO 16890 coarse pre-filter plus ePM1 60–80% main filter, with activated carbon / potassium permanganate cassettes for SO2 and H2S. Differential pressure alarms to SCADA, a full spare filter set in stock, and a campus-wide recirculation mode that closes fresh air intakes on an air quality alert.

**Why.** Ash destroys generator engines and blinds a filter bank in hours. SO2 events reach the capital area and the public advice is to close windows and turn off ventilation.

**Here.** The Reykjanes eruptions have put SO2 over Reykjavik repeatedly; above 2,000 µg/m3 the advice is to stay indoors with windows closed. Over 20 reference-grade air quality stations sit within 40 km — feed their data straight into the BMS and automate the intake damper response.

#### Comms: diverse fibre entries and honest egress figures

Two physically separate fibre entries from different directions, different ducts, different chambers, terminating in two separate on-site meet-me rooms. Minimum 10 Gbps committed with 100 Gbps burst to the Reykjavik exchanges. On-site OM4/OS2 backbone to every stage, workshop and post room.

**Why.** Dailies, VFX plates and volume content move as terabytes daily. One fibre entry means one digger stops the pipeline.

**Here.** Egress is genuinely good: the state-owned Farice runs IRIS (108 Tbps capacity, about 10.5 ms Reykjavik–Dublin, in service March 2023), FARICE-1 (about 11 Tbps lit) and DANICE. Iceland is not the bandwidth backwater producers assume — say so in the sales deck.

#### Operating licences and consents held before first shoot

Starfsleyfi from the local health authority (Heilbrigdiseftirlit) for the fuel and chemical stores and the effluent discharge; UST registration under reg. 884/2017 for oil storage; discharge consent for surface water and tank drain-down; fire authority sign-off on the water supply and access. Hold a live consents register with expiry dates in the BMS.

**Why.** Any one of these lapsing stops production, and none of them can be obtained in the week you notice.

#### Critical spares held on site, sized for Icelandic freight

One spare 1,600 kVA transformer, one spare RMU, a full set of HV cable joints and terminations, two fire pump seal kits, a full filter set, spare drainage pump, and 5 km of assorted cable. Estimated resupply from northern Europe is 7–14 days door to door by sea and is weather-dependent.

**Why.** Everything structural is imported. A failed transformer with a 30-week factory lead time and a 10-day sail is a season lost.

**Here.** Air freight exists but not for a 6-tonne transformer. Stock what cannot fly.

### Expected

#### Ring main: open-ring 11 kV around the campus

Single 11 kV ring, normally open at the midpoint, feeding a unit substation at each stage and at the workshops, energy centre, backlot and unit base. 240–300 mm2 XLPE, buried 900 mm in a shared duct bank. Any single cable fault loses one arm; restoration by switching, target under 5 minutes.

**Why.** A radial spur means one digger or one joint failure dark-stages a shoot day. A ring turns a fault into a switching operation.

#### Unit substation per stage, sized for the stage's whole appetite

Estimate per stage: 6,000 m2 room → 2 × 1,600 kVA 11/0.4 kV; water stage 3,200 m2 → 1 × 1,600 kVA; each 2,000 m2 stage → 1 × 1,000 kVA. Cast-resin, K-13 rated for harmonics, Dyn11, 6% impedance. Sited outside the acoustic envelope with no hard connection to the stage structure.

**Why.** Transformer hum and fan noise inside an acoustic envelope is unrecoverable. Sizing per stage also makes tenant recharge honest.

#### Standby generator plant

3 × 2,000 kVA prime-rated sets, synchronised, N+1, ISO 8528 class G3 (frequency and voltage stable enough for camera and LED loads). Radiator-cooled, acoustic enclosure to 65 dB(A) at 10 m, storm louvres, ash pre-filters. Monthly 60-minute on-load test against a 2 MW resistive/reactive load bank.

**Why.** G2 sets wander enough in frequency to beat against shutter and LED refresh. Untested sets fail on the night they matter.

**Here.** Iceland's own December 2019 storm knocked out 40 transmission towers nationally at an estimated USD 40 million cost — grid outage here is a real, dated event, not a hypothetical.

#### Fuel specification, polishing and turnover

HVO (hydrotreated vegetable oil) where a compliant, continuously available supply can be contracted; otherwise EN 590 winter-grade diesel with cold filter plugging point at or below −20 °C and biocide. Continuous fuel polishing loop, quarterly ISO 4406 particulate and water test, full turnover every 12 months.

**Why.** Waxed or bug-infested fuel is the commonest cause of a standby set failing to take load. Untouched diesel degrades in a year.

**Here.** HVO supply in Iceland is thin and should be treated as a procurement risk, not an assumption — verify with N1/Olis before designing the tank around it.

#### UPS for the technical core

2N modular UPS, 2 × 500 kVA, 10-minute autonomy at design load, lithium-iron-phosphate. Feeds the data centre, editorial/post, LED volume processing, camera and DIT power on stage, network core and the water stage's life-support and filtration controls. Static bypass, external maintenance bypass.

**Why.** Fifteen seconds of dark on a volume shoot loses the take, the tracking calibration and the render nodes. Ten minutes covers genset start plus a graceful stop.

#### On-site solar, honestly scoped

Roof-mounted PV on stage and workshop roofs, estimated 1.5–2.5 MWp across roughly 15,000 m2. Estimated yield 750–900 kWh/kWp/yr at 64°N, i.e. roughly 1.5–2.0 GWh/yr — but near-zero output in December and January. Snow and ash shedding pitch, wind uplift design to 66 m/s.

**Why.** Worth having for daytime summer load and for the carbon story, worthless for winter firm capacity. Do not let it into the capacity calculation.

**Here.** Sky Studios Elstree's 7,400-panel 3 MWp array at 51°N covers 40%+ of annual site energy. At 64°N the same array covers far less, and none of it in the months the lot most needs light.

#### Power quality regime for a lot full of LED and dimmers

Hold to EN 50160 at the point of common coupling; internally target THD(V) under 5% and THD(I) under 8% at each stage board. Active harmonic filters at the two largest stage substations, K-13 transformers, oversized neutrals (200% on final circuits), flicker limit Pst under 1.0.

**Why.** LED fixtures, dimmer racks, VFDs and EV chargers are all non-linear. Third-harmonic neutral current is what melts a stage's tails.

#### Metering, sub-metering and tenant recharge

Half-hourly metering at the point of connection; Class 0.5S sub-meters on every stage, workshop, unit base, office block, EV bank and the water stage plant. Automatic recharge into the booking system, with a tenant portal showing kWh, kW peak and kgCO2e per production per day.

**Why.** Power is a recharged cost, not overhead. Without per-stage metering the lot subsidises whichever production runs the biggest rig.

**Here.** BAFTA albert and the streamers' sustainability reporting now ask productions for energy data by production — a lot that hands it over in a clean export wins bookings.

#### Protection coordination and arc flash study

Full study at commissioning and re-run after every switchboard change. Grade upstream/downstream so a stage fault clears at the stage, not at the incomer. Arc flash labels with incident energy in cal/cm2 at every board; maintenance-mode switch on the 11 kV incomers to cut clearing time.

**Why.** An ungraded fault on stage 3 that trips the whole campus is a four-stage lost day, and lot electricians get in front of live boards daily.

#### On-site potable storage and boosters

2 × 250 m3 stainless break tanks (48 hours of non-fire demand, estimate) in the energy centre, duty/standby/assist variable-speed booster set, 4.5 bar at the highest outlet, chlorination or UV on the outlet, tanks compartmented so one can be cleaned in service.

**Why.** Break tanks give you a network outage buffer, a fixed inlet pressure to design plant against, and legal separation from the public main.

**Here.** Icelandic cold water is unchlorinated groundwater of very high quality — which means the moment you store it you own the microbiology. Design for turnover under 24 hours and monitor.

#### Sprinkler design basis stated once, campus-wide

NFPA 140 as the governing standard for the stages, with NFPA 13 for installation; stages typically Ordinary Hazard Group 2 rising to Extra Hazard where high-piled scenery and pyro are permitted. Workshops Extra Hazard Group 1. Design density and duration set the fire tank, not the other way round.

**Why.** NFPA 140 exists specifically for soundstages and production facilities. Designing a stage to a generic warehouse class either over-sprinklers it or leaves the grid unprotected.

**Here.** Iceland's byggingarreglugerd 112/2012 and reg. 723/2017 govern; an NFPA-based design will need a fire engineer's justification to HMS and the local fire authority as an equivalence case.

#### Tank water treatment and turnover

Filtration at 4–6 turnovers per day (estimate 250–375 l/s), sand or regenerative media, UV plus low-residual chlorination or bromine to a potable-adjacent standard, continuous ORP/pH/turbidity monitoring, and a bypass so a production can run clear, dyed or dressed water without contaminating the plant.

**Why.** A tank shot over six weeks with 40 performers a day is a swimming pool with a bigger bather load and no showers-before-entry rule.

#### Trade effluent handling from workshops and scenic

Plaster, paint-wash and scenic areas on a separate drainage leg to a settlement and pH-correction plant: 3-stage silt traps, pH 6–10 correction, solids skip. Plaster and joint compound never to the sewer. Discharge consent with the health authority listing pH, suspended solids, COD and hydrocarbon limits.

**Why.** Set-build effluent sets like concrete in the sewer. One plaster washout line has closed studio drainage for weeks.

#### Snowmelt and rain-on-snow in the drainage model

Design the surface system for rain-on-snow, not summer rainfall: a warm Atlantic front over a 400 mm snowpack produces peak flows well above any convective storm. Heated gullies and outfalls, oversized (minimum 225 mm) laterals, no flat falls, and no outfall that can freeze shut.

**Why.** An outfall frozen shut in February turns the attenuation basin into a lake and the backlot into a bog.

**Here.** This, not summer storms, is the governing drainage case at 64°N and it is what most imported drainage designs get wrong.

#### Once-through district heat and its plant-room consequences

Reykjavik's system is direct-use: geothermal water goes into radiators and taps without heat exchangers, and in the single-pipe case the return drains to sewer. No boilers, no calorifiers, no gas flues, no plant-room chimneys. Use heat exchangers only where the tank or a chilled circuit demands separation.

**Why.** It removes an entire building services discipline from the lot, and it means a heating fault is a plumbing fault, not a combustion one.

**Here.** Imported UK/US M&E designers will draw boilers and calorifiers by reflex. Brief them out of it at concept stage or you will pay for plant rooms you do not need.

#### Cooling and heat rejection

Free cooling almost year-round: dry air coolers sized on a 12 °C summer design ambient, no mechanical chillers for base building. Mechanical cooling only for the data centre, LED volume processing and dehumidification over the water stage. Estimated total rejection 1.2–1.8 MW. Adiabatic assist not required.

**Why.** A US or UK cooling design imported wholesale here buys chillers that never run and pays their standing losses forever.

**Here.** The same 12 °C ambient that kills the chiller case makes the dehumidification load over a heated 2,400 m2 tank the real cooling problem — estimate 480–960 kW of latent load off the water surface alone.

#### Compressed air ring main

Central plant: 2 × 55 kW variable-speed oil-free screw compressors plus one standby, 7 bar g working, ISO 8573-1 Class 0 oil, −40 °C pressure dew point, 2 × 4,000 l receivers. Stainless or aluminium ring around the campus with isolatable spurs; per-stage allowance 1,500–2,000 l/min free air.

**Why.** Scenic, paint, pneumatic effects, air movers and shop tools all draw from it. Oil carryover ruins paint finishes and contaminates FX rigs.

**Here.** −40 °C dew point is not a luxury on an outdoor ring here; anything wetter freezes the spurs and the outdoor drop points in December.

#### Seismic restraint of plant and flexible utility crossings

Design to IST EN 1998-1/NA:2010. The national map runs 0.05 g to 0.50 g; the capital region sits in the low band, well under the South Iceland Seismic Zone's 0.50 g (estimate 0.05–0.10 g for Mosfellsbaer — confirm against the NA map). Seismically restrain all plant; flexible bellows on every pipe crossing a building joint; seismic gas/water shutoff valves.

**Why.** The pipes and the transformer bushings fail long before the frame does. A ruptured district heat main under a stage is 80 °C water into a floor tank.

**Here.** Low PGA is not zero PGA, and the ground here moves often enough that flexible crossings and restrained plant are cheap insurance rather than over-engineering.

#### EV and vehicle charging

Estimated 400 kW day-one: 40 × 7–22 kW AC bays for crew, 4 × 150 kW DC for unit and camera trucks, plus 2 × 350 kW for electric HGVs. Dynamic load management capping the bank against the site's contracted demand. Trenching and ducting for 4× that count laid at first build.

**Why.** Crew arrive in electric cars and productions increasingly arrive with electric unit vehicles. Uncontrolled, the charging bank alone eats a megawatt of contracted capacity.

**Here.** Iceland has one of the highest EV shares of any national fleet, so the crew-park load is real from opening day rather than a future allowance.

#### No gas anywhere, and an LPG strategy for the exceptions

Iceland has no natural gas distribution network. All heat is geothermal, all cooking is electric or bottled LPG. Provide a permitted bulk LPG store for SFX flame work and catering: bunded, 6 m separation, gas detection, remote shutoff, with a licensed refill route — and a separate pyrotechnic magazine.

**Why.** SFX will need propane and the caterers will want it. Discovering there is no gas main after the kitchens are designed is a rebuild.

**Here.** The absence of a gas grid is a large part of why an Icelandic lot can credibly claim to be all-electric and low-carbon without doing anything clever.

### State of the art

#### Capacity headroom held for future stages

Reserve 100% of day-one demand as contracted spare: 12 MW contracted, 6 MW used. Lay the second ring's ducts in the first dig. Leave two switchboard bays, two transformer plinths and a genset plinth empty and foundationed. Reserve 0.4 ha next to the substation as unbuildable.

**Why.** Every studio that grew wishes it had. In Iceland the constraint is not money or land, it is whether the network will say yes a second time.

**Here.** Iceland's grid is expected to be tightest in 2025–26 before new generation arrives; Landsnet's capital-region reinforcement runs through a new Lyklafell substation feeding Hamranes.

#### Battery energy storage as a capacity extender, not just a backup

4 MWh / 2 MW grid-tied BESS at the energy centre, DC-coupled to the roof PV. Peak-shaving the site's 15-minute maximum demand, soft-starting big lighting rigs, and standing in for the first genset. Fire-rated enclosure, deflagration venting, 3 m separation, dedicated water supply.

**Why.** It lets a 12 MW-contracted lot behave like a 16 MW one, which matters when the network will not sell you more.

**Here.** Where the grid is the binding constraint and energy itself is cheap and clean, storage buys capacity rather than arbitrage — the opposite of the usual business case.

#### On-site wind generation, considered and probably declined

A single 850 kW–2 MW turbine at a site with mean wind speeds among the highest of any inhabited place would plausibly reach a 45–55% capacity factor (estimate). Against that: icing throw, infrasound and blade tonality into the stages, radar and aviation consultation, and planning through Mosfellsbaer.

**Why.** The one renewable that actually works in an Icelandic winter, and the one that most threatens a sound stage's acoustic case.

**Here.** Iceland has almost no operating wind capacity despite the resource; Burfellslundur (about 120 MW) is the first at scale. A studio would be an unusual and slow first mover.

#### Backlot and exterior power pods

Weatherproof power pillars at 60–80 m spacing along backlot roads and around exterior sets: per pod 1 × 400 A, 2 × 125 A, 4 × 63 A and 6 × 32 A 400 V outlets plus 230 V, RCD-protected, IP67, heated and lockable. Fed from the ring, not from LV spurs off the stages.

**Why.** Kills the diesel generator on the backlot, which is the single biggest carbon and noise line on a location day.

**Here.** With 21 hours of usable light in June and under 5 in December, the backlot's real electrical peak is winter night lighting — size the pods for lighting rigs, not for catering.

#### American 60 Hz / 120–208 V production supply

Static frequency converters at each stage substation: 300 kVA per 2,000 m2 stage, 500 kVA on the big stage, output 208 V/120 V three-phase 60 Hz, THD under 3%, plus US-pattern camlok/Bates tie-ins. Iceland is 230/400 V 50 Hz, Type C/F sockets.

**Why.** An incoming American production arrives with 60 Hz ballasts, motors, dollies and battery chargers. Without this they either rent converters or re-lamp.

**Here.** This is a real selling point against European competitors and costs a fraction of a stage. It is also how you win a US studio picture chasing the 25%/35% rebate before it expires at end-2028.

#### Campus SCADA / building management, one head end

Single head end covering 11 kV protection and switching, transformer temperatures, genset and UPS status, BESS state of charge, fire pump status, tank levels, district heat flow and temperature, compressed air pressure and dew point, drainage pump station levels, and every sub-meter. Redundant servers, out-of-band access.

**Why.** A duty engineer at 03:00 in a storm needs one screen, not eleven proprietary front ends in eleven plant rooms.

#### Water stage tank heating from district heat

Raising 6,000 m3 from 6 °C to 28 °C needs about 153 MWh. Delivered as 80 °C geothermal water cooled to 30 °C, that is roughly 2,640 m3 of hot water — a four-figure US dollar sum at Veitur tariffs (estimate). Plate heat exchanger, 2 × 1.5 MW, plus a 600–950 kW trim load to hold temperature.

**Why.** Cast and stunt performers work in that water for weeks. Anywhere else, tank heating is a budget line producers fight over.

**Here.** This is the lot's single strongest commercial argument. In Los Angeles or Malta the same heat is propane or electricity; here it is near-free geothermal water. Sell the tank temperature, not the tank.

#### Snow melting under the roads, aprons and stage doors

Cascade the 30–40 °C district heat return into embedded pipework. Icelandic design practice: 100 W/m2 idling, 30/15 °C supply/return, 5.7 l/m2·h flow, roughly 430 kWh/m2·yr. Heat the fire routes, elephant door aprons, ramps, unit base and main circulation — estimate 10,000–12,000 m2, about 1.0–1.2 MW idle.

**Why.** It is the difference between a lot that shoots in February and a lot that cancels. It also removes gritting salt from the drainage and the vehicle fleet.

**Here.** Iceland has 215 MW / 378 GWh a year of installed snow melting, about two thirds of it powered by heating return water that would otherwise go to the sewer. Reykjavik runs about 30,000 m2 of it in the old centre.

#### Backup heat and the single-point-of-failure on district heating

A 500 m3 insulated thermal store (estimated 8–12 hours of frost protection and tank hold at design conditions) plus electric immersion trim on the essential board. Freeze-protection sequence that sheds comfort heating to protect the tank, sprinkler tank and pipework.

**Why.** District heat is one pipe from one field. Losing it in a January storm freezes wet systems across four stages before the pipe is repaired.

**Here.** In February 2024 lava from the Sundhnukur fissure severed the Svartsengi–Fitjar hot water main and cut heat to Reykjanesbaer, Sudurnesjabaer and Grindavik; residents were told to lower heating and delay switching on electric heaters to avoid overloading the grid. Restored in four days. Assume it can happen here.

#### High-pressure breathing air and gas store for the water stage

Diver breathing air to EN 12021: 300 bar compressor, 4-cylinder storage bank giving at least 8 hours of surface-supplied diving for two divers plus reserve, CO/CO2/oil monitoring, quarterly air quality certificate. Separate bottled nitrogen, argon and CO2 store, bunded, ventilated, away from the plant room.

**Why.** A 2,400 m2 tank means standby divers and safety divers on every water day; you cannot buy that air locally at short notice.

**Here.** Specialist gas supply in Iceland is thin and freight-dependent. Hold deeper stock than a mainland lot would.

#### Utility spine: a walkable services corridor, not a buried guess

A 3 m wide × 2.5 m high walkable service tunnel or a continuous covered trench along the main spine carrying HV, LV, comms, district heat flow and return, potable, fire main and compressed air, with 40% spare tray and duct capacity. Drained, lit, alarmed, seismically detailed at building crossings.

**Why.** Every future stage, every capacity upgrade and every fault repair happens in this corridor. Buried direct means digging up the road that trucks are parked on.

#### Corrosion regime for electronics and switchgear

Classify control rooms, the data centre and switchrooms to ANSI/ISA-71.04 severity level G1 and monitor with copper/silver reactivity coupons quarterly. Sealed or positively pressurised, filtered switchrooms; conformal-coated boards where practical.

**Why.** Geothermal H2S tarnishes silver and attacks copper on circuit boards. It is a slow, invisible failure mode that takes out switchgear relays and servers years early.

**Here.** A well-known Icelandic problem near geothermal plant and one that imported M&E designs never account for.

#### Utility asset register, GIS and as-built digital twin

Every duct, chamber, valve, hydrant, isolator, meter and joint surveyed to ±50 mm and held in GIS with photographs, so a repair crew opens the right chamber first time. Tie to the BMS so a live alarm points at a real asset. Veitur runs its own network on Esri GIS.

**Why.** On an 809-hectare site with 20+ km of buried services, an incomplete as-built record turns a two-hour fault into a two-day dig.

#### Commissioning, black-building test and annual proving

Full black-building test before handover and annually thereafter: kill the incomers, prove genset start and take-up under 15 s, UPS hold, fire pump auto-start, BESS islanding, tank life support continuity, and district heat freeze protection. Publish the results and the restoration time to tenants.

**Why.** Untested resilience is a story. A published, dated black-building result is a commercial asset when a studio's risk team audits the lot.

#### Single-points-of-failure register and a utility outage playbook

A maintained register naming every SPOF (one district heat field, one fire tank, one fuel supplier, one wastewater treatment route) with its mitigation, its restoration time and its owner. Outage playbook by scenario, rehearsed twice a year with the duty engineers and the stage managers.

**Why.** Producers and completion bond insurers now ask this question directly. Having the document is part of the booking case.

**Here.** Integrate with Almannavarnir and the IMO alert levels so a red weather warning, an eruption or an ash advisory automatically triggers a defined lot state rather than an argument.

#### Production power service level, priced and contracted

Publish a stage power SLA: guaranteed capacity in amps per stage, restoration target under 5 minutes for a ring fault, under 15 seconds for the technical core, and a stated liability position. Price the difference between grid-only and standby-backed stage power as a bookable option.

**Why.** A lost shoot day on a mid-budget streaming production is roughly USD 150,000–400,000 (estimate). That number, not the capital cost, is what justifies every item above.

### What gets got wrong

- The binding constraint is not money, it is the connection agreement. Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load and Iceland's system is running at full capacity into 2025–26; a studio must present as a distribution-network customer of Veitur, not as industrial load, and must contract its ultimate capacity in the first agreement. Contracting 6 MW because that is what day one needs is the mistake that caps the lot at four stages forever.
- District heat is a single pipe from a single field and it can be severed. In February 2024 lava from the Sundhnukur fissure cut the Svartsengi–Fitjar main and left Reykjanesbaer, Sudurnesjabaer and Grindavik without heat for four days, with authorities asking people not to switch on electric heaters because the grid could not carry the swap. Design freeze protection that does not assume either utility.
- Reykjavik district heating is direct-use and largely once-through: geothermal water goes straight into radiators and taps and, in single-pipe areas, drains to sewer afterwards. Imported UK/US M&E consultants will draw boilers, calorifiers and flues by reflex. Brief them out of it at concept or you pay for plant rooms, chimneys and a gas strategy that Iceland does not have.
- There is no natural gas network in Iceland at all. Every kitchen, every SFX flame effect and every heater assumption in an imported design has to be re-based on electricity, geothermal water or bottled LPG, and the LPG store needs its own permit and separation distances.
- Rain-on-snow, not summer storms, governs the surface water design at 64°N. A warm Atlantic front over a deep snowpack produces the peak flow, and a frozen outfall in February turns the attenuation basin into a lake. Any drainage model imported from a temperate design office will be sized for the wrong event.
- The water stage tank is a hydraulic bomb in both directions. 6,000 m3 filled at 40 l/s will depress fire main pressure unless interlocked, and dumped uncontrolled it is a pollution incident into a salmonid catchment. Negotiate the drain-down consent at planning stage, and provide an attenuation basin and a de-chlorination step before discharge.
- Icelandic fire water demand is calculated at 70 l/s and the site is at the rural edge of the Slokkvilid hofudborgarsvaedisins area. Public mains pressure is not a fire strategy — the dedicated tank, the ring main and the diesel pump are what the insurer actually underwrites, and NFPA 140 (the soundstage-specific standard) will need a fire engineer's equivalence case against byggingarreglugerd 112/2012 and reg. 723/2017.
- Volcanic gas is a chronic utilities problem, not just an event. SO2 from the Reykjanes eruptions reaches the capital area repeatedly, and geothermal H2S corrodes copper and silver on circuit boards. Classify switchrooms and the data centre to ANSI/ISA-71.04 G1 and monitor with reactivity coupons, or lose relays and servers years early with no obvious cause.
- Verify the earthing arrangement Veitur presents at the point of connection rather than assuming TN-C-S. Icelandic low-voltage networks are not uniformly TN and older sections have run IT. Getting this wrong shows up as hum on the boom and as camera-body-to-genset potential differences on the first big exterior night.
- Solar is a carbon story, not capacity. At 64°N a 2 MWp array produces essentially nothing in December and January — the exact months the backlot needs the most light. Never let PV into the firm capacity calculation, and never let a sustainability consultant put it there.
- Everything structural is imported and freight is weather-dependent. A 1,600 kVA transformer will not fly. Hold a spare transformer, a spare RMU, jointing kits, pump seals and a full filter set on site, and size fuel storage for 72 hours rather than 24 because the tanker's arrival depends on a sailing.
- The Icelandic PGA figure for Mosfellsbaer needs confirming against the actual IST EN 1998-1/NA:2010 map. The national annex runs 0.05 g to 0.50 g and the capital region sits in the low band, but the annex has effectively not been revised since 2010 and the low band is not zero — restrain plant and use flexible pipe crossings regardless.
- The 2019 storm is the honest design case: an IMO red alert, a 149 mph gust at Skalafell about 32 km from the site, 40 transmission towers down and an estimated USD 40 million outage cost. Any overhead distribution on this campus is a guaranteed annual outage; bury everything and wind-rate every external enclosure to 66 m/s.
- American 60 Hz / 120–208 V provision is the cheapest competitive differentiator on this list and the one most likely to be value-engineered out. With the 25%/35% rebate legislated to expire at the end of 2028, the window for winning a US studio picture is short, and a production that has to import frequency converters will look at Hungary or Malta instead.
- A capacity headroom reservation that exists only in a spreadsheet is not headroom. It has to be spare switchboard ways cabled to blank ends, empty foundationed transformer plinths, 30% spare ducts in the first dig, and a contracted figure with Veitur. All four, or none of it counts.

---

## 5. Network, data and connectivity at campus scale

*Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær, Iceland*

### Essential

#### Campus dark-fibre backbone, dual counter-rotating rings

Two physically diverse OS2 (ITU-T G.652.D) rings, 288-fibre armoured, ~4.2 km and ~5.1 km circumference (est.); every building lands on both. Minimum 24 fibres per building per ring, 50% left dark. Loss budget 0.35 dB/km @1310 nm, splices ≤0.10 dB.

**Why.** One digger, one flood or one quake on a single path takes the whole lot off air mid-shoot day.

**Here.** All ducted, never aerial: gusts above 50 m/s and rime icing make aerial fibre a liability here.

#### Duct, chamber and pathway infrastructure

2× 110 mm primary ducts plus 4× 32 mm subducts per route; 1.2 m cover under roads; draw pits every ≤100 m and at each direction change; 3 m slack loop per chamber; 50% spare subduct kept empty and mandrel-proved at handover.

**Why.** Retrofitting duct across a live lot means trenching between working stages. Spare duct is the cheapest thing you will ever buy.

**Here.** Frost heave and seismic ground strain: slack loops and flexible building entries stop splice closures being pulled apart.

#### Two carrier meet-me rooms, diverse entries

Two carrier entry rooms in different buildings ≥50 m apart, each with its own duct entry from a different compass bearing, 4 carrier racks, A/B AC and -48 V DC, each terminating one of the two site rings.

**Why.** A fire or flood in one entry room should cost you a carrier, not the island link.

#### Core switching fabric

Spine-leaf across the two node buildings: 4 spines at 32×400G, leaf uplinks 2×100G, EVPN-VXLAN with a per-production VNI set, ECMP, sub-2 µs switch latency, 25G to workstations, 10G floor boxes, non-blocking to storage.

**Why.** Per-production isolation has to be a fabric primitive, not a firewall rule bolted on later.

#### Building distribution rooms (one SER per building)

4× 42U racks, two diverse fibre entries, 2×100G to core, 16 kW cooling, 6 kVA UPS at 30 min autonomy, environmental sensors and door contacts. Second SER in the 6,000 m² stage so no horizontal copper run exceeds 90 m.

**Why.** Cat6A dies at 90 m; a 30 m-high, 6,000 m² room cannot be served from one closet.

#### Central machine room (MER) — sizing

350 m² white space (est.), 60 racks at 900×1200 mm, 8 kW/rack average with four high-density rows at 35 kW/rack on rear-door heat exchangers; 600 kW IT day one, structured to 1.5 MW; hot/cold aisle containment; overhead cable, no raised floor.

**Why.** Undersizing forces a second room later, which is how lots end up with two of everything and one of nothing.

#### MER power topology

2N: two 11 kV Veitur feeds, two transformers, A/B PDUs to dual-corded kit, N+1 lithium UPS in 250 kW modules at 10 min autonomy, one 1,250 kVA standby generator with 72 h fuel, concurrent maintainability to Uptime Tier III intent.

**Why.** A machine room drop kills every camera-to-cloud upload, every door, every radio patch and the dailies at once.

**Here.** A sub-2 MW load is a Veitur distribution connection, not a Landsnet/Landsvirkjun transmission contract (est. reading) — which is exactly why the render farm does not live on the lot.

#### Ash and corrosion protection for all technical rooms

Positive pressure; ISO 16890 ePM1 80% (F9) finals behind ePM10 pre-filters; gas-phase carbon for SO2/HF; differential-pressure alarms; ISA-S71.04 G1 severity with copper/silver coupons read quarterly; a written ash-event damper-closure procedure.

**Why.** Free cooling means outside air; ash is abrasive and, wet, conductive at board level.

**Here.** 2010 Eyjafjallajökull ash: about 35% of particles under 30 µm, up to 25% respirable under 10 µm.

#### Seismic restraint of IT plant

Racks bolted to structure on seismic bases and top-braced; cable tray braced longitudinally and transversely; battery strings restrained; equipment qualified to Telcordia GR-63-CORE Zone 4 / IEC 60068 where a variant exists.

**Why.** An unbraced 42U rack walks in a M5 event and shears every fibre in its patch field.

**Here.** The lot sits between the Reykjanes and South Iceland seismic zones; capital-area ag roughly 0.10–0.15 g (est. — confirm against the ÍST EN 1998-1 national annex map).

#### International cable diversity — buy all three systems

IRIS: Þorlákshöfn–Galway, in service 1 March 2023, 6 fibre pairs, 22 Tbps/pair, 132 Tbps design at 500G line rate. DANICE: 2,250 km to Denmark, 4 pairs, up to ~40 Tbps. FARICE-1: 1,205 km via the Faroes to Dunnet Bay, Scotland, ~10 Tbps lit.

**Why.** Being on one Iceland–Europe system is the same as being on no system when it faults.

**Here.** Farice reports no wet-plant fault across FARICE-1 and DANICE from 2004 to 2023, with a 1 km cable protection zone worked with the fisheries.

#### Purchased transit capacity

2× 10 Gbit/s IP transit from two carriers on different subsea systems day one, burstable to 100 Gbit/s, plus a 10 Gbit/s protected wavelength to Dublin and 10 Gbit/s to London. Est. steady draw 1.5–4 Gbit/s, dailies-push peaks above 8 Gbit/s.

**Why.** Transit sized to the average is transit that fails at 21:00 when four units push the day.

#### Domestic backhaul on two independent owners

Two leased dark-fibre paths from the lot to Reykjavík on different owners' plant — Míla's national trunk and Ljósleiðarinn (Orkuveita Reykjavíkur) metro — each proved diverse at KMZ level, not just on paper.

**Why.** Two circuits from one wholesaler in the same duct is one circuit with two invoices.

**Here.** Míla's trunk descends from the 1989–91 ring-road fibre built for the Iceland Air Defence System; Ljósleiðarinn leases dark fibre as a municipal utility.

#### Latency budget to the places that matter (est.)

Est. RTT from lot: Reykjavík <0.5 ms; Dublin ~20 ms; London ~26 ms; Copenhagen ~33 ms; Frankfurt ~40 ms; New York ~75–85 ms; Atlanta ~110–125 ms; Los Angeles ~140–160 ms. Derived at ~1 ms per 100 km fibre RTT plus equipment — measure and republish at commissioning.

**Why.** Every remote-workflow promise you make to a production is really a promise about these numbers.

#### Camera-to-cloud provisioning

Frame.io C2C via Teradek-class encoders: H.264/H.265 proxies up to 15 Mbit/s per camera; a ProRes proxy path wants ≥80 Mbit/s upload per unit. Provision 1 Gbit/s committed upstream per shooting unit and QoS it above everything except life safety.

**Why.** C2C is the first thing a modern production tests and the first thing that exposes an asymmetric link.

#### Original camera negative volumes (the real load)

ALEXA 35 ARRIRAW 4.6K open gate 24p ≈ 33.4 GB/min ≈ 2.01 TB/hour; RED V-RAPTOR 8K 180–360 MB/s; Sony VENICE 2 sustains to 825 MB/s. Four cameras × ~3 h of rolls ≈ 24 TB/day, ≈1.4 PB across a 60-day schedule before proxies or second copies (est.).

**Why.** Every storage, transfer and archive decision on the lot is downstream of this one number.

#### Near-set ingest bays (tier 0)

Two ingest bays, each 200 TB NVMe buffer, 2× 25 Gbit/s to core, checksum-verified offload (Silverstack/ShotPut with MHL manifests) at ≥1.5 GB/s; two independent copies written before any card is cleared, logged and countersigned.

**Why.** The only moment original negative exists in one place is the moment the lot can lose a shoot day.

#### Tier 1 shared production storage

All-NVMe scale-out, 1 PB usable day one to 5 PB, ≥40 GB/s aggregate read, ≥100k IOPS, SMB3 + NFSv4.1 + S3, per-production tenancy with hard quotas, snapshots every 4 h retained 14 days, immutable snapshots for 7 days.

**Why.** Editorial, VFX pulls and volume content all hit the same shelf; a slow shelf shows up as idle crew.

#### Tier 3 archive — tape
