Two libraries in two different buildings: LTO-9 at 18 TB native per cartridge now, LTO-10 at 30 TB native as drives land; 500+ slots and 8 drives each; LTFS; two on-lot copies plus one cloud copy; checksum restore test on 2% of cartridges monthly.

**Why.** Tape is the only copy that survives a ransomware event and a cloud invoice at the same time.

#### Cloud on-ramps

Iceland has no in-country AWS, Azure or Google on-ramp; nearest Direct Connect and ExpressRoute locations are Dublin, London, Stockholm and Frankfurt. Buy 2× 10 Gbit/s at two of them, on different subsea cables, with BGP failover proved quarterly.

**Why.** Cloud-native finishing behaves differently here than at Trilith or Shinfield, and the difference is 20+ ms and a subsea cable.

#### Campus Wi-Fi system

Wi-Fi 7 (802.11be), ~250 APs across stages, offices, workshops and backlot (est.); redundant controllers in the MER; 802.1X EAP-TLS plus per-production PPSK; design floor -65 dBm at 5 GHz; 20/40 MHz channels in dense zones; 2.5G/5G PoE++ uplinks per AP.

**Why.** Wi-Fi is what the AD, the script supervisor, the video village and every C2C device actually touch.

#### 6 GHz regulatory constraint

In the EEA only 5945–6425 MHz is licence-exempt, low-power indoor and very-low-power portable, under Commission Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/1067 (ETSI EN 303 687). Upper 6 GHz is unresolved with CEPT's report due July 2027. 6 GHz is an indoor tool only.

**Why.** A backlot designed around high-power Wi-Fi 6E/7 is both unlawful here and non-functional.

**Here.** Iceland follows the EU harmonised framework through the EEA, so US-style 6 GHz standard-power outdoor operation is not available.

#### Public mobile coverage inside the buildings

Neutral-host DAS or small cells carrying all three Icelandic MNOs plus the emergency network into every stage, workshop and basement. Iceland reports 4G/5G at over 99% of homes, but a 30 m steel-clad stage is a Faraday cage.

**Why.** 112 coverage inside a stage is life safety, not convenience — and crew phones are the informal comms layer whether you like it or not.

**Here.** Emergency services run on the Tetra Ísland network (Neyðarlínan), with a move to mission-critical broadband planned — carry both.

#### RF spectrum coordination as a lot function

A named frequency coordinator holding Fjarskiptastofa licences for wireless mics, IEMs, camera links and comms; a live per-production register; scan before every block; a 470–694 MHz plan that survives four stages shooting simultaneously.

**Why.** Four productions arriving with their own unlicensed kit will step on each other by lunchtime on day one.

**Here.** Small national spectrum administration, so coordination is quick — but licences are per-user and imported kit must be checked against Icelandic allocations.

#### Timing and sync distribution

SMPTE ST 2059-2 PTP over the campus fabric: two grandmasters in different buildings, multi-constellation GNSS (GPS+Galileo+GLONASS), rubidium holdover ≥24 h, boundary clocks in every SER, NTP stratum-1 for IT, black-and-burst and word clock legacy feeds retained.

**Why.** Everything downstream of a wrong clock — 2110, dailies timecode, LED volume genlock — fails in ways that look like other faults.

**Here.** At 64°N, auroral ionospheric scintillation degrades GNSS at times; holdover and a second timing source are not optional here.

#### Per-production security segmentation

Isolation end to end: own VRF, own VLAN/VNI set, own firewall context, own SSID and PPSK, own storage tenancy, own directory OU. East-west default deny. No shared subnet other than metered internet egress and print-as-a-service.

**Why.** One production seeing another's material is the single failure a major studio will not forgive or forget.

#### Zero-trust architecture and industry standards

Build to the MovieLabs 2030 zero-trust security architecture and MPA Content Security Best Practices v5.3.1 (v5.3 published December 2024). Target TPN Gold Shield — third-party assessed — before the first major booking, not after.

**Why.** TPN status now appears as a contractual requirement in studio and streamer vendor agreements; retrofitting it onto a live lot is brutal.

#### Identity, onboarding and offboarding

SSO with phishing-resistant MFA (FIDO2), SCIM provisioning driven off the crew start paperwork and badge system, automatic deprovisioning at wrap plus 30-day grace, just-in-time elevation, weekly per-production access review.

**Why.** Access that outlives the contract is how content leaves a lot.

**Here.** HODs are imported and rotate constantly; onboarding 60 people in a week and removing them in a day has to be routine.

#### Perimeter, DDoS and egress control

NGFW cluster with ≥40 Gbit/s inspected throughput, IDS/IPS, DNS filtering, upstream DDoS scrubbing from both transit providers with a ≤60 s mitigation SLA, and outbound DLP with per-production policy on the egress path.

**Why.** A visible new studio with a famous title on it is a target from the day the title is announced.

#### Operational technology segregation

Cameras, access control, BMS, geothermal and water-stage plant, EV chargers and gates on physically separate VLANs and a distinct firewall zone, no direct internet egress, separate patch fields and cable colours, quarterly firmware baseline.

**Why.** The VMS and door controllers are the softest devices on any lot and they usually share copper with everything else.

#### Monitoring, NOC and support model

24/7 NMS with flow telemetry and synthetic probes to Dublin, London and Los Angeles every 60 s; an on-lot network engineer from call to wrap; 15-minute response in shooting hours; 4-hour hardware swap from a spares locker holding 10% of every optic, switch and AP model.

**Why.** Nobody on the island can courier you a line card at 02:00; the spares locker is the service level.

**Here.** Vendor engineers are a three-hour flight away and sea freight is weather-dependent — stock depth substitutes for supply chain.

#### Structured cabling standard

Cat6A screened, two ports minimum per desk, 90 m channel limit enforced by SER placement, 802.3bt Type 4 PoE++ (90 W) budgeted on 60% of ports, TIA-606-C labelling, Fluke certification and OTDR traces for every link retained in DCIM.

**Why.** A lot lives for forty years; undocumented cabling makes every later change a survey.

#### Day-one production package (what they actually plug into)

Delivered in 5 working days: 10 Gbit/s DIA with 1 Gbit/s committed upstream, /28 IPv4 + /48 IPv6, own VRF/SSID/firewall context, 2×10G to each occupied stage, 48 office ports, 200 TB project share, MAM tenancy, 4 review-streaming seats, SIP trunk and 20 DECT handsets.

**Why.** Productions judge a new lot in its first 48 hours, and it is the network they judge it on.

### Expected

#### MER cooling

Indirect free cooling all year: Reykjavík-area July mean daily maximum ~13 °C. Dry coolers plus N+1 CRAHs, 24 °C supply within ASHRAE class A1 allowable, design PUE ≤1.2 — the figure atNorth publishes for its Icelandic sites.

**Why.** Mechanical cooling in this climate is capex and carbon you do not need to spend.

**Here.** Reject heat into the site heating loop rather than the sky; atNorth runs a heat-reuse partnership on the same principle.

#### Peering at RIX

Own RIPE AS number and PI space (IPv4 /23 or /22, IPv6 /44); 100G ports at two of the three RIX sites — RIX-KT (Katrínartún) and RIX-MH (Múlastöð, Ármúli 25); RPKI ROAs published, IRR objects maintained. RIX is ISNIC-operated, founded 1999.

**Why.** Local peering puts Icelandic crew services, banking and the MNOs one hop away and off your paid transit.

#### Remote workstation and remote-seat sizing

HP Anyware/PCoIP-class remoting stays comfortable to ~50 ms RTT and workable to ~100 ms (est. industry practice). Budget 25–50 Mbit/s per 4K remote seat, 100 seats concurrent. London and Dublin editorial works; live LA colour does not.

**Why.** Sell a remote suite that a Los Angeles supervisor cannot actually drive and you have sold a complaint.

**Here.** Imported HODs will want to work from home countries between blocks; this is the number that decides whether they can.

#### Private media network membership

Two 10 Gbit/s Sohonet Media Network tails on diverse routes. SMN connects 500+ M&E companies; Media Fabric, launched 7 April 2026, bundles connectivity, multi-cloud access, LAN/Wi-Fi and security under one managed model. Sohonet already sits on-lot at Trilith.

**Why.** Post houses, studios and streamers expect to reach you inside a network they already trust, not over the public internet.

#### Review and approval streaming

ClearView Flex: sub-100 ms, up to 2K DCI 10-bit 4:2:2 HDR in Rec.2020/P3-D65, 3/10/20 Mbit/s quality tiers, up to 40 viewers. ClearView Pivot for sign-off: JPEG 2000, 2K DCI 60p or 4K DCI 30p, up to 12-bit 4:4:4, 16 channels uncompressed audio, 150 Mbit/s–1 Gbit/s.

**Why.** A studio executive in Burbank approving a look is the whole reason the lot is reachable at all.

#### Tier 2 nearline

Hybrid disk tier, 4 PB usable, erasure coded 8+2, ≥8 GB/s, policy demotion at 21 days untouched, transparent recall. Holds completed dailies, VFX plates and LED volume content packages between blocks.

**Why.** Keeping everything on NVMe is a way to spend a sugar fortune on cold data.

#### Cloud cold copy and egress economics

Third copy to archive-class object storage in eu-west-1 or eu-north-1. Restoring 100 TB at typical €0.02–0.09/GB egress is €2,000–9,000 per pull (est.), which is why on-lot LTO stays the primary restore path and the cloud copy is insurance only.

**Why.** Studios ask for a cloud copy; nobody budgets the retrieval until they need it.

#### Off-lot replication and DR

Async replication of the active project tier to a colocation cage off the lot: atNorth ICE02, Keflavík — 9 ha campus, 80 MW, Tier III, published PUE ≤1.2, ~48 km away — RPO 15 min over a dedicated 10 Gbit/s wave, annual failover rehearsal.

**Why.** A campus fire, an eruption exclusion zone or a long power event must not stop a show that is mid-block.

**Here.** Keflavík is on a different seismic and volcanic exposure to Mosfellsbær and sits beside the airport for physical retrieval.

#### Accelerated managed file transfer

Signiant or Aspera-class UDP transfer licensed to 10 Gbit/s aggregate for anything over 100 GB. TCP alone will not fill a 10 Gbit/s pipe at 130 ms RTT without heavy tuning; accelerated transfer will. Checksum manifests and per-production audit logs mandatory.

**Why.** Deliveries to Los Angeles across a 140 ms path are the case that breaks naive transfer tooling.

#### Physical media shuttle as a designed path

Two hardware-encrypted (AES-256) courier shuttle cases with chain-of-custody logging. Keflavík–London is about a 3 h flight, so a 24 TB shoot day can be in Soho next morning — faster than any circuit the lot will ever buy for that volume.

**Why.** Sneakernet is a tier of the storage architecture, not an admission of failure.

**Here.** Freight for hardware is sea and weather-dependent, but data can fly daily from Keflavík — different logistics, different plan.

#### Backlot outdoor wireless

IP67 outdoor APs on 6 m poles covering the ~20 ha working backlot at 5 GHz, fed by ducted fibre to six powered node cabinets; 60 GHz point-to-point only under 300 m; licensed 18/23 GHz for longer hops; all mounts and radomes designed to a 55 m/s gust (est.).

**Why.** 809 ha cannot be blanketed; the working backlot can, and everything else falls to cellular.

**Here.** Among the windiest inhabited places on earth — pole and mount design, not radio design, is what fails first.

#### Return video and lot-wide IPTV

Every stage feed available to 40+ endpoints in production offices, screening rooms and executive suites; glass-to-glass under 150 ms (est.); forensic watermark burned per viewer session; HDR in the two review rooms; hard segmentation between productions.

**Why.** Producers walking to video village across a wet backlot in December is a scheduling loss you can design out.

**Here.** December daylight is minimal and the walk between stages is exposed — remote viewing is a working-conditions feature, not a luxury.

#### DCIM, IPAM and documentation discipline

One source of truth: DCIM for racks, power and ports; IPAM for every subnet, VLAN and DNS record; as-builts updated within 5 working days of any change; per-production network handover pack issued on day one and reclaimed at wrap.

**Why.** Four concurrent productions and no source of truth is how you lose a whole VLAN and find it during a shoot.

#### Media asset management and metadata spine

Campus MAM with per-production tenancy: auto-ingest of camera, sound and script metadata, proxy generation at 1080p H.264 8 Mbit/s, timecode-accurate search, API for editorial and VFX pulls, retention aligned to Iceland's 7-year accounting record rule (verify with counsel).

**Why.** Without a metadata spine the shared storage is just a very fast pile.

**Here.** The 25%/35% rebate is audited; asset and cost records need to survive long after wrap.

#### Truck park and fly-pack tie-ins

Six technical vehicle bays, each with 12 single-mode fibres and 63 A three-phase, patchable to any stage and to the MER; satellite uplink pad with clear southern sky; ability to hand a visiting broadcaster a 10 Gbit/s circuit within 24 hours.

**Why.** Visiting OB, EPK, press junkets and live events all arrive expecting a patch, not a project.

**Here.** Geostationary look angles at 64°N are only about 10–15° elevation (est.) — site the pad clear of the stage block or the dish sees nothing.

#### Tertiary and location connectivity

2× Starlink Performance terminals (service available in Iceland since February 2023; typical 25–60 ms, 65–400+ Mbit/s) as automatic failover for office internet, plus four bonded-cellular kits (4 SIM) for distant units across the 809 ha.

**Why.** When both fibre paths are cut you still need the call sheet out and payroll in.

**Here.** Starlink works at this latitude but wants a wider sky view than in central Europe — site terminals away from stage walls and masts.

### State of the art

#### Fourth path westward

Greenland Connect (Landeyjarsandur–Qaqortoq–Nuuk–Milton, Newfoundland, operated by Tusass) is the only cable off Iceland that does not go to Europe. Hold a small protected capacity on it for control-plane and voice continuity.

**Why.** Every European path shares the same weather, the same repair ships and the same seismic corridor.

#### Burst render and heavy compute placed off-lot

Keep ~64 GPU nodes on-lot for interactive and volume work; place 200–2,000 vCPU and 40–200 GPU render bursts at an Icelandic colo (atNorth ICE02/ICE03) or AWS eu-west-1, reached over a dedicated 10 Gbit/s NNI with a per-production billing tag.

**Why.** A render farm is a megawatt problem; on this island that is a permitting problem, not a procurement one.

**Here.** Landsvirkjun has stated it cannot supply new industrial load on system capacity grounds, and ESA is investigating the refusals — do not design a lot that needs a new large power contract.

#### On-lot cloud edge

AWS Outposts has been available in Iceland since 29 August 2023, in 42U rack and 1U/2U server forms, with the control plane in the nearest region. Two racks give in-country S3 and EC2 at LAN latency for cloud-native tooling and for data-residency clauses.

**Why.** It lets a production run its cloud pipeline without every object round-tripping to Dublin.

**Here.** Also answers EEA/GDPR data-residency questions from European broadcasters without shipping media off the island.

#### Private LTE/5G

Band n78 private network, three sectors from one 30 m mast, ~3–5 km usable radius over the estate (est.), 5G SA core on-lot, eSIM provisioning per production. Iceland's 3.6 GHz licences went to Síminn, Sýn and Nova in 2020 and no local/private licensing regime is published.

**Why.** It is the only way to give a unit base 3 km out the same network the stages have.

**Here.** With no German- or UK-style local licence, the practical route is an MNO-hosted private network or slice — a commercial negotiation, not a form.

#### IP video transport between buildings

SMPTE ST 2110-20/-30/-40 tie-lines building to building on the 100G fabric, with JPEG XS (ST 2110-22) at 4:1–10:1 and sub-frame latency for anything crossing the campus. 1080p50 uncompressed ≈3 Gbit/s, UHD ≈12 Gbit/s — hence XS, not raw, on the ring.

**Why.** Once a signal leaves a stage it is a network problem, and uncompressed UHD is a bandwidth bill nobody needs to pay.

### What gets got wrong

- No cloud on-ramp exists in Iceland. Every AWS, Azure or Google path is a 20+ ms subsea hop to Dublin or London, so cloud-native colour and finishing will not feel the way it feels at Trilith or Shinfield. AWS Outposts (available in Iceland since 29 Aug 2023) is the only way to get a real cloud control plane on the lot.
- Landsvirkjun has said it cannot supply new industrial load and ESA is investigating its refusals. Do not design a lot whose network plan implies a megawatt-scale render farm needing a new power contract — keep the MER under about 1.5 MW as a distribution connection and put burst compute at atNorth or in-region cloud.
- In the EEA only 5945–6425 MHz of 6 GHz is licence-exempt and it is indoor low-power only (Decision (EU) 2021/1067); upper 6 GHz is undecided until CEPT reports in July 2027. Any backlot design that assumes US-style outdoor Wi-Fi 6E/7 standard power is unlawful and will not work.
- Iceland has no published local/private 5G spectrum regime like Germany's 3.7–3.8 GHz or Ofcom's shared access. The 3.6 GHz band went to Síminn, Sýn and Nova in 2020. Private 5G here is an MNO commercial negotiation, and that changes the schedule and the control you get.
- Farice reports no submarine fault on FARICE-1 or DANICE from 2004 to 2023 — the wet plant is not the risk. The risk is that all three European cables' terrestrial backhaul converges toward the capital region, some of it across the South Iceland Seismic Zone. Buy diversity you have proved on a map, not diversity you inferred from a cable count.
- Upstream, not downstream, is the number that breaks. Four ARRIRAW cameras is roughly 24 TB a shoot day (33.4 GB/min, 2.01 TB/hour per camera). You will never push originals off the island in real time; you push 15 Mbit/s proxies and you ship the negative on encrypted drives via Keflavík.
- Estimated 140–160 ms RTT to Los Angeles makes live remote grading with an LA colourist impractical. Sell London and Dublin remote workflows honestly and plan for the LA supervisor to fly in or work rested sessions.
- Free cooling means outside air, and Iceland has ash events. Without ePM1 80% filtration, gas-phase scrubbing, positive pressure and a written damper-closure procedure, one eruption puts abrasive, conductive dust through the machine room.
- TPN Gold and MPA Best Practices v5.3.1 are now contract language, not marketing. Per-production VRFs, tenancy and identity have to be in the design from day one; retrofitting segmentation onto a running lot with four productions on it is close to impossible.
- The 25%/35% rebate is legislated to expire at the end of 2028. That is a short depreciation window for network capex — lease optics, switches and storage where you can, and build a refresh assumption into the operating model rather than a 10-year write-down.
- A national crew base of about a thousand means your network team is three to five people and every vendor engineer is a flight away. Depth of on-site spares and ruthless documentation are the substitutes for the London engineer who can be there in an hour.
- GNSS at 64°N is exposed to auroral scintillation. A single grandmaster with a single antenna will occasionally lose lock — two grandmasters, multi-constellation receivers and rubidium holdover, or you will chase phantom 2110 and timecode faults.
- Get RIPE PI address space and an AS number early. IPv4 is scarce and expensive, and four concurrent productions each wanting public statics will consume more than a small allocation.
- December has almost no daylight and June almost no darkness. Remote video village, IPTV to offices and reliable backlot wireless are not comforts here — they are what keeps crew out of a dark, wind-driven backlot between setups.

---

## 6. Running a studio lot as a business

*inventory and booking, rate architecture, contracts, master leases, occupancy, sales, invoicing, credit and the operator's metrics (Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær, Iceland)*

### Essential

#### Single booking calendar as system of record

One facility-scheduling platform (Xytech MediaPulse / ScheduALL class; Xytech acquired ScheduALL Feb 2021) holding every rate-bearing unit; no parallel spreadsheets. Target: booking written to system within 2 hours of verbal hold, 100% of holds carrying an expiry timestamp. (Estimate on targets.)

**Why.** Two salespeople selling the same stage from two spreadsheets is how a lot loses a streamer and a reputation in one week.

**Here.** Time zone GMT year-round: LA calls land 16:00-19:00 Icelandic, London 09:00-17:00. The calendar must be readable by a client at 08:00 in Burbank.

#### Sellable-unit inventory model

Every unit priced and calendared: 4 stages (6,000 / 3,200 / 2,000 / 2,000 m2), tank water-time (2,400 m2), workshops (m2/week), production offices (m2/week or per desk), set storage (m2/month), unit base and parking bays, backlot zones (ha/day), screening room (per session), power (per kWh), heat (per MWh).

**Why.** Revenue that is not an inventory line is revenue nobody sells, meters or bills; lots leak 10-20% of ancillary this way.

**Here.** Interconnect between the two 2,000 m2 stages is itself a sellable unit: sold jointly as a 4,000 m2 clear span at a premium, or split with the acoustic separation guaranteed.

#### Hold ladder with first refusal

First hold = first right of refusal, second and third holds ranked behind it. Published precedent (Riverfront Stages): holds are non-binding, all holds auto-release one week before the held date, and the studio does not warn you.

**Why.** Without ranking and expiry, a stage shows as busy for months while earning nothing, and the sales team cannot see real availability.

#### Challenge rule

Any hold is challengeable by a later enquiry: holder is emailed and must contract or release within 24 hours (Riverfront Stages published policy). Suggest 48 hours for a hold longer than 8 weeks out, 24 hours inside 8 weeks. (48-hour variant is an estimate.)

**Why.** It converts polite optionality into a decision, and it is the only fair way to run a scarce four-stage lot.

#### Booking states and gate criteria

Enquiry - qualified - soft hold - hard hold/option - contract issued - signed - deposit cleared - occupancy - shoot - strike - reinstatement sign-off - final invoice - closed. Each state has an owner, an expiry and an exit test; nothing moves forward on optimism.

**Why.** Pipeline maths, occupancy forecasts and cash forecasts are all built on state; fuzzy states produce fiction in every downstream report.

#### Published rate card, quoted in weeks

Film stages are quoted weekly for the whole production, not daily. UK benchmark (LSH 2018): a prestige 20,000 sq ft near-London stage listed at £25,000-30,000/week, i.e. £1.25-1.50/sq ft/week; warehouse conversions ~£1/sq ft/week inclusive of rates, ~£25-30/sq ft/yr.

**Why.** Producers compare weekly rates across territories in a single spreadsheet; a lot that only quotes bespoke cannot be shortlisted.

**Here.** Sugar Land list estimate, 2026 money: 6,000 m2 at EUR 14-16/m2/week (EUR 84-96k); 2,000 m2 at EUR 20-23/m2/week; water stage at EUR 24-27/m2/week plus tank charges.

#### Shoot / prep / strike / hold rate structure

Four rates per stage. Published practice: build, strike and pre-light at 75% of day rate (Stage A Studios, UK) or 50% (US standing-set practice); LSH notes a lower rent during build and strike, higher during filming. Recommend prep/strike 60-70%, hold 25-30%, dark hiatus 20%. (Recommendation is an estimate.)

**Why.** Prep and strike are 40-60% of a feature's stage weeks; pricing them wrong is the single biggest determinant of a lot's yield.

#### Utility recharge and per-stage submetering

Power and heat submetered per stage and recharged; published precedent for per-kWh recharge: £0.55/kWh (Stage A Studios). Recommend cost-plus 10-15% on electricity, cost-plus on district heat, water and tank heating billed per m3 and per MWh. (Markup is an estimate.)

**Why.** Unmetered power on an LED-volume-era stage is an open cheque; it also removes the client's incentive to switch anything off.

**Here.** Cheap, low-carbon kWh is a sales asset - publish the gCO2e/kWh and let productions book it into their albert/carbon reporting.

#### Deposit and payment schedule

Signature deposit 25-30% of contract value; balance monthly in advance on the 1st; security deposit equal to 4 weeks of the largest booked stage; ancillary billed monthly in arrears. Published small-studio precedent: 50% deposit on booking (Caster Studios). (Percentages are estimates.)

**Why.** Stage licences are unsecured trade credit against single-purpose vehicles; money in advance is the only real protection.

#### Cancellation and liquidated-damages ladder

Suggested: >180 days 10% of contracted value; 90-180 days 25%; 30-90 days 50%; <30 days 100%, each reduced by any re-let. Published small-studio precedent: cancellation 10 days to 48 hours out refunds 50% of deposit, inside 48 hours nothing. (Ladder is an estimate.)

**Why.** Productions collapse routinely; without a ladder the lot carries the whole cost of a green-lit show going away.

#### Licence, not lease

Contract as a licence to occupy for a defined period with the operator retaining control, house rules incorporated by reference, no security of tenure, no assignment or sub-licence without consent. Four-walling is exactly this: the client hires the space and contracts the rest separately.

**Why.** A lease gives statutory and renewal rights over the lot's most valuable asset; a licence keeps the calendar the operator's to sell.

**Here.** Icelandic commercial letting is largely freedom of contract, but have Icelandic counsel confirm the licence is not recharacterised as a tenancy under húsaleigulög; rents locally are usually CPI-indexed.

#### Four-wall inclusion schedule

A one-page schedule stating what the stage fee includes: shell, floor, grid, house power to the panel, base HVAC/heat, house lighting, cleaning of common areas, security perimeter, wifi tier 1, X parking bays, Y m2 of office. Everything else is a priced line.

**Why.** Four-walling means the client hires the empty box and contracts everything else; ambiguity there is the source of every wrap-time credit note.

#### Covenant, guarantees and bonds

Every booking is to a single-purpose production company. Require: parent guarantee from the financing entity, or a letter of credit / escrow of 100% of stage fees for unrated counterparties; completion-bond details (Film Finances and peers) where one exists; named commissioner or distributor on file.

**Why.** When a production folds, the SPV has no assets; the lot is left with a stripped stage and an unsecured claim.

#### Occupancy: define it before you report it

Report three numbers separately: contracted stage-days / available stage-days; physically occupied stage-days; and revenue-weighted occupancy (hold days at 25-30% weight). Publish the definition. Real benchmarks: FilmLA 62% (1H 2025), 63% (2024), mid-90s 2016-2022; HPP TTM stage 69.1% (Q4 2025).

**Why.** Occupancy is the number the board, the lender and the buyer look at, and it is trivially inflatable by counting holds and prep as full days.

**Here.** With four stages, one 6,000 m2 booking moves total occupancy by roughly 45 percentage points - report by stage and by m2, never as a bare average.

#### Break-even occupancy, stated and monitored

Compute and publish internally the occupancy at which the lot covers cash operating cost and then debt service; a new-build four-stage lot should model break-even at 50-60% revenue-weighted occupancy. (Estimate - depends on the capital structure the sugar fortune imposes.)

**Why.** Every pricing decision is really the question 'does this deal move us above or below break-even', and nobody can answer it without the number.

#### Client tour playbook

A 2.5-4 hour scripted visit: stage walk with tape measure and load chart in hand, workshops, offices, tank, backlot, crew route, canteen, then a rate conversation in a room with the calendar on the wall. Attendees: line producer, UPM, production designer, gaffer, transport captain, sometimes the director.

**Why.** Stage decisions are made by the line producer and the designer standing in the room; a tour that does not answer their questions loses the show that day.

**Here.** Iceland's tour is a two-day trip, not a lunchtime drop-in. Own the whole visit: airport pickup, hotel, weather contingency, a location day. Budget ISK 400,000-900,000 per client visit (estimate).

#### Sales team structure and comp

Estimate for this lot: commercial director; two sales directors (Los Angeles-facing, London/Europe-facing); one bookings manager owning the calendar; one marketing manager; client-services manager. Commission 0.5-1.5% of net contracted revenue with clawback on cancellation. (Estimate.)

**Why.** Stage business is relationship business conducted in two cities; a lot represented only from Iceland is a lot nobody calls first.

**Here.** Physical presence matters more than for a London lot: the sales case is 'come to a country you have to fly a crew to', so the seller has to be where the decision is made.

#### National partnership with the film commission

Film in Iceland (Business Iceland) and the Icelandic Film Centre are the front door for inbound enquiries and the administrators of the rebate. Co-fund familiarisation trips, share leads, appear on their stand and their location database, brief them on stage availability monthly.

**Why.** Most inbound Iceland enquiries land at the commission first; a lot that is not in that conversation hears about shows after the budget is locked.

**Here.** IFC data: 114 incentivised projects in 2022, of which only 14 were foreign - the commission's foreign pipeline is small enough to know every project by name.

#### Invoicing mechanics

Monthly in advance for space, monthly in arrears for consumption; invoice on the 25th for the following month; PO/cost-code and production-accountant contact captured at contract; itemised backup (meter reads, gate logs, waste tickets) attached to every recharge line.

**Why.** Production accountants reject any invoice they cannot reconcile to a cost code, and a rejected invoice ages 30 days before anyone notices.

#### VAT position and registration

Iceland: standard VAT 24%; letting of real property is outside VAT unless the operator takes voluntary registration for non-residential letting, which is what makes input VAT on the build and on operating costs recoverable. Accommodation is 11%. Foreign clients recover Icelandic VAT via reimbursement to foreign enterprises.

**Why.** Getting the voluntary registration wrong on a nine-figure build strands 24% of construction cost as unrecoverable tax.

**Here.** Crew housing let to a production is likely accommodation at 11%, not stage letting at 24% - the two lines must be split at contract, not at year-end.

#### Credit control and DSO

Credit check every counterparty; terms net 30 (streamer/studio parents) or payment in advance (indies, commercials); target DSO under 30 days, bad debt under 0.5% of revenue; weekly aged-debt review with named owner; contractual right to suspend access and to retain sets and materials for unpaid sums. (Targets are estimates.)

**Why.** A stage business bills large sums to entities designed to disappear; the only leverage is the gate and the set sitting inside it.

**Here.** Confirm with Icelandic counsel that a contractual retention/lien over a client's set and equipment is enforceable before relying on it.

#### The rebate as a sales instrument

25% of eligible Icelandic spend; 35% where spend is at least ISK 350m, at least 30 working days in Iceland including at least 10 shooting days, and at least 50 crew for at least 50 working days taxed in Iceland. No cap. Apply to the Icelandic Film Centre before production starts. (Act 43/1999.)

**Why.** Stage rent paid in Iceland is eligible spend, so the rebate is effectively a 25-35% discount on the lot's own invoice - the single strongest thing the sales team owns.

**Here.** Sell the thresholds, not just the headline: a show that shoots 8 days and posts abroad gets 25%; the same show restructured to 30 Icelandic working days gets 35%.

#### The 2028 cliff, managed in the contract book

The incentive legislation runs to 31 Dec 2028 (Film in Iceland). Track the share of the forward book that depends on it, model a 25%-only and a no-rebate case, and put a rebate-change clause in every long contract saying who carries the risk.

**Why.** A lot whose 2029 pipeline assumes 35% is one parliamentary vote away from repricing its whole business.

**Here.** Film in Iceland's own legislation page still shows an older consolidated text expiring 31 Dec 2025 - get the operative expiry confirmed in writing by the ministry before quoting it to a client.

#### FX policy

Quote in EUR or USD to clients, incur costs in ISK; policy: forward-cover 70-80% of contracted ISK cost exposure for bookings beyond 90 days, review monthly, state the contractual rate and the rate-change threshold (e.g. re-price if spot moves more than 5%). (Estimates.)

**Why.** An unhedged ISK strengthening turns a priced-to-win booking into a loss, and ISK is a small, volatile currency.

**Here.** Iceland's rebate pays in ISK against ISK-denominated spend, so a client's rebate and the lot's invoice move together - explain that; it is a selling point.

#### Client insurance schedule

Certificate before access, naming the operator as additional insured with waiver of subrogation: commercial general liability at least $1m per occurrence / $2m aggregate (higher for water, aerial or SFX work), employer's liability/workers comp to statutory limits, hired/non-owned auto, and all-risk cover on the client's property.

**Why.** Studio COI practice is standard and non-negotiable at the majors; accepting a thin certificate makes the lot the insurer of last resort.

**Here.** Icelandic statutory employer cover and EEA policy validity must be confirmed for foreign productions; a US package does not automatically respond in Iceland.

#### The lot's own insurance, including compulsory catastrophe cover

Property, business interruption (indemnity period 24 months minimum), public liability, employers' liability, D&O, cyber - plus Iceland's compulsory Natural Catastrophe Insurance (NTÍ): premium raised from 0.025% to 0.0375% of insured value from 1 Jan 2025; deductible 2% of each loss, minimum ISK 400,000 on buildings.

**Why.** Business interruption after a seismic or ash event is the loss that actually kills a single-site lot; NTÍ is compulsory but its cover and deductibles are narrow.

**Here.** NTÍ covers earthquake, volcanic eruption, avalanche, landslide, rockfall and flood - but ash contamination, access loss and client cancellation are the exposures to test against the wording.

#### Operator KPI pack

Weekly: stage-days sold, revenue-weighted occupancy, RevPAS, holds outstanding and hold-to-contract rate, pipeline coverage, cash collected, aged debt. Monthly: revenue by line, ancillary attach, NOI and NOI margin, DSO, forward-booked months, client concentration, repeat rate, cost per enquiry.

**Why.** These are the numbers that let an operator see a bad year eight months early, which is the only time it can still be fixed.

#### Power procurement and resale

Contract firm supply and grid capacity in writing before selling stage-time. Landsvirkjun has stated it cannot meet demand from new large users and has refused supply to e-fuel/hydrogen projects; ESA opened a formal investigation into that refusal in 2025. Constraint reported as most acute 2025-26.

**Why.** A lot that sells 30 m stages and an LED volume it cannot power has sold something it does not have; capacity is the binding commercial constraint in Iceland.

**Here.** Contract through a retailer plus a distribution agreement with Veitur/Landsnet, size on-site generation and storage for the shortfall, and treat spare capacity as a sellable asset to clients.

#### Demand sizing and the honest market case

Iceland's incentivised production spend: ISK 11.1bn (2019), 9.7bn (2020), 13.3bn (2021), 28.9bn / US$222m (2022, inflated by one HBO series). Foreign projects: 8 in 2019 rising to 14 in 2022; 82% of all incentivised projects are domestic. Additionality of the incentive: 86%.

**Why.** A four-stage lot needs perhaps 6-10 substantial bookings a year in a country that attracts about a dozen foreign projects; the business plan is import, not capture.

**Here.** Sugar Land's 13,200 m2 roughly doubles Iceland's stage stock at a stroke - the lot must create demand, and its sales spend should be sized accordingly.

#### Competitive set and pricing position

RVK Studios: 3 stages, over 6,500 m2, offices, six edit suites, 4K screening room. Truenorth at Fossar: 2 stages, 6,000 m2. RVK and Truenorth are jointly building a Gufunes site of about 8,800 m2 with up to seven stages (construction from 2024).

**Why.** Sugar Land's competitors are also the service companies that place foreign shows; pricing and channel terms have to be set knowing that.

**Here.** Sugar Land's defensible ground is what nobody else has: a 30 m tall 6,000 m2 room, a 2,400 m2 tank, and stage pairs that interconnect.

### Expected

#### Paid holds beyond a free window

Free hold 14 days; thereafter a hold fee of 15-20% of the shooting rate for the held weeks, credited in full against the contract if it converts, forfeit if not. Dark/hiatus holds for a returning series: 20-30% of shoot rate. (Estimates, benchmarked to hold-day practice.)

**Why.** A free option on a 6,000 m2 stage is worth tens of thousands a week; giving it away for months is a gift with no upside.

#### Discount authority matrix

List price, then a written ladder: sales director to -10%, commercial director to -20%, board above that. LSH 2018 is blunt that substantial discounts are nearly always negotiated off rate cards - so publish list rates you intend to discount, and track net effective rate per stage-week.

**Why.** Undocumented discretion means the last deal sets the market; every producer talks to every other producer.

#### Long-run taper and volume terms

Tiered by contracted stage-weeks: 0-12 weeks list; 13-26 weeks -8%; 27-52 weeks -15%; 52+ weeks or multi-stage -20-25%, with the taper conditional on payment terms and a cancellation floor. (Estimates; LSH: discounts negotiable particularly for long periods.)

**Why.** Long bookings are worth more than their headline rate because they kill void weeks and re-let costs; price that explicitly rather than ad hoc.

#### Overtime, weekend, holiday and 24-hour access rules

Define the contracted day (10h or 12h), overtime rate (precedent: £70/hour, Stage A Studios), weekend uplift (precedent +40%), public-holiday uplift and whether the licence is 24-hour. Film stages are conventionally 24-hour access; commercials and stills are not.

**Why.** Most disputes at wrap are about hours and access, not rent; if the clock is not defined, the client's clock wins.

**Here.** Iceland has ~12-13 public holidays; several fall in the spring shooting window. Publish the calendar with the rate card.

#### Minimum booking and seasonality pricing

Minimum 1 week on stages (2 weeks on the 6,000 m2), 1 day on backlot/tank for commercials. Seasonal pricing bands: peak May-Sep, shoulder Mar-Apr/Oct, low Nov-Feb, with a 10-20% spread. (Estimate; seasonality is Iceland-specific, see note.)

**Why.** Small bookings churn the calendar and block the long ones; unpriced seasonality means the lot is empty exactly when it costs most to run.

**Here.** Inverted logic vs a sunbelt lot: December's ~4-5 daylight hours push daylight exteriors onto stage, June's white nights push night exteriors onto stage. Sell winter stage-time against the darkness, not despite it.

#### Portfolio mix policy

Board-set target for contracted vs open-market capacity, e.g. no more than 50% of stage m2 on MLA and no single counterparty above 40% of revenue. Knight Frank's warning: MLA space is removed from the open market, so a lot that leases everything stops being a lot.

**Why.** Concentration is the studio business's classic failure: Hudson Pacific's Quixote stages ran 53.3% TTM occupancy in Q4 2025 while its Hollywood in-service stages ran 86.2%.

#### On-lot vendor concessions

Lighting/grip, catering, security, cleaning, waste, unit vehicles, fuel and IT let as on-lot concessions paying base rent plus 5-12% of gross on-lot turnover, with published rate cards and open access for the client's own preferred vendors at a facility fee. (Percentages are estimates.)

**Why.** Ancillary is a third of studio revenue and a large part of the margin: HPP's in-service studios earned $10.1m rental against $5.8m ancillary in Q4 2024.

**Here.** With a national crew base of just over 1,000 FTEs, some vendors will not exist. Expect to seed them - equity, guaranteed minimums or rent-free years - rather than tender them.

#### Stage-days and shoot-days as the volume metric

Track stage-days sold, and separately shoot-days actually filmed. FilmLA's participants logged 8,671 shoot days in 2023 falling to 7,940 in 2024 (-8%) across 1,287 projects; episodic TV fell from ~30% to 20% of stage-based filming.

**Why.** Shoot days are the demand signal; stage-days sold is the revenue signal. Divergence between them predicts your renewal rate a year out.

#### Revenue per available stage-day (RevPAS)

Net stage revenue / available stage-days, per stage and blended, tracked weekly against budget and against the same week last year - the studio analogue of hotel RevPAR. Decompose into achieved rate x occupancy so you can see which one moved. (Metric construction is an operator convention.)

**Why.** Occupancy alone rewards discounting; rate alone rewards empty stages. RevPAS is the only number that catches both.

#### Ancillary attach rate

Ancillary revenue / stage revenue, target 35-45%. Real benchmark: Hudson Pacific in-service studios, Q4 2024, $5.8m ancillary on $10.1m rental = 36%. Track attach by line: offices, workshops, storage, parking, power, catering, housing. (Target is an estimate.)

**Why.** A stage let with nothing attached is a warehouse rental; attach rate is where a lot's margin over an industrial shed actually lives.

#### Data room and stage pack

Per stage: dimensioned DWG and PDF plans, BIM/IFC model, LiDAR point cloud, grid load chart (kg/m2 and point loads), power one-line with available amperage, door sizes, floor loading, acoustic ratings, HVAC capacity, 360-degree tour, plus the licence template, rate card, insurance schedule and house rules.

**Why.** Producers shortlist on documents before they ever fly; missing a load chart or a door dimension removes the lot from a spreadsheet you never see.

#### Market and event calendar

FOCUS London (Business Design Centre, 7-8 Dec 2026, 200+ exhibitors, AFCI present with an Amazon MGM Studios brunch) is the core studio/commission market. Add EFM Berlin (Feb), Marché du Film Cannes (May), Produced By (June, LA), MIPCOM (Oct), AFM (Nov), Content London (Dec), Series Mania (Mar).

**Why.** These are where line producers, commissioners and film commissions actually meet studios; the annual pipeline is largely seeded at four or five of them.

**Here.** Add Nordic entry points: Nordic Film Market at Göteborg (Feb), Stockfish (Reykjavík), and Business Iceland/Film in Iceland delegations that pay part of the stand cost.

#### Pipeline and conversion measurement

Track enquiry - qualified - hold - contract with conversion at each gate and weighted pipeline value; run pipeline coverage of 3-5x the remaining-year revenue target. Plausible conversion for a new lot: 25% enquiry-to-hold, 35% hold-to-contract, ~9% end to end. (Estimates.)

**Why.** Stage sales cycles run 3-12 months; without coverage ratios you discover a hole in occupancy when it is too late to fill it.

#### Account coverage map

Named coverage of: streamer physical-production and real-estate teams (Netflix, Amazon MGM, Apple, Disney, WBD, Sky/NBCU), major studio production executives, UK/US line producers and UPMs, Icelandic service companies (Truenorth, RVK Studios, Pegasus), and the commercials/stills market.

**Why.** Stage bookings come from perhaps 200 individuals worldwide; coverage is a list, not a market.

**Here.** Icelandic service companies are competitors and channel at once - they book stages for their clients. Terms for them (agency commission of 3-5%) need to be explicit.

#### Quote turnaround SLA

Indicative rate within 4 working hours; a full written quote with schedule, inclusions and exclusions within 2 working days; contract issued within 3 working days of terms agreed. (Estimates - but publish them and measure against them.)

**Why.** Producers budget against the first credible number they get; a slow quote is a lost bid regardless of price.

#### Rebate cash-flow and the disbursement clock

Reimbursement is claimed after completion: the disbursement request is due within six months of completion (defined as premiere); costs must be attested by an auditor where the reimbursement exceeds ISK 3m; the project must complete within 3 years of the pledge, extendable to 5.

**Why.** Clients budget the rebate as cash, but it arrives long after they have paid the lot; knowing the clock lets the sales team answer the cash-flow question a producer actually has.

**Here.** Icelandic banks and specialist lenders will discount an approved pledge; a lot that can introduce that financing wins deals on cash flow rather than on rate. (Introduction practice is an estimate.)

#### Indexation and annual rate review

Annual rate-card review each September for the following calendar year, published to clients by 1 October; contracted long lets indexed to the Statistics Iceland CPI with a floor and cap; construction- and wage-index sensitivity reviewed at the same time.

**Why.** Iceland has lived with high and volatile inflation; a static rate card silently transfers all of it to the operator.

#### Property tax and fixed-cost base

Model municipal property tax (fasteignagjöld, assessed on official property value by Mosfellsbær), insurance, security, maintenance, utilities standing charges, and staff as an annual fixed nut per stage; publish stage-level fixed cost so pricing decisions see it.

**Why.** Knight Frank documented UK studios blindsided by the 2023 business-rates revaluation; property tax is the fixed cost operators consistently under-model.

**Here.** Confirm the assessed value and rate with Mosfellsbær before completion; a new-build 13,200 m2 stage estate is a large new entry on a small municipality's roll.

#### TPN Gold Shield certification

Trusted Partner Network (MPA-owned) assessment of physical, logical and operational content security. Four-tier Shield system and v5.3.1 questionnaire launched 9 Sept 2025; Gold Shield requires an on-site assessment, is valid 2 years and needs an interim Blue Shield at 12 months.

**Why.** Major studios and streamers increasingly write TPN status into vendor agreements; without it the lot cannot hold client media on site and loses the post and vault revenue.

#### Forward book and visibility metric

Report contracted revenue for the next 3, 6, 12 and 24 months as a percentage of budget, refreshed weekly. A healthy independent lot should enter a year with 45-60% of stage revenue contracted; an MLA-heavy lot with 80%+. (Estimates.)

**Why.** Visibility, not occupancy, is what a lender and a buyer pay for; it is also what tells the operator when to cut price rather than hold rate.

#### Client services and production liaison

A named liaison per production from contract to reinstatement, a 24/7 duty manager, a ticketed help desk with response targets (safety immediate, access 15 min, facilities 4 h), a weekly production meeting, and an exit survey scored 1-10 with the score reported to the board. (Targets are estimates.)

**Why.** Repeat bookings are won by whether the wrap went smoothly; the lot's reputation is made by the person the second AD phones at 2am.

**Here.** A crew imported for months judges the lot on canteen, transport and housing as much as on stage quality - service is a commercial line here, not a courtesy.

#### Reinstatement, damage and make-good regime

Photographic condition survey at handover and at exit, itemised make-good schedule, floor repaint charged as a line (published precedent: £75 floor repaint, Stage A Studios), waste and skip charges at cost-plus, and a defined period (10 working days) to agree deductions from the security deposit.

**Why.** Set strikes damage floors, walls and grids as a matter of routine; unrecovered make-good is a hidden discount on every booking.

#### Set storage and standing-set economics

Storage let per m2 per month on 3-month minimums with 60 days' notice to clear; standing sets on the backlot priced as a build fee plus a monthly ground rent plus a per-day use fee, with an agreed removal bond.

**Why.** Storage is the highest-margin, lowest-effort revenue on a lot and it locks the client in; unbonded standing sets become the operator's demolition bill.

#### Freight windows written into the booking

Sea freight is the only realistic route for set materials and heavy kit. Eimskip's express Blue Line runs Rotterdam-Reykjavík in roughly 6-7 days, weather permitting. Contracts should define a materials-on-site date, not just a prep start, and say who carries weather delay.

**Why.** A prep start the lot cannot supply materials for is a dispute in week one; freight is the invisible dependency in every Icelandic schedule.

**Here.** Build a bonded/consolidated freight service and a customs broker relationship into the lot's offer - it is a genuine differentiator against London lots.

#### Governance, entity and statutory reporting

Icelandic ehf/hf paying 20% corporate income tax (other legal forms 37.6%); employer social security 6.35%, employer pension 11.5%, VIRK 0.1%; stamp duty 0.8-1.6% on documents transferring real property; annual audited accounts filed to the register.

**Why.** An owner-operator with a US arm needs arm's-length pricing between the entities or the tax position, and the rebate claim, are both exposed.

**Here.** If the owner's own productions shoot on the owner's own stages, the rent is Icelandic spend and potentially rebate-eligible - but only at a defensible arm's-length rate with documentation.

#### Confidentiality discipline

NDA before a tour; project code names on the calendar and on invoices; no client logos, stills or titles published without written consent; controlled photography on the lot; staff contracts carrying confidentiality clauses; a single named spokesperson.

**Why.** Studios treat a leak as a vendor failure; one photograph on social media can cost a lot an entire slate.

### State of the art

#### Deal calculator behind every quote

Quote engine returns: gross contracted value, net effective rate per m2 per week, ancillary attached, contribution after direct cost (power, heat, cleaning, security, liaison), displaced-booking cost, and payback against the stage's break-even occupancy.

**Why.** Without contribution per deal, a lot fills stages at rates that lose money once power, heat and staffing are counted.

**Here.** Heat is cheap (geothermal district heat), but a 30 m tall stage in a gale is a real thermal load; put the actual MWh in the calculator, not a flat allowance.

#### Master Lease Agreement product

A 10-year-plus whole-facility let to a streamer or studio. Real deals: Netflix-Pinewood 2019, the first MLA, 10 years, 14 stages at Shepperton; Amazon 2022, over 10 years, 450,000 sq ft / 9 stages; Disney 10 years at Pinewood. Knight Frank: all new Shepperton space pre-let on MLAs.

**Why.** An MLA converts a weeks-long, void-prone hire business into secure indexed income underpinned by a strong covenant - the difference between an operating risk and a financeable asset.

**Here.** Iceland has never had one. Realistically a Sugar Land MLA is a 3-7 year, 2-3 stage commitment tied to a specific series order, not a 199-year-lease-scale deal.

#### What an MLA is actually worth

Price it as income x covenant, not as weeks sold: an MLA should clear 65-75% of the gross value the same stages would earn at 70% open-market occupancy, in exchange for term, indexation and no void. Benchmarks: HPP studio ABR $47.41/sq ft/yr; UK effective £25-30/sq ft/yr (LSH 2018).

**Why.** Operators routinely give away 40%+ for term and then find the covenant did not protect them; the discount has to be quantified against a modelled void.

#### MLA clause set to insist on

Full repairing obligations or a fixed dilapidations sum; CPI indexation with a 2% floor and 6% cap; break at year 5 with 12 months' notice and a break penalty; sub-licensing to third-party productions permitted with operator revenue share (20-30%); ancillary services not bundled. (Terms are estimates.)

**Why.** An unindexed, break-heavy MLA in a 5-8% inflation economy is a slow write-down of the whole asset.

**Here.** Icelandic leases are conventionally index-linked to the CPI published by Statistics Iceland, with a two-month lag - write the lag into the clause.

#### albert Studio Sustainability Standard and BREEAM

BAFTA albert's Studio Sustainability Standard is the first sustainability assessment built for studio facilities, scoring Climate, Circularity, Nature, People, Management, Data. 2025/26: average score 77% (70% in 2024), 22 studios 'Very Good' or better, 3 'Outstanding'; Sky Studios Elstree scored 95%.

**Why.** Streamers ask for the score in the RFP; a low or absent score is a scored deduction against a lot that will otherwise win on clean power.

**Here.** With geothermal heat and hydro power, Sugar Land should be targeting Outstanding on day one - it is the most credible marketing claim the lot owns.

#### Crew housing as a revenue line

Operator-owned or master-leased apartments let to productions per unit per week, billed at Icelandic accommodation VAT (11%), with a service package. Olsberg-SPI found 40-60% of cast and crew on a TV series need accommodation, and hospitality/catering took 12% of one show's Icelandic below-the-line spend.

**Why.** Heads of department are imported and must be housed; whoever solves that captures spend that would otherwise leak to Reykjavík hotels.

**Here.** 15 minutes from Reykjavík is close enough that hotels compete - so compete on term (12-week lets), family suites, parking and a shuttle, not on nightly rate.

#### Revenue management cadence

Weekly pricing meeting (commercial director, bookings manager, finance): review holds, aging availability, competitor moves, and set stage-by-stage floor prices for the next 6 months. Rate floors, not rate cards, are the live control. (Practice adapted from hotel revenue management; an estimate for this sector.)

**Why.** Stage-weeks are perishable inventory exactly like hotel rooms; without a floor and a regular decision, the discount is set by whoever is on the phone.

### What gets got wrong

- Occupancy definitions are not comparable. FilmLA's 62% (1H 2025) is participant-reported facility occupancy; Hudson Pacific's 69.1% is trailing-twelve-month percent LEASED. A lot can be 100% leased and 40% physically occupied. Publish which one you mean, every time, or the board will compare apples to a different fruit for years.
- The LSH £25,000-30,000/week for a 20,000 sq ft stage is 2018, London-adjacent, and a LIST price the report says is nearly always discounted; the £25-30/sq ft/yr figure is inclusive of UK business rates. Do not lift either number into an Iceland 2026 rate card without inflating, de-rating for market and stripping the rates element.
- Master leases are the modern product but they remove your lot from the open market. Knight Frank notes almost all new UK stage space is pre-let, which is why short-term availability collapsed. Lease everything to one streamer and you no longer have a business, you have a single tenant with a break clause.
- A hold with no expiry and no challenge rule is worse than no hold: it makes the lot look busy in the pipeline report, blocks real bookings, and produces the phantom-occupancy problem that eventually gets discovered in a cash-flow crisis.
- The 35% rebate is not a rate, it is a test. ISK 350m of Icelandic spend, 30 working days including 10 shoot days, and 50 crew for 50 days with pay TAXED IN ICELAND. A stage-heavy show with imported heads of department paid through a UK payroll can miss the 50-crew-taxed-in-Iceland limb and drop to 25% after the budget was built on 35%.
- Film in Iceland's own legislation page still displays a consolidated text saying the Act expires 31 December 2025, while its incentives page says the scheme is valid through 31 December 2028. Get the operative expiry confirmed in writing before any client quote or long contract relies on it.
- Selling stage-time you cannot power. Landsvirkjun has publicly said it cannot meet demand from new large users, and ESA opened a formal competition investigation in 2025 into its refusals. Firm grid capacity must be contracted, in writing, before the sales team sells the 30 m stage and the volume.
- Ancillary is not a rounding error and it is not free money. Hudson Pacific's ancillary was 36% of in-service studio revenue but its Quixote services business ran NEGATIVE NOI of $6.3m in one quarter. Sell ancillary through concessions with a turnover share before running it on your own balance sheet.
- Deposits do not cover cancellation. A 25% deposit against a booking cancelled 20 days out leaves you with 25% of the money and 100% of an unsellable stage-quarter. The ladder has to be liquidated damages on contract VALUE, not a deposit forfeiture, and it has to be reduced by any genuine re-let.
- Iceland's whole incentivised production market ran ISK 13.3bn in a normal year (2021) and attracted 14 foreign projects in 2022. Sizing a 13,200 m2 lot against 'Iceland's market' guarantees failure; it has to be sized against the London/Budapest/Belfast shortlist it wants to be on.
- Voluntary VAT registration for non-residential letting is what makes input VAT on the build recoverable. Getting it wrong, or letting crew housing (11%) and stage licences (24%) get billed as one line, is a nine-figure-scale error on a new build and an audit magnet afterwards.
- Everything is priced in a currency you do not spend. Clients want USD or EUR, the rebate pays ISK, and the cost base is ISK. An unhedged book of 12-month bookings is a currency position the size of the year's EBITDA.
- The seasonality is inverted, not absent. December's four-odd hours of daylight and June's white nights both drive work onto stage, but they drive different work. Pricing summer as peak because sunbelt lots do is leaving winter revenue on the floor.
- Do not report a four-stage lot's occupancy as a single blended percentage. One booking on the 6,000 m2 room moves the number by tens of points and hides the fact that two 2,000 m2 stages have been dark for a quarter.

---

## 7. Vendors, services and the crew base

*Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær, Iceland (campus scale: who sits on the lot, who visits, who does the work)*

### Essential

#### Lighting and grip rental — the anchor tenant

ESTIMATE: 2,500 m2 warehouse, 1,200+ fixtures (SkyPanel/Orbiter/Vortex-class LED, 18k/12k HMI, 20k tungsten), 400+ stands, 60 sets of rigging hardware, 12 t of chain hoists, 4 condors to 40 m, distro to 4,000 A. REAL: Trilith's on-lot HERC and MBS Equipment Co. cover lighting/grip.

**Why.** Lighting and grip is the largest and heaviest below-the-line rental line. Every kilometre it travels is a truck, a driver and an hour of a 13-hour legal day.

**Here.** Freight in is by sea and one-way expensive, so it pays to land inventory once and keep it. Cheap geothermal/hydro power makes running big fixtures unusually cheap by European standards.

#### Expendables store — open when the stage is open

ESTIMATE: 300 m2, ~1,500 SKUs, staffed 06:00–22:00 with a badge-access after-hours room; gaffer/paper tape, black wrap, diffusion and gel rolls, batteries, sash, fixings, PPE; min-max reorder with 6-week cover on sea-freighted lines.

**Why.** An expendables gap is the cheapest way to lose an hour: no camera tape, no shoot. Off-lot, the nearest replacement is a 30-minute round trip in a gale.

**Here.** Sea freight Rotterdam–Reykjavik runs about 4 days and 4 hours door-to-port with frequent departures (Eimskip); a stock-out means air freight at a multiple, so hold deeper cover than a UK lot would.

#### Transport department and crew coaches

ESTIMATE: 12 x 16-seat 4WD minibuses, 4 x 50-seat coaches, 8 x 7.5 t trucks, 2 tractor units and 6 trailers, plus honeywagon and 3-way facility units. Winter tyres/studs mandatory 1 Nov–14 Apr.

**Why.** Unit moves in wind and dark are the highest-risk hour of a shooting day. A lot-run transport department is the only way to control driver hours and vehicle spec.

**Here.** Route 1/Vesturlandsvegur closes on wind and ice. Drivers' hours run inside the same 11-hour rest rule as crew, so travel time eats the shooting day, not the night.

#### Unit catering kitchen — lot-owned, licensed to an operator

ESTIMATE: 450 m2 production kitchen to serve 600 hot covers in a 45-minute window, blast chiller, 2 x 200 L bratt pans, allergen-segregated line, HACCP plan, ISK 4,500–6,000 per head per hot meal at Icelandic food prices.

**Why.** Catering is the single largest daily crew-morale lever and the one thing a 200-person unit cannot self-supply in a 13-hour legal day.

**Here.** Food is imported and expensive; Iceland has no statutory minimum wage but ~90% collective-agreement coverage, so kitchen labour prices off Efling rates (effective floor ~ISK 513,000/month gross, Jan 2026).

#### Cleaning, waste and recycling contractor

ESTIMATE: resident 6-person day crew plus night team; source-separated streams (paper, plastic, metal, timber, organics, hazardous) to Icelandic sorting-at-source rules; skip yard with 8 x 30 m3 hooklifts; scenery timber diverted to reuse.

**Why.** A stage strike produces 20–40 t of waste in 48 hours. Unmanaged, it blocks the loading yard and kills the next booking's load-in.

**Here.** Iceland's landfill capacity is limited and much residual waste is exported for treatment; sorting is a legal duty, not a sustainability gesture. Terra and Íslenska Gámafélagið are the realistic contractors.

#### Security operation — gate, patrol, control room

ESTIMATE: 24/7/365 manned gatehouse, 2 mobile patrols, control room with 120+ cameras, ANPR, badge access with per-stage zones, visitor pre-clearance, drone detection. Contracted to Securitas hf or Öryggismiðstöðin with a lot-employed head of security.

**Why.** Content leaks and paparazzi drones are contractual failures, not embarrassments. A studio that cannot evidence access control does not get the show.

**Here.** Iceland is low-crime and high-trust, which breeds slack practice. The threat model here is content theft and press, not burglary — so specify to studio standards, not to Icelandic norms.

#### Medical — set medics, occupational health, and the trauma chain

ESTIMATE: 2 resident paramedics on shooting days, treatment room, 8 AEDs on 3-minute-reach spacing, immersion/hypothermia kit for the water stage. REAL: Iceland requires a safety representative in workplaces of 10+ employees and a safety committee at 50+ (Act 46/1980).

**Why.** A crush or a drowning on the water tank is survivable only if the first ten minutes are covered on site; the hospital is not next door.

**Here.** Landspítali in Reykjavik is the national trauma centre — roughly 15–20 min by road from Mosfellsbær in good conditions, much longer in a storm. Plan for the day the road is shut.

#### Freight forwarder and customs broker with a desk on the lot

REAL: Iceland honours the ATA Carnet for temporary admission of professional equipment; other temporary imports go on a customs declaration (form E1) with the exemption code in box 14. Icelandic VAT is 24%. Eimskip runs frequent Rotterdam–Reykjavik sailings at roughly 4 days' transit.

**Why.** A container stuck in customs at Sundahöfn is a stage sitting idle at full rate. Broker capacity on site is the difference between a day and a week.

**Here.** Everything structural is imported and freight is weather-dependent. A blown sailing is a schedule event; the lot needs its own bonded store and a broker on speed-dial, not a shared one.

#### Payroll bureau on lot — Icelandic contracts, Icelandic tax

REAL: under Act 43/1999 wages and contractor payments qualify for reimbursement only if verifiably taxed in Iceland; the 35% band additionally requires at least 50 crew working at least 50 working days on the project.

**Why.** An untaxed offshore payroll silently deletes the rebate on the largest cost line a production has.

**Here.** This is the single strongest reason to host crew services on the lot: every head you put on Icelandic payroll increases both the qualifying spend and the crew-count test.

#### Immigration and work-permit desk

REAL: EEA/EFTA nationals work freely; non-EEA crew (most US HODs) need a permit under the Foreign Nationals' Right to Work Act No. 97/2002 via Vinnumálastofnun and Útlendingastofnun. ESTIMATE: 4–10 weeks for an expert permit, so start at greenlight.

**Why.** An HOD who cannot legally start on day one is a recast or a delayed start; nobody discovers this early enough without a desk that owns it.

**Here.** Iceland is in the EEA and Schengen but not the EU. The permit answer differs sharply for a UK gaffer (post-Brexit, non-EEA) and a Danish one.

#### Posted-worker registration for visiting foreign vendors

REAL: Act No. 45/2007 obliges a foreign undertaking or self-employed person providing services in Iceland for more than ten working days in any twelve months to notify the Administration of Occupational Safety and Health with details of the service and the workers involved.

**Why.** A visiting VFX rig crew or a foreign SFX house that never registers exposes the production and the lot to enforcement mid-shoot.

**Here.** The ten-day trigger is low. A specialist who visits four times in a year crosses it without either party noticing.

#### Crew depth: the real size of the pool, and the gap

REAL: Iceland's film workforce is roughly 1,000 full-time equivalents (2025 reporting); Klapptré records 1,806 FTEs in film production in 2020 and 3,431 work-years including indirect. REAL: the 35% band requires at least 50 crew for at least 50 working days on one project.

**Why.** Four stages at 13,200 m2 can generate demand for 400–600 crew at peak — a majority of the country's working pool on one campus.

**Here.** The two counts differ by definition (film-production employment vs on-set crew), so never quote them as the same number when sizing a schedule.

#### A lot-run training academy with a university and a union partner

REAL PRECEDENT: Screen Alliance Wales, created by Bad Wolf with the University of South Wales in 2018 and backed by BBC, HBO, S4C and Channel 4, reports working with over 80,000 people. REAL: Trilith Studios hosts both the Georgia Film Academy and the Trilith Institute as on-lot vendors.

**Why.** A thousand-person national pool cannot staff a new lot. Either you build the pipeline or you import forever and never bank the rebate's crew test.

**Here.** Partner with Iceland University of the Arts, which launched Iceland's first BA in filmmaking (six disciplines: directing, screenwriting, creative producing, cinematography, editing, sound) with its first intake starting 29 August, and with Kvikmyndaskóli Íslands (roughly 115 students across four departments).

#### Rest-law compliance built into the shooting day

REAL: Act No. 46/1980 requires at least 11 continuous hours of rest in each 24 hours, work may not be arranged so the working period exceeds 13 hours, rest should fall between 23:00 and 06:00 where possible, at least one weekly day off attached to the daily rest, and a maximum 48-hour average week over four months.

**Why.** An American 14-hour day is unlawful here. Every vendor SLA, catering window, transport rota and turnaround must be sized to a 13-hour ceiling.

**Here.** Shortened rest must be compensated with equivalent rest as soon as possible (Arts. 53–54). Travel time to a backlot or location eats the 13 hours; it does not sit outside them.

#### Union footing and the missing collective agreement

REAL: Félag kvikmyndagerðarmanna (FK), founded 1966, became a trade union in 2014 and has campaigned for a comprehensive film collective agreement; it has complained about SÍK-affiliated producers asking freelancers to waive the statutory 11-hour rest. REAL: around 90% of Icelandic workers are covered by a collective agreement.

**Why.** Operating in the gap between freelance custom and Icelandic labour law is the fastest route to a public dispute that costs you a streamer's business.

**Here.** FK has argued that rebate eligibility should be conditioned on full compliance with Icelandic law on hours, wages, accommodation and hygiene. Assume that becomes policy before 2028 and build to it now.

#### Vendor governance — exclusivity, conflicts and open access

ESTIMATE: no on-lot vendor gets exclusivity; any production may bring its own supplier and use the loading yard and shells at a published day rate; the lot takes a landing fee (ESTIMATE 5–8% of visiting-vendor invoice or a flat yard charge) instead of blocking access.

**Why.** Exclusivity wins the vendor's rent and loses the show. Big productions travel with their own camera and lighting packages and will not be told otherwise.

**Here.** With so few Icelandic suppliers, an exclusive deal effectively hands one company the national market — a competition and reputational problem in a country this small.

#### Marine and dive unit for the water stage

ESTIMATE: dive supervisor plus 4 commercial divers to HSE Media Diving standard, 2 safety swimmers per in-water performer, surface-supplied capability, hot-water suits, recompression plan with named chamber, immersion-hypothermia protocol at tank temperature 28–30°C.

**Why.** Water work is the highest fatality-risk activity on a lot. A 2,400 m2 tank without a standing dive team is a stage you cannot legally use.

**Here.** Iceland has commercial and scientific divers but almost no film-trained ones; this team must be built and retained by the lot, or flown from the UK for every water booking.

#### Year-round load — the commercials, music-video and broadcast floor

ESTIMATE: target 30–40% of stage-days from short-form (commercials, stills, music video, live broadcast) at a published sub-day rate, deliberately booked into winter. REAL: Truenorth's Kristinn Thordarson: the industry relies on Icelandic projects but people need to work all year round.

**Why.** Vendors and crew do not survive on two features a year. Short-form is what keeps the camera house's lights on in February.

**Here.** Iceland's tourism and commercials sector already shoots here year-round; that is the existing demand a new lot can convert rather than create.

### Expected

#### Vendor village — leasable shell units against the stage road

ESTIMATE for this lot: 7,000 m2 in 12 units of 250–1,200 m2, 6 m clear, 4.0 x 4.5 m level-access roller doors, 400 V 3-phase 63–125 A per unit, sub-metered. REAL: Trilith Studios reports 40 on-site production vendors on its 700-acre site.

**Why.** Without leasable shells no vendor can stand up in Mosfellsbær; every rental order becomes a 15-minute van run to Reykjavik and a half-day loss on a re-order.

**Here.** No Icelandic vendor will build speculatively 15 min outside Reykjavik. The lot builds the shells and leases them, or there is no vendor village at all.

#### Camera rental — on-lot branch of an international house

ESTIMATE for a 4-stage lot: 12–16 digital bodies (ARRI Alexa 35 / 265 / LF, Sony VENICE 2), 6+ prime sets incl. 2 anamorphic, 4 zooms, 30 AKS carts, ~600 line items, 2 techs resident. REAL: Kukl (Reykjavik, est. 1996, Jöfursbás 4) lists 12 staff across camera, lighting, grip, vehicles.

**Why.** A dead body or a lens with a soft edge on a 200-person day costs the day. Local re-supply inside 20 minutes is the whole argument for on-lot camera.

**Here.** Iceland has essentially one full-line house (Kukl). Depth for a 6,000 m2 stage plus a water unit means a satellite branch of Panavision, ARRI Rental or Keslow, stocked from Europe.

#### Prop and set-dressing house on the lot

ESTIMATE: 3,500 m2 racked to 8 m, 18°C/50% RH, 40 t of dressing, furniture, drapes, period and contemporary, own van fleet. REAL: RVK Studios and Truenorth announced a film prop and costume company for the Gufunes lot.

**Why.** With no domestic prop trade, dressing is bought new and thrown away every show. A house that buys once and rents fifty times turns a cost line into an asset and a revenue line.

**Here.** Iceland's prop rental is thin — Emblustabblar (Tangarhöfði 7, Reykjavik) and ad-hoc sourcing. Building the stock is a 5–10 year capital project, not a lease.

#### Costume house, workroom, dye and ager bay, and crew laundry

ESTIMATE: 1,200 m2; 20 machinist stations, 6 industrial washers/extractors, dye vats with trade-effluent interceptor, ager/distresser bay with LEV, 4,000 m of hanging rail, fittings rooms with 3,200 K/5,600 K switchable light.

**Why.** Fittings and breakdown at 03:00 are normal. A costume department billeted 15 minutes away loses the ability to age a hero coat between takes.

**Here.** Announced for Gufunes by Kormákur/Truenorth — meaning national capacity is being built once. Duplicating it here needs a partnership, not a competing build.

#### Construction materials yard and timber merchant on lot

ESTIMATE: 1,200 m2 covered plus 2,000 m2 hardstanding; 18 mm ply, 2x4 studding, MDF, steel section, fixings, paint mixing to BS/RAL, forklift and Hiab. Standing stock 8 weeks; all softwood and sheet goods imported.

**Why.** Set construction dies on sheet goods. A stage crew of 60 idle for a ply delivery costs more in a morning than the yard's annual rent.

**Here.** Iceland grows almost no structural timber. Every sheet is a sailing; BYKO/Húsasmiðja lead times govern build schedules, so the lot must hold buffer stock itself.

#### Greens, plant hire and picture greens

ESTIMATE: 600 m2 heated glasshouse plus a silk/artificial store; 2,000 potted units, moss and turf handling, cut-greens cold room at 4°C. REAL: Cinema Greens is a listed on-lot vendor at Trilith Studios.

**Why.** Every interior needs plants and every exterior needs cover. Sourced ad hoc, greens are the department that most often turns up wrong on the day.

**Here.** Iceland is near-treeless and imports of live plants are phytosanitary-controlled. Geothermal heat makes a lot-owned glasshouse absurdly cheap to run — a genuine local advantage.

#### Action vehicles, picture cars and the vehicle shop

ESTIMATE: 800 m2 heated workshop, 2 four-post lifts, 3,000 m2 secure yard; low-loader, process trailer, tracking vehicle with a crane arm, 20-car standing picture fleet, LHD/RHD both.

**Why.** Picture vehicles are the longest single lead-time item on a schedule and the one most likely to be refused at a border.

**Here.** Iceland drives on the right with a small, young, very specific vehicle fleet. Period or foreign-market picture cars must be imported and temporarily admitted; salt and volcanic grit destroy them fast.

#### Craft services — 24-hour, weather-aware

ESTIMATE: two 60 m2 posts (stage side, backlot side); hot drinks continuously, soup from 10:00, hot food at every unit move, vitamin D on the counter Oct–Mar, drying racks and a boot wash at every entrance.

**Why.** Cold, wet crew make mistakes and go home. Craft is the cheapest safety intervention on a sub-arctic lot.

**Here.** Iceland's December daylight is roughly 4–5 hours; the crew arrives and leaves in the dark for four months. Hot food and drying capacity are not a nicety, they are attendance management.

#### Fuel, propane, gases and generator hire

ESTIMATE: bunded 20,000 L HVO tank, LPG cage to 4 t with 3 m separation, industrial gas store (argon/CO2/acetylene) with racks and LEV, plus 6 x 200–500 kVA sets for backlot and location work. REAL: Trilith lists Production Propane and Supplies as an on-lot vendor.

**Why.** Effects, heating, welding and location work all run on stored energy. Ad-hoc deliveries into a live lot are a fire and traffic problem.

**Here.** Grid power is cheap and low-carbon but Landsvirkjun has refused new industrial supply, so on-site generation is a capacity answer, not just a backup — and HVO must be imported.

#### Dailies, data lab and lot-level post rooms

ESTIMATE: 2 dailies suites, LTO-9 archive with 3-copy/2-media/1-offsite policy, 500 TB working tier, colour-managed review room to DCI P3. REAL: Sohonet is an on-lot vendor at Trilith; Cinespace Chicago lists Periscope Post & Audio among preferred vendors.

**Why.** If the media has to leave the island to be looked at, the director sees yesterday's work tomorrow, and the 35% rebate's post-production days go abroad.

**Here.** Post days count toward the 35% rebate's 30-working-day test, so keeping dailies and finishing in Iceland is a rebate decision as much as a creative one.

#### Housing for imported heads of department — a leased portfolio, not a hunt per show

ESTIMATE: 40 units held on 3–5 year leases (25 x 2-bed, 10 x 3-bed, 5 family houses) in Mosfellsbær and 105/108 Reykjavik. REAL: average market rent on newly registered Icelandic contracts was ISK 268,926 per unit in Feb 2026; Reykjavik rent per m2 was ISK 4,041 — the highest in the country.

**Why.** An HOD who cannot find a flat declines the job. Holding stock converts a recruitment blocker into a line item.

**Here.** Reykjavik's rental market is structurally supply-constrained and short-term tourist lets have removed stock. A studio taking 40 units becomes a visible political actor in a 13,000-person town.

#### Commissary, café and licensed bar

ESTIMATE: 250-cover commissary open 06:00–20:00 on shooting days, grab-and-go counter at each stage cluster, and a licensed room for wraps. Alcohol must be bought through the state monopoly ÁTVR; premises need a municipal licence from Mosfellsbær.

**Why.** The commissary is where departments meet. A lot without one has crew driving to Reykjavik at lunch and coming back late.

**Here.** Off-licence alcohol is a state monopoly, so wrap and hospitality supply chains have one supplier and fixed prices; plan the licence early with the municipality.

#### Crew registry and call service run by the lot

ESTIMATE: a vetted database of ~1,500 individuals (Icelandic and Nordic within a 3-hour flight), tracking grade, credits, tickets (IPAF, rigging, first aid, dive), availability, right-to-work status and rate; booked by two full-time crew coordinators.
